    CISCO One Cisco Experience Lab

    📘 One Cisco Experience Lab

    One Cisco Experience Lab

     Cisco Secure Networking delivers the One Cisco Experience by unifying networking, security, observability, and policy into a single integrated architecture designed to protect modern enterprises across campus, branch, WAN, cloud, and remote access environments. By combining deep visibility, identity-driven access control, and Zero Trust enforcement across every connection point, organizations can simplify operations, strengthen security posture, and improve digital resilience.

    In this hands-on lab, participants will deploy either a BGP EVPN fabric or an SDA-LISP Campus fabric as the foundation for a modern Cisco Secure Network supporting both macro and micro segmentation. Through guided exercises, you’ll experience how Cisco technologies work together seamlessly across domains using Cisco Security Cloud Control, Secure Firewall Management Center (cdFMC/FTD), Cisco Secure Access, Duo, Catalyst SD-WAN, Identity Services Engine (ISE), ThousandEyes, and Security Group Tags (SGTs) to deliver unified policy enforcement, shared telemetry, and consistent user experiences.

    You’ll implement integrated workflows that connect networking and security operations across campus, branch, data center, and remote users. Real-world use cases include securing users and devices accessing local and cloud applications, protecting IoT environments through identity-based segmentation, and enabling secure remote access using Zero Trust Network Access (ZTNA). Participants will also explore how Cisco’s unified platform approach enables consistent policy, end-to-end visibility, and faster troubleshooting across switching, wireless, SD-WAN, cloud security, and firewall infrastructure.

    By the end of the lab, you’ll gain practical experience deploying and validating a fully integrated Cisco architecture that demonstrates the power of the One Cisco Experience—bringing together connectivity, security, and observability into a cohesive operational model for the modern enterprise.

    General

    📄

    1. About this Lab

    About This Lab  

    Step into the role of the lead architect for PseudoCo and take on a mission that mirrors what enterprises are facing right now—modernizing the network while securing everything, everywhere. In this hands-on lab, you won’t just configure technology—you’ll build a full Cisco-powered Secure Network architecture from the ground up, integrating identity, segmentation, secure access, and observability into a unified Zero Trust strategy.

    PseudoCo is at a turning point. Rapid growth, a distributed workforce, IoT expansion, and increasing reliance on cloud and AI-driven applications have exposed the limits of traditional network security. Policies are inconsistent, visibility is fragmented, and legacy VPN-based access models are no longer enough. Your job is to fix that—by transforming the network into a policy-driven, identity-aware security platform using Cisco’s integrated portfolio.

    Throughout this lab, you’ll make real architectural decisions (yes—even choosing your fabric path), deploy and integrate technologies like Cisco Secure Access, Duo, ISE, SD-WAN, and Secure Firewall, and validate how identity and policy follow users across campus, branch, cloud, and remote environments. You’ll enforce segmentation with SGTs, deliver Zero Trust application access, protect internet usage with DLP and AI guardrails, and gain end-to-end visibility with ThousandEyes.

    This isn’t a simulation—it’s a fully interactive experience including real hardware infrastructure. You’ll act as both the architect and the operator, testing real-world scenarios, validating policy enforcement, and troubleshooting like you would in production.

    By the end, you won’t just understand Zero Trust—you’ll have built it.

    One network. One identity. One policy. Enforced everywhere.
    Let’s get to work. 💪

    📄

    2. Learning Objectives

    🎯 Learning Objectives

    By completing this lab, you will gain the practical skills and confidence to design, deploy, and operate a modern Cisco Secure Network built on Zero Trust principles.

    🔐 Zero Trust Architecture & Secure Access

    • Design and implement a Zero Trust architecture across campus, WAN, data center, and remote users

    • Configure Cisco Secure Access for secure internet and private application access

    • Apply identity-driven access control using Duo and Active Directory

    • Compare and validate ZTNA vs traditional VPN access models

    🧠 Identity, Policy & Context Sharing

    • Integrate Cisco ISE, Duo, and Active Directory for centralized identity and policy enforcement

    • Configure and validate Security Group Tags (SGTs) for identity-based segmentation

    • Extend identity context across Secure Access, SD-WAN, and firewall platforms

    • Build dynamic, role-based access policies using identity, device posture, and group membership

    🌐 Segmentation: Macro & Micro

    • Implement macro segmentation using Virtual Networks (VNs)

    • Enforce micro segmentation using TrustSec and SGT-based policy

    • Validate end-to-end segmentation across wired, wireless, WAN, and data center

    • Prevent lateral movement using identity-based policy enforcement

    🏗️ Campus & WAN Fabric Deployment

    • Deploy a campus fabric using Cisco SDA or BGP EVPN architecture

    • Provision and automate infrastructure using Cisco Catalyst Center

    • Integrate campus fabric with Catalyst SD-WAN for end-to-end connectivity

    • Configure wireless access using Catalyst 9800 WLC with FlexConnect

    ☁️ Secure Internet & Private Access (SASE)

    • Configure Secure Internet Access (SIA) policies with web filtering and threat protection

    • Implement Data Loss Prevention (DLP) and AI Guardrails for data security

    • Enable ZTNA, VPN-as-a-Service, and browser-based access for private applications

    • Apply consistent policy across branch, remote, and mobile users

    🏢 Data Center & Application Security

    • Configure Cisco Secure Firewall (FTD) for application protection

    • Implement SGT-based firewall policy enforcement

    • Secure application access using identity-aware policy models

    🌍 SD-WAN & Edge Security

    • Configure Catalyst SD-WAN for secure branch connectivity

    • Apply local enforcement policies using NGFW and UTD at the edge

    • Protect IoT and branch traffic with SGT-based and threat-aware controls

    📊 Observability & User Experience

    • Integrate Cisco ThousandEyes for end-to-end visibility

    • Monitor endpoint health, network performance, and SaaS experience

    • Correlate telemetry across endpoint, network, and cloud security layers

    • Accelerate troubleshooting using Experience Insights dashboards

    🛠️ Operational Readiness & Validation

    • Validate end-to-end connectivity, policy enforcement, and segmentation

    • Perform real-world testing across user personas and access scenarios

    • Troubleshoot identity, policy, and connectivity issues across domains

    • Gain hands-on experience operating a fully integrated Cisco Secure Network

    By the end of this lab, you won’t just understand Zero Trust—you’ll have built, enforced, and validated it across an entire enterprise architecture. Now let’s put those skills into action.

    Environment Topology

    📄

    1. Environment Details

    PseudoCo Topology

    Understanding the PseudoCo Network Topology

    The lab topology represents the enterprise environment for PseudoCo, a company modernizing its infrastructure to support Zero Trust networking, identity-based segmentation, and secure application access using technologies from Cisco.

    The environment simulates a realistic enterprise deployment consisting of four major operational domains: core services, application environments, campus/branch infrastructure, and remote users.

    At the start of the lab, these domains are not fully connected or secured, allowing you to progressively build the secure architecture throughout the exercises.

    Topology Walkthrough

    1. Core Services Environment

    At the center of the topology is PseudoCo’s core services environment, representing the company’s primary infrastructure services.

    This environment provides the foundational systems that support identity, authentication, and enterprise operations, including:

    • Active Directory

    • DNS and DHCP

    • Identity services used for authentication and policy decisions

    • Shared enterprise infrastructure

    These systems act as the identity and services backbone for the entire architecture.

    Many of the security decisions made later in the lab rely on identity and context originating from this environment.

    2. Application and Data Center Zones

    Adjacent to the core services environment is PseudoCo’s data center application infrastructure.

    This includes internal applications such as the company intranet along with externally accessible services located in a DMZ security zone.

    The DMZ hosts the PseudoCo User Lookup Tool, which simulates an externally accessible application used by employees and partners.

    Later in the lab you will secure access to these applications using identity-based policy and Zero Trust access controls.

    3. Branch Campus Environment

    The branch environment represents a modern campus network deployment.

    This site includes:

    • Campus switching infrastructure

    • Wireless access infrastructure

    • Wired and wireless endpoints

    • WAN edge connectivity

    This branch site operates using the campus fabric architecture you selected earlier in the lab.

    Within the branch network, endpoints are assigned to one of three primary business security segments used throughout the architecture:

    • MAIN – Corporate users and enterprise devices

    • PROD – Production applications and critical services

    • IoT – Operational and non-user devices

    These segments are implemented using fabric-based macro segmentation and identity-based micro segmentation using Security Group Tags (SGTs).

    Connectivity between sites is transported over the enterprise WAN using Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN, ensuring segmentation and identity context are preserved as traffic moves between locations.

    4. Remote User Environment

    Two remote workstations represent employees accessing corporate resources from outside the traditional network perimeter.

    These endpoints simulate common scenarios such as:

    • Work-from-home employees

    • Remote contractors

    • Mobile administrators

    Instead of traditional VPN access, these users will later connect to internal applications using Zero Trust application access workflows, providing secure and identity-driven access to private resources.

    5. Extended Data Center Environment

    The topology also includes a secondary data center environment representing additional enterprise infrastructure.

    This environment mirrors the same MAIN, PROD, and IoT segmentation model used across the campus and WAN.

    As the lab progresses, you will establish secure end-to-end connectivity between sites, validating that segmentation and policy enforcement remain consistent across the enterprise.

    Lab Access and Collaboration

    All environments in the topology are accessed through dedicated jumphosts.

    Depending on the lab format, attendees may have access to:

    • A single jumphost for individual operation

    • Up to three jumphosts to support collaborative teams

    This design allows multiple engineers to work within the same lab environment simultaneously, simulating real-world operational collaboration between network, security, and infrastructure teams.

    What You Will Build

    At the beginning of the lab, these environments operate as independent infrastructure domains.

    Throughout the exercises you will progressively:

    • Deploy a campus fabric architecture

    • Implement identity-based segmentation

    • Extend segmentation across the WAN

    • Share identity and SGT context with security platforms

    • Secure applications hosted in the data center

    • Enable Zero Trust access for remote users

    By the end of the lab, the topology will operate as a fully integrated secure enterprise network, where identity and policy follow users and devices across campus, WAN, and application environments.

    Environment Preparation

    In this scenario you will familiarize yourself with the dCloud Lab environment and activate the Duo account associated with the lab. The steps outlined in this section will be performed from one of the Jump Hosts and AD1 Server. 

    📄

    1. Familiarize Yourself with the Jumphost 

    Familiarize Yourself with the Jumphost 

    Each Lab session has (3) Jumphosts to support up to three participants logging into and working on the Lab together. Normally all Lab operations should be done from Jumphost1. If your are VPN'd into the lab session with Secure Client you can RDP directly to Jumphost 1 at IP Address: 198.18.133.36 and logging in with Username: corp.pseudoco.com\demouser with Password: C1sco12345 . The other option is to use WebRDP directly from your topology or from an EXPO session. Your Proctor will make sure to highlight the correct way to access your session for the event. 

    🛑 Note you must login using the domain login, Username: corp.pseudoco.com/demouser with Password: C1sco12345.  If you DO NOT, you will not see any of the configured tools and favorites that are needed for the lab. (Please see your Proctor if you are unclear on this step)

    Jumphost Tools Overview:

    Once logged into your Jumphost you will see various different tools installed on the Jumphost. Please verify you see the following highlighted int the below Screen Shot.

    1. Chrome Browser: Has all the pre-configured bookmark favorites, including our dashboard automation. 
    2. mRemote: This tool includes all of the CLI and RDP access needed for the lab.

    These both will be described in detail next. 

    Chrome Browser Favorites and Dashboard Automation. 

    From within the Jumphost, launch the Chrome Browser. Once launched explore the Bookmark Favorites as shown below. This will allow you to access various management platforms as required by the lab. For example, click on Cisco SD-WAN  and it will launch the management platform for SD-WAN. Notice all the other dashboards you have access to. 

    Next, a very important element of the Lab is the dashboard automation as shown above. By clicking on this icon, you will launch the dashboard automation interface giving you access to various different accounts the lab session has automated for you. Click the dashboard icon and we will explore how to use the accounts next. 

    🛑 Note depending on the size of your desktop you might have to expand the toolbar favorites to see the dashboard icon. It is always the last favorite in the toolbar.

    Once clicked, you will see all of our accounts associated with your Lab session. Throughout the guide there will be specific instructions on how to use each of these when needed. To practice accessing these accounts, let's explore your Cisco Security Cloud Control (SCC) org.  You can click view to practice the auto-login process to your Org. 

    mRemote - device and workstation access 

    Next on the Jumphost, launch mRemote. mRemote has all of the infrastructure device access information including SSH to network devices or RDP access to things like Workstations or Servers. Throughout the lab, mRemote will be called out and referenced for accessing devices and workstations etc. All the session credentials are pre-configured for easy access. As shown below, either double-click or right-click and select connect to practice accessing your "Branch Router", and likewise practice RDP to "Workstation-1"

    🛑 In the Next section we will be activating your Duo Admin Account. This is an import process to complete before starting the Lab. Please proceed to the next section. 

    📄

    2. Activate Duo Admin Account

    Activate your Duo Admin Account

    In this section, we will activate your Duo Admin Account. The steps in this section should be performed from Jumphost1.  While you can technically activate your Duo Admin Account from your local computer, if you do so, please be sure to add Duo Push as a second factor authentication method so that you can login to your Duo Admin Panel from inside the lab environment.

    First, on Jumphost1, open the dashboard automation favorite in the google chrome toolbar as described in the previous section. Once your dashboard accounts are displayed, locate your Duo account information. Next, click the copy icon next to the Suggested Password.

      

    Click Activate Account to setup the Duo console.  Then click Get started.

    🛑 Important: If activation fails call a Proctor over.

     

    At the Create password prompt, paste the Suggested Password you copied to create and confirm the Duo admin password.  Click Continue.

             

     

    Next, we will add Duo Push as a second factor, click Continue again.  You need to setup and use Duo Push with Duo Mobile using your mobile phone so that you can login to Duo Admin Panel from virtual machines inside the lab environment.  You may also create a Passkey as a second factor authentication option at a later time when logging into the Duo Admin Panel from your local computer. 

    Duo Push with Duo Mobile Option: This option is available whether you are activating Duo in a local browser tab (by using the option in the Launch Panel), or doing so using the web browser inside Jumphost1 by opening the dashboard automation favorite in your Google Chrome favorites tool bar. Click Continue to start the process.

    Next, click Skip for now for Passkey configuration (you can do this later if desired).

     

    Duo Push setup is required.  If you do not have the Duo App installed on your phone, you must first install it from the Google Play or Apple App Store to finish the Duo Admin setup.  Inside the Duo Mobile App and click Add + 

    Next, select the Use QR code option. 

    Then scan the QR code that is displayed in your activation window (🛑 Note: NOT the one displayed below).

     

    Once scanned, activation should complete successfully. Select Continue.

    Back on your mobile device complete the setup by clicking Save on your mobile device.

    Now in your Duo mobile app you will see your Duo admin account now active.

    Next, Click Continue to Duo Admin Panel Login.

    Finally, log into Duo Admin Panel.  The password will always be available to copy from the dashboard automation accessed via the dashboard icon in your Chrome bookmark.

    If you can successfully log into the Duo Admin Portal, then save your credentials in the browser to more easily login in the future. 

       

                   

    Once you login successfully you will see a 3-Digit code to enter into your mobile app. Likewise you should the notification on your mobile app. 

    Back on your mobile app, enter the 3-Digit verification code and click Verify.

    Switch back to Jumphost1 and you will see you have been successfully logged into your Duo Admin Account. 

    🛑 NOTE: You can always repeat the process above by first accessing the Duo Admin dashboard account in Jumphost1 with the dashboard icon in your Chrome Toolbar. Login and use the provided username and password, along with you newly setup 2-factor authentication.

    🧭 Complete

    🎉 Congratulations you have completed all of the environment preparation! Now the fun starts!! Please proceed to the official start of the Lab. Good Luck on your Adventure. 🤩

    Choose your own Secure Network Adventure

    📄

    Choose Your Fabric

    Choose Your Own Secure Network Adventure🤔

    The Architect’s Dilemma at Pseudoco

    You are a seasoned network architect brought in to modernize the enterprise network fabric of Pseudoco, a fast-growing, technology-driven organization operating across headquarters, data centers, branch offices, and a highly distributed remote workforce.

    The pressure is real. This initiative has executive visibility, and leadership is counting on you to design an architecture that will support Pseudoco’s business for the next decade—securely, reliably, and at scale.

    Your mission is clear: design a secure, scalable, automated, and future-proof enterprise campus and branch network fabric that integrates seamlessly with Pseudoco’s broader security and observability strategy.

    After weeks of discovery sessions, stakeholder interviews, and technical assessments, you narrow the design decision down to two leading campus fabric architectures:

    • Cisco Software-Defined Access (SDA)
    • BGP EVPN-based Campus Fabric

    This decision will directly influence Pseudoco’s operational efficiency, security posture, user experience, and ability to adapt to emerging demands such as AI-driven workloads and rapid business expansion.

    There is no single “right” answer—but every choice comes with tradeoffs that must align with Pseudoco’s strategy, skills, and long-term goals.

    If you choose Cisco SDA, you choose:
    • Operational simplicity
    • Policy and security integration
    • Centralized control
    • But at the cost of flexibility and vendor lock-in

    If you choose BGP EVPN, you choose:
    • Maximum flexibility and scalability
    • Open standards and potential cost savings
    • But with greater operational overhead and complexity

    In the end, you are the architect. And the network-your network-must reflect the needs of the organization, not just the flavor of the month. You know Cisco is the right answer... just are not sure which architecture makes sense.

    The SDA Path

    You decide to roll with Cisco SDA-embracing intent-based networking, full automation, and a tight integration with Cisco Catalyst Center and Identity Services Engine(ISE).

    Pros:
    • Automated Segmentation: VN and SGT-based segmentation built-in.
    • Policy-Driven: Identity-based access control through ISE.
    • Plug-and-Play: Rapid deployment and endpoint onboarding via automation.
    • Centralized Management: Catalyst Center provides a single pane of glass for monitoring, provisioning, and assurance.
    • Future-Ready: Built for loT, telemetry, and machine-learning analytics.

    Drawbacks:
    • ALL or Nothing: Tight integration is what make SDA great, but that requires Catalyst Center appliances, Identity Services Engine, licensing, and Catalyst switching infrastructure.
    • Vendor Lock-in: You're now heavily committed to Cisco's ecosystem.
    • Learning Curve: Your ops team will need training on Catalyst Center, ISE, and fabric roles (control/edge/border nodes).
    • Less Flexibility: Custom or hybrid deployments outside Cisco's vision get tricky.

    The BGP EVPN Route

    You lean into your routing roots and pick BGP EVPN-leveraging VXLAN overlays to create a scalable, vendor-agnostic campus fabric.

    Pros:
    • Open Standards-Based: Works with multiple vendors-Juniper, Arista, Cisco, etc.
    • Highly Scalable: Proven in large-scale data centers and now campus networks.
    • Customizable: You design the control/overlay plane to your exact needs.
    • No Need for Catalyst Center: Simpler if you're allergic to large management platforms.

    Drawbacks:
    • Operational Complexity: You'll need deep BGP, EVPN, and VXLAN expertise. Your ops team will need serious training. Make sure multiple team members have operation knowledge of this subject. We always build redundancy into our networks, make sure you have redundancy in the employees who know how to run it!
    • No Built-In Identity-Based Segmentation: You must bolt on security (like 802.1X, ACLs, or integrate with ISE separately).
    • Longer Time-to-Deploy: More manual work upfront.
    • Limited Campus-Specific Tooling: Less baked-in support for wired/wireless convergence, telemetry, and user-based policies.

    The Business Context

    PseudoCo is facing several pressing challenges:

    • Security policy is inconsistent across campus, branches, data center, and remote users

    • User and IoT mobility creates visibility gaps when policy doesn’t follow identity

    • Infrastructure is reaching end-of-life while bandwidth and AI-driven workloads are increasing

    • Security teams need shared context across network, security, and access platforms

    At the same time, PseudoCo is investing in a broader secure architecture that integrates identity, SASE, and observability platforms including Splunk and ThousandEyes, while strengthening identity security with Duo Security.

    These investments mean the campus fabric is no longer just about connectivity — it must become a policy enforcement and identity context distribution platform.

    This aligns directly with PseudoCo’s Zero Trust initiative, where access decisions must be based on identity, device posture, location, and risk — not just IP address or VLAN. 

    Why This Decision Matters

    The campus fabric you choose will determine how PseudoCo:

    • Onboards new locations and devices at scale

    • Distributes security group tags and identity context

    • Integrates with SD-WAN, data center security, and Secure Access

    • Automates operations and reduces human configuration errors

    • Extends Zero Trust principles into the campus itself

    PseudoCo understands that the network is now a security enforcement point, not just a transport medium. 

    Your Role in This Lab

    In this lab, you will step into the role of both:

    • Infrastructure Architect — designing the campus fabric foundation

    • Security Architect — ensuring the fabric supports Zero Trust policy enforcement end-to-end

    You will evaluate the operational model, automation capabilities, security integration, and long-term scalability of each fabric approach before implementing your chosen design.

    The Decision Moment

    PseudoCo is looking to you to answer a key strategic question:

    Should we deploy a fabric optimized for policy automation and identity-driven access
    or
    Should we deploy a fabric optimized for open standards flexibility and deterministic control?

    There is no wrong answer — only the design that best aligns with business priorities, operational maturity, and long-term architecture goals.

    🧭 Next up

    Stop procrastinating, its time to make your choice.💪

    If you chose to do a SDA Fabric please proceed to: SD-Access Overview
    If you chose to do a EVPN Fabric please proceed to: EVPN Overview

    📄

    SD-Access Overview:

    You Choose: Software-Defined Access (SDA)

    🛑NOTE: If you chose EVPN please skip this SDA Section 

    PseudoCo has decided to prioritize operational simplicity, identity-driven automation, and fast security policy deployment across all campus locations.

    The CIO has made it clear:

    “We need security policy to follow users and devices automatically — regardless of where they connect.”

    With workforce mobility increasing and IoT onboarding accelerating, PseudoCo needs a fabric that natively understands who and what is on the network, not just where traffic is coming from.

    What This Means for PseudoCo

    By choosing SDA, PseudoCo is building a campus designed around:

    Identity as the control plane

    • User identity and device posture drive access decisions

    • Policy follows users between wired, wireless, branch, and remote access

    End-to-End Segmentation

    • Macro and micro-segmentation using SGTs across campus, WAN, and data center

    • Reduced lateral movement risk

    Automation at Scale

    • New sites and devices onboard faster

    • Reduced configuration drift and human error

    Native Zero Trust Alignment

    • Campus becomes an active enforcement point in the Zero Trust architecture

    • Policy and identity context can extend into Secure Access and SD-WAN

    Observe that there are currently no configured discoveries.

    Configure the first device discovery job for the three 9300 switches, which make up our Site 105 fabric:

    • Start by giving the discovery job the name Site-105-Discovery.
    • Next for discovery type, select Range.
    • For the IP address range, we will be using the loopback interfaces which were pre-configured on the switches 172.30.255.1 – 172.30.255.3.

    CLI = CLI Admin

    SNMP = v2c read and v2c write

    NETCONF = Don't enable NETCONF 

    HTTP(S) = HTTP(S) write, HTTP(S) read (select Show advanced settings to see HTTP(S) credentials)

    Leave the rest default and once complete, click Next.

In the Assign devices to an existing site pop-up. In the Site Hierarchy, select North Carolina -> Durham -> Site-105-> Main. Once selected, click Save.

    🛑 NOTE: If you are unable to see MAIN , please use control-scroll to enlarge the window. Alternatively, you can search for MAIN in the Search Hierarchy field. This is an issue when accessing the Catalyst Center GUI via RDP.

    Next, verify all settings are correct as below and click Next.

    Finally, review the summary of your selections then click Start Discovery. This will start the discovery process.

    👀 This discovery job will now be saved under the main menu under Tools -> Discovery. You can use this discovery again to re-discover the devices if needed.

    Click on View discovery so we can verify the discovery of our campus fabric 9300 switches.

    Task - Configure Software Defined Access

    Software Defined Access(SDA) is an all-in-one campus fabric solution aimed at providing ease of network operations.

Utilize mRemote on Jumphost1 to access the CLI interface of the Catalyst switches, if needed. The main configuration will be driven and automated from within Catalyst Center UI.

    Within Catalyst Center, navigate to Provision -> Fabric Sites.

    Review the Summary of the Fabric Site settings, Click Next.

    Catalyst Center will do some pre-flight checks prior to deploying the Fabric Site. Finally, click Submit.

    👀 Wait for the fabric site to complete. Next, we will configure the Virtual networks. If you would like to monitor the progress of the deployment, click on Activities. Then click on the most recent task to review.

    Provision SD-Access Fabric

    The next step is to Provision the SD-Access Fabric. Start by selecting Fabric Sites then select the single Fabric Site 1 to start the provisioning.

    Site_105-Border-Spine will have both the Border and Control Plane node roles co-resident. Toggle the Border Node slider to begin the configuration.

    Select Layer 3 Handoff. Select Enable Layer-3 Handoff and enter the Local BGP Autonomous Number (65535) which will be configured on the border.

    Uncheck Do not import external routes.

    Select Add Transits to add then start the configuration of the XAR-Transit connection to the SD-WAN infrastructure.

    Next, Click the XAR-Transit and then select Add External Interface.

    Select the External Interface the Border Router will use to peer with the upstream SD-WAN router. This will be (GigabitEthernet1/0/48). Add an interface description of your choice.

    Next, we will configure the peering relationships between the Local Interface (Border) and The Peer Interface (SD-WAN Router).

    Start by enabling the Layer-3 Handoff by using the toggle button as shown for each of the (3) VN’s and configure the corresponding VLAN, Local and Remote Peer IP addresses as listed below, then select Save.

    VN VLAN Local IP Address Peer IP Address
    IOT 102 192.168.255.5/31 192.168.255.4/31
    Main 10 192.168.255.1/31 192.168.255.0/31
    PROD 101 192.168.255.3/31 192.168.255.2/31

    Review and complete the Border configuration, then select Add.

    The Control Plane Node attributes will be configured next. Move the slider on Control Plane Node to enable the role.

    Select LISP Pub/Sub as the route distribution protocol, then select Add.

    To complete the Border / Control Plane Node configuration, select Add.

    Now that the Border/Control Plane for the Fabric is configured, the next step is to configure both leaf switches as Fabric Edge Nodes. Start the configuration by selecting Site_105-Leaf1.

    In the configuration pane move the slider for Edge Node and then select Add.

    Repeat the same operation for Leaf 2. Select Site_105-Leaf2.

    Then in the configuration pane move the slider for Edge Node and the select Add.

    This completes the initial configuration of the fabric. Select Deploy.

    Select Now then click Apply in the Modify Fabric screen.

    Wait for the pre-flight checks to complete. Click Submit.

    Configure Trunk Ports for Access Points

    Use Port Assignments to configure trunk ports and native VLAN's for access points. 

    For proper AP functionality, we will need to configure port GigabitEthernet1/0/2 on each Leaf switch as a trunk port and set the native vlan as 10.

    Start by going the the MAIN Fabric Site and click Port Assignment

    You will see both Leaf1 and Leaf2 listed as well as all of their ports. Start by clicking the drop-down on Leaf1 and then select GigabitEthernet1/0/2. Repeat the same process for Leaf2. 

    Once both Leaf switches are selected, click Configure, then select Trunking Device.

    Next, configure the Port Assignment properties. Set the Native VLAN as 10, then set the Allowed VLANs as 10,101,102, then add an optional description. Finally, click Update.

    Next, click Deploy All to start the deployment of the Port Configurations we set.

    .

    Select Now, then click Apply to begin the Update Port Assignment process.

    Next, Catalyst Center will complete some in-flight validations. Once complete, click Submit.

    Finally, to verify the port was correctly set, log into Leaf1 using mRemote on Jumphost1. Then execute the command: show run interface GigabitEthernet 1/0/2

    You should see the port set as a trunk port and the native VLAN of 10 and allowed Vlans as 10,101,102. This port configuration will allow the AP to function correctly. Repeat the port verification for Leaf2 as well.

By leveraging Cisco Catalyst Center, PseudoCo can rapidly deploy a fully functional fabric with built-in Virtual Networks (VNs) and Security Group Tag (SGT) support, dramatically reducing manual configuration and human error. This Cisco-native approach is a key differentiator, enabling centralized orchestration, simplified operations, and seamless integration with identity services like ISE—positioning the network as both a connectivity platform and a core enforcement point for Zero Trust policy.

    EVPN Overview:

    You Choose: BGP EVPN Campus Fabric

    🛑NOTE: If you chose SDA and already deployed it please skip this EVPN Section. 

    PseudoCo has decided to prioritize standards-based design, deterministic control, and architectural flexibility while still enabling modern segmentation and secure connectivity.

    The network engineering leadership has emphasized:

    “We need a fabric that gives us deep control of routing behavior, scalability, and multi-domain integration.”

    PseudoCo expects rapid growth, potential mergers, and hybrid environment expansion — requiring a fabric that can integrate cleanly with multiple network domains and operational models.

    What This Means for PseudoCo

    By choosing EVPN, PseudoCo is building a campus designed around:

    Standards-Based Architecture

    • Open control plane using BGP

    • Alignment with data center and service provider design models

    Deterministic Network Behavior

    • Deep visibility into route propagation and traffic flow

    • Predictable convergence and failover characteristics

    Architectural Flexibility

    • Easier integration into multi-domain routing strategies

    • Strong alignment with existing BGP-based WAN and data center designs

    Security Through Integrated Segmentation

    • Segmentation still enforced using identity and policy systems

    • Fabric becomes a high-performance, policy-aware transport layer

    🧭

    EVPN: Choose Your Provisioning Path

    Two ways to build the same fabric

    PseudoCo has committed to a BGP EVPN campus fabric. How you build it is now your decision. This lab supports two complete, independent paths that produce the same end state — the same fabric, the same Catalyst Center configuration, the same wireless service. Pick one and follow it through to the end.

    🛑 Do not run both paths on the same pod. They configure the same devices. Choose one, finish it, then move on to the Segmentation sections. If you want to see the other approach, read it — don't run it.

    Path 1 Traditional Provisioning

    You type the configuration. You paste IOS-XE CLI into three Catalyst 9300 switches over SSH, then use the Catalyst Center GUI to discover the devices, build templates, and provision wireless through the 9800 controller GUI.

    Best if you want to:

    • See every line of BGP EVPN VXLAN configuration and understand what it does
    • Learn the Catalyst Center and 9800 user interfaces hands-on
    • Work entirely from the Jumphost with no additional tooling

    What you need: a browser, mRemoteNG, and the ability to copy and paste.

    Start here: 1. EVPN Fabric Provisioning

    Path 2 Automated — Infrastructure as Code

    The configuration types itself. A set of Ansible playbooks drives Catalyst Center through its REST API. The fabric design lives in Jinja2 templates in a Git repository; Catalyst Center renders and pushes it to the switches. A final read-only stage proves the fabric matches the design.

    Best if you want to:

    • See how a network is treated as versioned, reviewable source code
    • Understand how a design change flows from a Git commit to live switch configuration
    • Learn the Catalyst Center template model that scales past three switches

    What you need: the dCloud VPN, a terminal, and your POD number from the lab printout.

    Start here: 1. Prepare Your Workstation & Script Server

    Why offer both?

    Clicking through a GUI is how most engineers learn a technology, and it is the right tool when you are building one fabric once. It does not survive contact with fifty sites. Infrastructure as Code applies the discipline of software engineering to network configuration: the design is written down in files, changes are reviewed and version-controlled, and the same change is applied identically everywhere.

    The automated path in this lab is not a shortcut around understanding EVPN — the Jinja2 templates contain exactly the same VRF, VNI, NVE, and BGP constructs you would type by hand. The difference is that the design is expressed once, as data, and rendered for every device.

    “Manual configuration doesn't scale, and it doesn't leave an audit trail. If we can't diff it, we can't govern it.”

    The two paths cover the same ground in a different order

    This is the single most important thing to understand before you start. The traditional path builds the fabric first, then brings Catalyst Center in afterwards. The automated pipeline is the other way round: it builds Catalyst Center first, and then uses Catalyst Center to push the fabric configuration in stage 09.

    Use this table to map between the two:

    Lab objective Traditional path Automated path
    Site hierarchy, network settings, credentials Done in the Catalyst Center GUI during discovery Stages 01 – 03
    Discover devices and assign them to sites 2. EVPN Catalyst Center Discovery / Templates Stages 04 – 05
    Build the EVPN fabric configuration 1. EVPN Fabric Provisioning — paste CLI over SSH Stage 06 uploads the templates, stage 09 deploys them
    Network profiles, 802.1X templates, wireless design 2. EVPN Catalyst Center Discovery / Templates Stages 06 – 07
    Provision switches, WLC, and access points Catalyst Center GUI, then the 9800 GUI Stage 08 for the switches and WLC; stage 10 for the access points, once they have booted
    Configure and test the wireless SSID 4. Configure & Test WLC — 9800 GUI Stage 07 builds it; 6. Verify Wireless & Provision the Access Point confirms it
    Prove the fabric matches the design Manual show commands Stage 11 — generates a pass/fail evidence report

    👀 Tip: even if you choose the traditional path, read 4. Understanding the EVPN Template Model. It explains how the same fabric design is expressed as data, and it is the part of the automated path most useful in a real network.

    🧭 Make your choice

    Building it by hand? Continue to EVPN Fabric and WLC — Manual Provisioning.

    Building it as code? Continue to EVPN Fabric and WLC — Infrastructure as Code.

    Both tracks finish at the same place: an EVPN fabric and a working wireless SSID. Pick one and stay on it — running both against the same pod will produce confusing results.

    EVPN Fabric and WLC — Manual Provisioning

    📄

    1. EVPN Fabric Provisioning

    You Choose: BGP EVPN Campus Fabric

    BGP EVPN VXLAN is a campus network solution for Cisco Catalyst 9000 Series Switches running Cisco IOS-XE software. It is designed to provide a unified overlay network solution addressing the challenges and drawbacks of existing technologies. Traditionally, VLANs have been the standard method for providing network segmentation in campus networks. VLANs use loop prevention techniques such as Spanning Tree Protocol (STP), which impose restrictions on network design and resiliency. Further, there is a limitation with the number of VLANs that can be used to address layer 2 segments (4094 VLANs). VXLAN is designed to overcome the inherent limitations of VLANs and STP. Many customer’s also enjoy the added benefit of macro-segmentation through the use of VRF’s (virtual-routing and forwarding) and the ability stretch subnets across L3 boundaries.

    A detailed explanation of BGP-EVPN VXLAN can be found here: https://www.cisco.com/c/en/us/td/docs/switches/lan/catalyst9300/software/release/17-15/configuration_guide/vxlan/b_1715_bgp_evpn_vxlan_9300_cg/bgp_evpn_vxlan_overview.html

    During this section of the lab, you will configure a BGP-EVPN Fabric between three Catalyst 9300 switches. The switches have already been configured with a basic OSPF configuration allowing IP connectivity between the Loopback0 interfaces. Utilize mRemoteNG on the Jumphost to access the three Catalyst switches. Next follow the steps below and copy and paste each of the config sections into the console of the correct switchs.

    NOTE: Some configs go on multiple devices, DO NOT write your configs until you verify the fabric is up and working correctly. 

    Configure the VRF / Tenant definitions on the 3 switches

    VRF’s (virtual-routing and forwarding) provide the layer-3 separation (macro-segmentation) within the EVPN fabric. In general, VRFs have little to no communication between one another allowing complete isolation across the fabric. Many organizations find this functionality critical for keeping users and devices associated with guests or contractors separate from their corporate network.

    VRF definitions are attached to layer-3 interfaces (physical or virtual) thereby isolating the traffic to the VRF routing domain. This lab will contain 3 VRF’s (Main, IOT, and PROD) simulating a typical customer environment.

    BORDER-SPINE

    vrf definition IOT
 rd 172.30.255.3:102
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:102
  route-target import 65535:102
  route-target export 65535:102 stitching
  route-target import 65535:102 stitching
 exit-address-family
!
vrf definition Main
 rd 172.30.255.3:10
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:10
  route-target import 65535:10
  route-target export 65535:10 stitching
  route-target import 65535:10 stitching
 exit-address-family
!
vrf definition PROD
 rd 172.30.255.3:101
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:101
  route-target import 65535:101
  route-target export 65535:101 stitching
  route-target import 65535:101 stitching
 exit-address-family
!

    LEAF1

    vrf definition IOT
 rd 172.30.255.1:102
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:102
  route-target import 65535:102
  route-target export 65535:102 stitching
  route-target import 65535:102 stitching
 exit-address-family
!
vrf definition Main
 rd 172.30.255.1:10
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:10
  route-target import 65535:10
  route-target export 65535:10 stitching
  route-target import 65535:10 stitching
 exit-address-family
!
vrf definition PROD
 rd 172.30.255.1:101
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:101
  route-target import 65535:101
  route-target export 65535:101 stitching
  route-target import 65535:101 stitching
 exit-address-family
 !

    LEAF2

    vrf definition IOT
 rd 172.30.255.2:102
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:102
  route-target import 65535:102
  route-target export 65535:102 stitching
  route-target import 65535:102 stitching
 exit-address-family
!
vrf definition Main
 rd 172.30.255.2:10
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:10
  route-target import 65535:10
  route-target export 65535:10 stitching
  route-target import 65535:10 stitching
 exit-address-family
!
vrf definition PROD
 rd 172.30.255.2:101
 !
 address-family ipv4
  route-target export 65535:101
  route-target import 65535:101
  route-target export 65535:101 stitching
  route-target import 65535:101 stitching
 exit-address-family
!

    Configure Static Multicast Replication

    BGP-EVPN VXLAN utilizes a routed underlay network to interconnect the switching infrastructure. VXLAN also allows the encapsulation of layer-2 networks across the routed underlay. Emulating a traditional layer-2 network is accomplished by forwarding BUM (broadcast, unknown unicast, and multicast) traffic via the underlay utilizing ingress or multicast replication. This lab will utilize static multicast replication to forward BUM traffic.

    LEAF1 / LEAF2 🛑 The config below goes on BOTH Leaf1 and 2.

    l2vpn evpn
 replication-type static
 router-id loopback 0 
  ! 

    Define L2VNI VXLAN to VLAN mappings

    BGP-EVPN VXLAN has the capability to carry 16M layer 2 segments. This is done by mapping the local access VLANS to a unique L2VNI (virtual network identifier) across the fabric.

    The following VLANs are the access/client VLANS on the leaf switches utilizing subnets from the table below. Configure the L2VNI EVPN instance and map to the access VLAN.

    VLAN ID L2VNI VLAN Name Subnet 
    VLAN 10 100010 Main 10.10.255.x/24
    VLAN 101 100101 PROD 10.101.255.x/24
    VLAN 102 100102 IOT 10.102.255.x/24

     

    LEAF1 / LEAF2  🛑 The config below goes on BOTH Leaf1 and 2.

    Vlan 10
Name Main
Vlan 101
Name PROD
Vlan 102
Name IOT

l2vpn evpn instance 10 vlan-based
 encapsulation vxlan
!
l2vpn evpn instance 101 vlan-based
 encapsulation vxlan
!
l2vpn evpn instance 102 vlan-based
 encapsulation vxlan

vlan configuration 10
 member evpn-instance 10 vni 100010 
vlan configuration 101
 member evpn-instance 101 vni 100101 
vlan configuration 102
 member evpn-instance 102 vni 100102
!

    Define L3VNI’s

    When endpoints connected to a Layer 2 VNI need to communicate with endpoints belonging to different IP subnets, they send the traffic to their default gateway. Communication between endpoints belonging to different Layer 2 VNIs is possible only through a Layer 3 routing function. In an EVPN VXLAN deployment, the various Layer 2 segments that are defined by combining the local VLANs and the global Layer 2 VNIs can be associated to a VRF to communicate between each other.

    BORDER-SPINE / LEAF1 / LEAF2  🛑 The config below goes on ALL (3) Switches

    vlan 1010
 name L3-VRF-CORE-VLAN-10
!
vlan 1101
 name L3-VRF-CORE-VLAN-101
!
vlan 1102
 name L3-VRF-CORE-VLAN-102
!
vlan configuration 1010
 member vni 110010
vlan configuration 1101
 member vni 110101
vlan configuration 1102
 member vni 110102
!

    Configure Distributed Anycast Gateway (DAG)

    Configure the gateways for VLANs 10 (Main), 101 (PROD), and 102 (IOT) on the leaf switches. Notice the use of the same MAC address on the SVI’s allowing L2 mobility across the fabric leaf nodes.  As clients move, they will not have to re- ARP for the default gateway as it stays the same during movement.

    LEAF1 / LEAF2  🛑 The config below goes on BOTH Leaf1 and 2.

    interface Vlan10
 mac-address 0001.0001.0010
 vrf forwarding Main
 ip dhcp relay source-interface Loopback0
 ip address 10.10.255.1 255.255.255.0
 ip helper-address global 198.18.5.102
!
interface Vlan101
 mac-address 0001.0001.0101
 vrf forwarding PROD
 ip dhcp relay source-interface Loopback0
 ip address 10.101.255.1 255.255.255.0
 ip helper-address global 198.18.5.102
!
interface Vlan102
 mac-address 0001.0001.0102
 vrf forwarding IOT
 ip dhcp relay source-interface Loopback0
 ip address 10.102.255.1 255.255.255.0
 ip helper-address global 198.18.5.102
!

    Configure an SVI for the L3VNI and assign to each VRF/Tenant

    Efficient (symmetric) VRF traffic forwarding between the switches participating in the EVPN fabric is accomplished by anchoring the L3VNI to an SVI which is mapped to a VRF for macro-segmentation. The SVI is set to always be up (no-autostate) and anchored to the loopback0 interface which will be encapsulated via the NVE interface (defined later).

    BORDER-SPINE / LEAF1 / LEAF2   🛑 The config below goes on ALL (3) Switches

    interface Vlan1010
 vrf forwarding Main
 ip unnumbered Loopback0
 no autostate
!
interface Vlan1101
 vrf forwarding PROD
 ip unnumbered Loopback0
 no autostate
!
interface Vlan1102
 vrf forwarding IOT
 ip unnumbered Loopback0
 no autostate
!

    Create Network Virtualization Endpoint (NVE) interface on Leaf1 and Leaf2

    The NVE interface on each device is responsible for encapsulating the L2 / L3 traffic from each VNI into the VXLAN format and forwarding across the network. The VNI interface is anchored to the loopback0 interface to allows efficient forwarding across all layer-3 paths in the network allowing ECMP (equal cost multipath) to be utilized if available. In addition, the NVE interface allows SGT’s (scalable group tags) to be carried across the network providing micro-segmentation.

    LEAF1 / LEAF2   🛑 The config below goes on BOTH Leaf1 and 2.

    interface nve1
 no ip address
 source-interface Loopback0
 host-reachability protocol bgp
 group-based-policy
 member vni 110010 vrf Main
 member vni 110101 vrf PROD
 member vni 110102 vrf IOT
 member vni 100010 mcast-group 232.1.1.1
 member vni 100101 mcast-group 232.1.1.1
 member vni 100102 mcast-group 232.1.1.1
!

    BORDER-SPINE

    interface nve1
 no ip address
 source-interface Loopback0
 host-reachability protocol bgp
 member vni 110010 vrf Main
 member vni 110101 vrf PROD
 member vni 110102 vrf IOT
!

    Configure BGP peering to SPINE, EVPN address family, and VRF

    Learning of MAC / IP address across the fabric is accomplished via BGP (control plane) between the switches, hence the name BGP-EVPN. The endpoint addresses are learned via traditional methods such as ARP and MAC learning on the local leaf switch, sending the information via BGP to a router-reflector (RR), the BORDER-SPINE in the lab network. The information learned is used between the various switches to establish VXLAN tunnels thereby creating layer-2/3 segmented networks across the routed underlay.

    LEAF1

    router bgp 65535
 bgp router-id 172.30.255.1
 bgp log-neighbor-changes
 no bgp default ipv4-unicast
 neighbor 172.30.255.3 remote-as 65535
 neighbor 172.30.255.3 update-source Loopback0
 !
 address-family ipv4
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family l2vpn evpn
  neighbor 172.30.255.3 activate
  neighbor 172.30.255.3 send-community both
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family ipv4 vrf IOT
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family ipv4 vrf Main
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family ipv4 vrf PROD
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
 exit-address-family
!

    LEAF2

    router bgp 65535
 bgp router-id 172.30.255.2
 bgp log-neighbor-changes
 no bgp default ipv4-unicast
 neighbor 172.30.255.3 remote-as 65535
 neighbor 172.30.255.3 update-source Loopback0
 !
 address-family ipv4
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family l2vpn evpn
  neighbor 172.30.255.3 activate
  neighbor 172.30.255.3 send-community both
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family ipv4 vrf IOT
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family ipv4 vrf Main
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family ipv4 vrf PROD
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
 exit-address-family
!

    Configure BGP / EVPN-RR on BORDER-SPINE

    BGP, specifically iBGP, requires a full-mesh of peering between all switches participating in the fabric. Removing the full-mesh configuration requires the use of a route-reflector (RR). This function is typically done on the spine devices. Since the lab only has one spine, we will utilize it as the RR for our fabric, with the leaf switches as RR clients.

    BORDER-SPINE

    router bgp 65535
 bgp router-id 172.30.255.3
 bgp log-neighbor-changes
 no bgp default ipv4-unicast
 neighbor 172.30.255.1 remote-as 65535
 neighbor 172.30.255.1 update-source Loopback0
 neighbor 172.30.255.2 remote-as 65535
 neighbor 172.30.255.2 update-source Loopback0
!
 address-family l2vpn evpn
  neighbor 172.30.255.1 activate
  neighbor 172.30.255.1 send-community both
  neighbor 172.30.255.1 route-reflector-client
  neighbor 172.30.255.2 activate
  neighbor 172.30.255.2 send-community both
  neighbor 172.30.255.2 route-reflector-client
 exit-address-family
 !

    Configure Spine external interface toward SD-WAN router (VRF-Lite)

    BGP-EVPN VXLAN utilizes overlay tunnels between the fabric devices to encapsulate the traffic across the fabric underlay.  Exiting the fabric requires the use of a border device. The border device de-encapsulates the VXLAN traffic and forwards to the external network via layer-2 or layer-3 connections. The BORDER-SPINE in the lab is the Layer 3 exit point to the SD-WAN router via VRF-Lite. Separate sub-interfaces for each VRF are defined on the spine.

    BORDER-SPINE

    default interface GigabitEthernet 1/0/48

interface GigabitEthernet1/0/48
 Switchport mode trunk
 cts manual
 no shutdown 
!
Vlan 10 
name Main
vlan 101
name PROD
Vlan 102
name IOT

interface vlan 10
 description Main
 vrf forwarding Main
 ip address 192.168.255.1 255.255.255.254
!
interface vlan 101

 vrf forwarding PROD
 ip address 192.168.255.3 255.255.255.254
!
interface vlan 102
 vrf forwarding IOT
 ip address 192.168.255.5 255.255.255.254
!

    Configure Spine BGP peering towards SD-WAN router

    BORDER-SPINE

    router bgp 65535
 neighbor 192.168.255.6 remote-as 65534
!
address-family ipv4
  network 172.30.255.1 mask 255.255.255.255
  network 172.30.255.2 mask 255.255.255.255
  network 172.30.255.3 mask 255.255.255.255
  neighbor 192.168.255.6 activate
  neighbor 192.168.255.6 send-community both
 exit-address-family
 !
address-family ipv4 vrf IOT
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
  neighbor 192.168.255.4 remote-as 65534
  neighbor 192.168.255.4 activate
  neighbor 192.168.255.4 send-community both
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family ipv4 vrf Main
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
  neighbor 192.168.255.0 remote-as 65534
  neighbor 192.168.255.0 activate
  neighbor 192.168.255.0 send-community both
 exit-address-family
 !
 address-family ipv4 vrf PROD
  advertise l2vpn evpn
  redistribute connected
  neighbor 192.168.255.2 remote-as 65534
  neighbor 192.168.255.2 activate
  neighbor 192.168.255.2 send-community both
 exit-address-family
!

    Configure access / client and AP ports

    The Windows clients are located on Gig 1/0/1 and Gig 1/0/3 of each leaf switch. The AP’s are located on Gig 1/0/2 of each leaf switch. IEEE 802.1x policies and templates will be applied via templates in Catalyst Center after we discover and provision the devices next. 

    LEAF1/ LEAF2 🛑 The config below goes on BOTH Leaf1 and 2.

    interface GigabitEthernet1/0/1
 description Client
 switchport mode access
 device-tracking attach-policy IPDT_POLICY
 source template WIRED_DOT1X_CLOSED
 spanning-tree portfast
 ip nbar protocol-discovery
!
interface GigabitEthernet1/0/2
 description AP
 switchport trunk native vlan 10
 switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,101,102
 switchport mode trunk
 spanning-tree portfast trunk
 cts manual
  policy static sgt 2 trusted
  propagate sgt
!
interface GigabitEthernet1/0/3
 description Client
 switchport mode access
 device-tracking attach-policy IPDT_POLICY
 source template WIRED_DOT1X_CLOSED
 spanning-tree portfast
 ip nbar protocol-discovery
!

    Verify BGP-EVPN Operations

    Issue various show commands to verify reachability across the EVPN fabric. We will only see items in VRF Main as there is an access point attached to the Leafs keeping the SVI 10 up. SVI 101 and 102 will continue to be down until we get to client testing later in the lab. Issue the following command on the BORDER-SPINE node. Ensure you are seeing at least 1 prefix being advertised from the two LEAF nodes.  You should also see the NVE Peers up on Leaf1 and 2.

    Prefix verification command for:  BORDER-SPINE

    show bgp l2vpn evpn summary

    👀 If you have just completed the Border/Spine configuration, the show command can take up to two minutes to populate. Verify that you are learning at least 1 additional prefix as the fabric is populated with devices. 

    NVE peers verification command for BORDER-SPINE. You should see (3) NVE peers per Leaf Switch.

    show nve peers

    Provisioning a BGP EVPN-based fabric gives PseudoCo a highly flexible and standards-based architecture built on VXLAN overlays and robust routing principles. This approach allows for granular control over the network design, multi-vendor interoperability, and massive scalability—making it ideal for organizations with deep routing expertise. While it requires more manual configuration and operational knowledge, EVPN empowers architects with deterministic control and customization capabilities, enabling a tailored solution that can adapt to complex and evolving enterprise requirements.

    EVPN - Discovery Jobs in Catalyst Center

    In this section, we create and configure a device discovery job in Catalyst Center. We will be discovering all three 9300 switches as well as the 9800-CL Wireless LAN controller. We will be using pre-configured credentials and properties for the Discovery. Once the discovery is complete and the devices are in inventory, we will then assign the devices to their respective site and then provision the devices, which will automate the integration with the ISE (Identity Services Engine) for policy and segmentation.

    First, login to Cisco Catalyst Center either using the Chrome favorite in the bookmark or you can login directly by browsing to: https://cat-center.corp.pseudoco.com and entering the username and password below.

    Username: admin

    Password: Demo@C!sco

    From the main menu, click on Tools -> Discovery.

    Observe that there are currently no configured discoveries.

    Click on Add Discovery to start the first discovery job configuration.

    Configure the first device discovery job for the three 9300 switches which make up our Site 105 fabric:

    • Start by giving the discovery job the name Site-105-Discovery.
    • Next for discovery type select IP Address Range.
    • For the IP address range we will be using the loopback interfaces which were pre-configured on the switches 172.30.255.1 – 172.30.255.3.
    • Under Advanced Settings, select Loopback (if applicable) 
    • Verify all settings are correct and then Click Next.

    The next step will be to provide credentials for the discovery. In this case, the necessary credentials have been saved to the Global CLI Credentials. For the properties highlighted below, select the pre-configured global credential for each.

    CLI = CLI Admin

    SNMP = v2c read and v2c write

    NETCONF = Don't enable NETCONF 

    HTTP(S) = HTTP(S) write, HTTP(S) read (select Show advanced settings to see HTTP(S) credentials)

    Leave the rest default once complete Click Next.

    The next step is to schedule the job and select the site to assign the devices to. First, leave the default to run the task Now. Click "Select Site" in the Assign devices to an existing site pop-up. In the Site Hierarchy select North Carolina -> Durham -> Site-105 -> Main. Once selected, click Save.

    🛑 NOTE: If you are unable to see MAIN , please use control-scroll to enlarge the window. Alternatively, you can search for MAIN in the Search Hierarchy field. This is an issue when accessing the Catalyst Center GUI via RDP.

    Next, verify all setting are correct as below and click Next.

    Finally, review the Summary of your selections, then click Start Discovery. This will then start the discovery process.

    👀 This discovery job will now be saved under the main menu under Tools -> Discovery. You can use this discovery again to re-discover the devices if needed.

    Click on View discovery so we can verify the discovery of our campus fabric 9300 switches.

    👀 Please allow UP TO 3-5 MINUTES for the discovery process to complete, and you may have to hit Refresh. You can view the discovery directly or from the main menu under Tools -> Discovery. Verify that THREE switches were discovered correctly.

    Congratulations! 🎉 You have successfully discovered your infrastructure and provisioned the basic settings! You ROCK! It's time to apply our 802.1x security policies via templates in Catalyst Center! 

    EVPN - Catalyst Center Templates

    In the last section, you had the opportunity to deploy a fabric via CLI. If we had needed to implement this on a larger scale, we could have utilized templates to push the configurations to the infrastructure.

    Do you remember the VRFs we created in the BGP EVPN configuration? We now need a method to assign our users and devices to the appropriate VRF. Our goal is to deploy this campus so that devices are dynamically granted access to the network and security policies are enforced. To achieve this, we will use 802.1x. We are now going to explore templates in Catalyst Center.

    Catalyst Center templates are powerful tools designed to automate and standardize the configuration of network devices across their entire lifecycle—from initial onboarding to ongoing provisioning and updates.

    Types of Templates

    • Regular Templates: These define specific CLI configurations using scripting languages like Jinja2 or Apache Velocity. They support variables, conditional logic, and loops, allowing for dynamic and reusable configurations tailored to different devices or scenarios.
    • Composite Templates: These are sequences of regular templates executed in a defined order. They enable modular and scalable deployments by combining multiple configuration steps into a single workflow.

    Template Usage Scenarios

    • Day 0 (Onboarding) Templates: Used during the initial setup of devices, often in conjunction with Plug and Play (PnP) workflows. These templates provide the foundational configurations necessary to bring devices online.

    • Day N (Provisioning) Templates: Applied post-onboarding for ongoing configuration changes, feature deployments, or policy updates. They offer flexibility for managing devices throughout their operational lifecycle.

    Create an 802.1x template

    First, we need to create a template. In Catalyst Center, Navigate to Design -> CLI Templates. You will see that Catalyst Center comes with some canned templates that can be helpful examples when crafting your own templates. Click Add to create your own.

    In the configuration window, enter the following attributes and click Add Device Details.
    Template Name: Leaf 802.1x
    Project Name: Onboarding Configuration
    Software Type: IOS-XE

    Catalyst Center needs to know what type of infrastructure this template is intended for. In the section for Device Details,  click Add Device Details. 

    First, select Switches and Hubs. Our template will be intended for Catalyst 9300 Series Switches, so we will select that as an option under Device Series. Next click Add.

    Click Continue.

    It is now time to craft our template! Templates can be easy and they can get very complex. Catalyst Center allows users to leverage scripting to create a dynamic nature to the template. This can be handy when managing large networks at scale. We are going to keep this simple by just providing the CLI commands.

    🛑 Paste the following text into the template editor. 🛑

    COPY and PASTE the configuration below:

    service password-encryption
!
no logging console
!
no ip domain lookup
netconf-yang
no ip dhcp snooping information option
no ip tftp blocksize
access-session attributes filter-list list ISE-DS-list
 vlan-id
 cdp
 lldp
 dhcp
 http

access-session authentication attributes filter-spec include list ISE-DS-list
access-session accounting attributes filter-spec include list ISE-DS-list

service-template CRITICAL_DATA_ACCESS
 access-group PERMIT-ISE
service-template CRITICAL_VOICE_ACCESS
 access-group PERMIT-ISE
 voice vlan
dot1x system-auth-control
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all AAA_SVR_DOWN_AUTHD_HOST
 match result-type aaa-timeout
 match authorization-status authorized
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all AAA_SVR_DOWN_UNAUTHD_HOST
 match result-type aaa-timeout
 match authorization-status unauthorized
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all AUTHC_SUCCESS_AUTHZ_FAIL
 match authorization-status unauthorized
 match result-type success
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all DOT1X
 match method dot1x
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all DOT1X_FAILED
 match method dot1x
 match result-type method dot1x authoritative
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all DOT1X_NO_RESP
 match method dot1x
 match result-type method dot1x agent-not-found
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all DOT1X_TIMEOUT
 match method dot1x
 match result-type method dot1x method-timeout
!
class-map type control subscriber match-any IN_CRITICAL_AUTH
 match activated-service-template CRITICAL_DATA_ACCESS
 match activated-service-template CRITICAL_VOICE_ACCESS
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all MAB
 match method mab
!
class-map type control subscriber match-all MAB_FAILED
 match method mab
 match result-type method mab authoritative
!
class-map type control subscriber match-none NOT_IN_CRITICAL_AUTH
 match activated-service-template CRITICAL_DATA_ACCESS
 match activated-service-template CRITICAL_VOICE_ACCESS
!
!
!
policy-map type control subscriber DOT1X_MAB_POLICY
 event session-started match-all
  10 class always do-until-failure
   10 authenticate using dot1x retries 2 retry-time 0 priority 10
 event authentication-failure match-first
  5 class DOT1X_FAILED do-until-failure
   10 terminate dot1x
   20 authenticate using mab priority 20
  10 class AAA_SVR_DOWN_UNAUTHD_HOST do-until-failure
   10 clear-authenticated-data-hosts-on-port
   20 activate service-template CRITICAL_DATA_ACCESS

    This CLI closely mimics what Catalyst Center deploys for 802.1x when doing Software Defined Access. We have also configured ports GigabitEthernet 1/0/1-3 to accommodate the end devices we have attached in this lab. This crude template is nothing more than the commands that we would traditionally enter via CLI directly on the switch. No knowledge of scripting required! Click Commit

    First, enter Commit Notes then click Commit. 

    Click Yes to ignore content warnings. Next, you will see a notification that your template was created successfully. 

    Create a Network Profile and associate it to Leaf 1 and Leaf 2

    Now that we have a template created, we need to associate the template to the Leaf1 and Leaf2 switches. To do this we need to create a Network Profile.


    In Catalyst Center, click Design -> Network Profiles, then click Add Profile -> then select Switching

    Next, you will provide a name (802-X-Profile). Select Day-N Template(s), then click Attach Template. Finally, select Leaf 802.1x and then click Add. Close out the Add Template pop-out by clicking the X.

    Save the Network Profile.

    Next, we need to assign a site to the Network Profile we just created. Select Assign Site.

    Select Site-105 / MAIN, then click Save.

    🎉 Congratulations! Your Network Profile has been assigned to Site-105 / MAIN. Time to provision your new Template/Network Profile to your leaf switches.

    Provision the Network Profile to Leaf 1 and Leaf 2

    It’s now time to apply the template we just created to the appropriate switches. We will do this by provisioning the template we just created. Let’s start by logging into one of the leaf switches and checking the current configuration.  Run the show command below.


    show run | section policy-map

    You will notice that the switch currently has limited or no policy-map configurations. We need to augment this configuration with additional CLI commands necessary to accommodate this environment. Next we will apply our template. If you are interested in watching the switch and Catalyst Center interaction, enter terminal monitor at this time.


    Next, open Catalyst Center and go to Provision -> Inventory.

    Since our objective is to apply 802.1x configuration to leaf switches, Select Site_105-Leaf1 and Site_105-Leaf2. Then select Actions -> Provision -> Provision Device.

    Leaf1 and Leaf2 have already been assigned to a site. Click Next.

    Select Site_105-Leaf1 or Site_105-Leaf2. Once selected you can see the templates that will be applied to the switch during the provisioning process. Click Next to proceed.

    Catalyst Center provides you a graphical representation of the provision process. Take a moment to review the configurations that will be applied to the infrastructure. In the Advanced Configuration you will see the template that will be provisioned. Click Next.

    Select Now and give the task a name, then click Apply. If you are interested in seeing the log events on the leaf switches make sure to have an active SSH session with terminal monitor running.

    Click Submit once your compliance checks have completed.

    You will observe several logs associated with the applied configuration occur in your SSH .

    In Catalyst Center, you can observe the provisioning status as in previous steps by going to Activities -> Tasks.

    Return to your SSH session to view the policy-map applied to the Leaf switches with the command:

    show run | section policy-map

    In this section, you successfully leveraged Cisco Catalyst Center to automate device provisioning and configuration using templates, transforming how PseudoCo manages its network at scale. By standardizing configurations and applying them consistently across the infrastructure, you reduced manual effort, minimized configuration drift, and accelerated deployment timelines. A key Cisco differentiator is the tight integration between automation, assurance, and policy—allowing PseudoCo to not only deploy configurations rapidly, but also validate intent and maintain consistency across the entire fabric from a single platform. This establishes a powerful operational model where the network is not just deployed—but continuously aligned with business and security intent.
