    Your fabric is built and provisioned. Now extend it to wireless: 3. Wireless Overview.

    📄

    3. Wireless Overview

    Extending Secure Access to the Edge: Configuring Wireless with FlexConnect

    After establishing the foundation of the campus fabric, your next priority as lead architect for PseudoCo is enabling secure, resilient wireless access that aligns with the company’s Zero Trust strategy — not just inside headquarters, but across branches and remote sites.

    PseudoCo’s workforce is increasingly mobile. Employees move between headquarters, branch offices, and home environments daily. At the same time, IoT and OT devices are being deployed at edge locations where local survivability and consistent policy enforcement are critical.

    To support this shift, PseudoCo has standardized on enterprise wireless infrastructure from Cisco, and now needs to ensure wireless access behaves as a secure extension of the fabric and identity architecture, not a separate network silo.

    The Business Driver

    PseudoCo’s branch locations present unique challenges:

    • WAN connectivity may experience latency or temporary disruption

    • Local devices (IoT, printers, scanners, badge readers) must continue operating during outages

    • Security policy must remain consistent whether traffic breaks out locally or traverses the fabric

    • Identity and segmentation must still apply to wireless users and devices

    PseudoCo leadership has made it clear:

    “Wireless cannot be the weak link in our Zero Trust strategy. Policy must follow users — even at the edge.”

    Why FlexConnect Matters to PseudoCo

    By deploying wireless in FlexConnect mode, PseudoCo enables:

    Local Survivability

    • Branch APs can continue forwarding traffic even if controller connectivity is interrupted

    • Critical business operations remain online

    Local Switching with Central Policy

    • Traffic can break out locally while still enforcing segmentation and identity policy

    • Supports Zero Trust principles closer to the user

    Consistent User Experience

    • Authentication, authorization, and policy enforcement remain consistent across locations

    • Supports seamless roaming between HQ, branch, and remote environments

    Operational Efficiency

    • Centralized configuration with distributed enforcement

    • Faster branch deployment and simplified operations

    This directly supports PseudoCo’s goal of maintaining consistent identity-driven policy across campus, branch, and remote users. 

    Story Context for the Lab

    PseudoCo is in the middle of a branch modernization initiative. Many legacy branch AP deployments relied on centralized switching models, which created bottlenecks and introduced risk during WAN instability.

    Your mission is to transform wireless into a distributed security enforcement layer that:

    • Extends fabric segmentation to wireless endpoints

    • Integrates with identity services for authentication and policy

    • Maintains operations during WAN disruption

    • Preserves user experience for critical applications

    What This Means for Your Role

    In this task, you are not just configuring wireless profiles —
    You are enabling PseudoCo to:

    • Deliver Zero Trust access over wireless

    • Maintain business continuity at the branch edge

    • Extend segmentation and identity policy to every endpoint

    • Support future growth in IoT and edge computing

    Forward Story Tie-In

    Once FlexConnect is deployed, PseudoCo will be able to:

    • Enforce identity-based policy across wired and wireless

    • Extend segmentation across campus, WAN, and data center

    • Validate user experience and application reachability using observability tooling

    • Support secure application access regardless of user location

    Wireless is now a first-class citizen in PseudoCo’s secure network architecture — not just an access method, but a policy enforcement and visibility point.

    📄

    4. Configure & Test WLC

    Task: Configure the Catalyst 9800 WLC for Flex-Connect

    In this section we will configure the Catalyst 9800 WLC for Flex-Connect, then verify the wireless workstation can connect to the SSID which we provisioned.

    Configure the WLC and AP’s for a Flex-Connect SSID

    First log into the WLC by using the favorite in the Google Chrome bookmark (C9800-WLC) or you can browse directly to https://wlc.corp.pseudoco.com.  Login using (admin/C1sco12345) and explore the Home Dashboard to verify there is at least 1 AP registered to the controller.

    👀 A minimum of one AP is required. If there are no APs registered, please engage the Lab Proctor.

    Next, we will configure the SSID to include a Unique Identifier of your POD#.  This POD# is located on your lab printout sheet.

     
    👀 If you cannot locate your POD#, please see a Proctor.
     
    Once you have identified your POD#. From the menu Click Configuration -> Tags & Profiles -> WLANS.

    You will find one pre-configured WLAN with the Name: DCLOUD-XAR-FLEX-Profile

    Next, click on the DCLOUD-XAR-FLEX-Profile to open the Edit WLAN window. Once in the window, edit the default SSID: PSEUDOCO-POD# to reflect your POD# and then click Update & Apply to Device.

    🛑 NOTE: The SSID format needs to be exact to match a pre-configured Group Policy which sets our authentication settings.  PLEASE ENSURE THERE IS NOT AN EMPTY SPACE AT THE END OF THE SSID, AND ENSURE THAT SINGLE DIGIT POD NUMBERS ARE PREFIXED WITH A ZERO (EXAMPLE: PSEUDOCO-POD01).

    Now that we have configured the SSID to match your corresponding assigned POD#, we will configure the AP’s into Flex-Connect mode. To do this, click Configuration -> Wireless –> Access Points. You will find one or two registered AP’s.

    Next, click on one of the AP’s to bring up the Edit AP window:

    Follow the below steps to update the configurations on your AP:

    • First in the General Section, give your AP a friendly name and a location description of your choice.
    • Next in the Tags section for Policy select the DCLOUD-XAR-FLEX-PT policy
    • For Site select FLEX-SITE
    • Finally, click Update & Apply to Device. The AP will then reboot and come up with the new configuration.

    🛑 NOTE: It could take between 1-3 min For the AP to re-boot and register again. 

    Repeat the process for the second AP (if your POD has one). Feel free to explore the WLC configurations to better understand the new Catalyst wireless controller configuration methodologies.

    Step 2: Verify wireless client connectivity to the Flex-Connect SSID

    Connect to Workstation-1:

    First, log into Workstation-1 via RDP by using the mRemote Client on the Jumphost1. The session is pre-configured to automatically connect you using the user Pat in the PROD network.  We will be exploring different segmentation use cases in the upcoming sections with various different users and the other workstations.  For now, we are just testing our wireless access.

    Once logged in, locate the SX Virtual Link application. This application allows us to physically connect wired and wireless NICs to our workstations for testing. If the application is not open by default, you might have to click the SX Virtual Link icon in the system tray.

    Locate the Linksys wireless adapter as shown below. This is the only wireless adapter available. First, click the Linksys NIC, then connect the wireless NIC by clicking the connect icon.

    Click the Network Icon in the desktop system tray and then click on > to view the available wireless networks.

    Search for your configured SSID that matches your POD's SSID. In our example here, we are using the SSID: PSEUDOCO-POD12

    Next, click Connect to connect to your SSID.

    Workstation-1 has been pre-configured to authenticate using TEAP and is logged in as Pat, who is a PROD user.

    Once connected, click the ping-test app on the desktop. From the app menu, enter 5 and then hit enter to run the ipconfig command. Your client should be given an IP address in the PROD network segment of 10.101.255.X/24

    You can also log into the ISE server as we did previously and examine the Live Logs to view the authentication and login information. You can also click and view the details of the authentication report as shown below. Feel free to explore the Wireless ISE Policy as well at Policy -> Policy Sets.

    After clicking to view the details of the authentication, notice the credentials sent from the workstation to ISE.  This is called EAP-Chaining, which uses a combination of user and machine authentication combined into one authentication.

    This capability was previously unique to EAP-FAST, but has now been incorporated into the TEAP industry standard protocol used here in the lab.

    🧭 Complete

    🎉 This concludes the wireless network configuration, provisioning, and testing section.

    🛑 Client did not connect, or connected but got no IP address? Work through 8. Appendix — Wireless Troubleshooting Checklist. It was written for the Infrastructure as Code track, but every check applies here too — the 9800, ISE, and FlexConnect behave the same way whether the controller was configured by hand or by the pipeline.

    EVPN Fabric and WLC — Infrastructure as Code

    🤖

    1. Prepare Your Workstation & Script Server

    Infrastructure as Code — Step 1 of 8

    This card gets you from a freshly connected VPN session to a script server that is ready to run the EVPN pipeline. You will SSH in, clone the repository onto that host, and let it bootstrap itself. Nothing here touches the fabric yet — every command in this card is safe to re-run.

    🛑 This is the Infrastructure as Code track. It provisions the EVPN fabric and the wireless service from one pipeline. If you already built the fabric by hand in 1. EVPN Fabric Provisioning, skip this entire track. Running both against the same pod will produce confusing results.

    The whole journey, at a glance

    Before you type anything, here is where you are going. Read it like a transit map: each line is a phase, each station is one command, and you ride them left to right, top to bottom. This card is the grey line at the top — the other three come later.

    Three things are worth noticing now, because they explain most of the questions students ask later:

    • Nothing touches the network until stage 08. Stages 01 – 07 only build intent inside Catalyst Center. If you make a mistake there, you have not broken anything on a switch.
    • There is exactly one unavoidable wait, and it is not in the software. A physical access point has to boot and find its controller, which cannot happen until stage 09 turns its switch port into a trunk. That is why wireless finishes last.
    • The orchestrator stops at stage 09. Stages 10 and 11 are deliberately yours to run — one blocks on that hardware wait, and the other is the proof you keep.

    One machine does the work

    Everything in this track runs on the script server — a Kali Linux host inside the pod. Your laptop only runs the VPN client and an SSH session. Nothing is installed on it.

    Machine Address What runs here
    Your laptop local Cisco Secure Client and an SSH terminal. That is all — you install nothing.
    Script server
    (Kali Linux)    		 198.18.134.12, user cisco You clone the repository here and run everything here, including stages 01 – 11. It is the only host with network reachability to Catalyst Center.

    The script server bootstraps itself from the repository you clone onto it: it fixes DNS, installs packages, builds a Python environment, and installs the Ansible collections.

    Before you begin

    • An active dCloud PseudoCo / Cisco One session, powered on
    • Cisco Secure Client (AnyConnect) connected to the dCloud session
    • An SSH client — nothing else is needed on your laptop
    • Your dCloud POD number, from the lab printout — ask a proctor if you cannot find it

    Catalyst Center in this pod is version 3.1.5, reachable at cat-center.corp.pseudoco.com.

    Step 1 — Confirm the VPN, then SSH in

    Everything downstream depends on this. Run it first, from your laptop:

    nc -z -G 5 198.18.134.12 22 && echo reachable

    If you do not see reachable, your VPN is not up. Reconnect Cisco Secure Client before continuing — no amount of retrying the later steps will help.

    Now connect. Every remaining command in this track runs inside this SSH session.

    ssh cisco@198.18.134.12

    Step 2 — Clone the repository onto the script server

    git clone https://github.com/imanassypov/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation.git
cd cisco-one-experience-lab-automation/ansible-automation/00_scriptserver_bootstrap

    You can clone anywhere you like — every path in the automation is worked out from where the playbooks live, so the home directory is just a convention.

    Step 3 — Stage the box

    There is a chicken-and-egg problem here: you cannot use Ansible to install Ansible. A small shell script does the minimum needed to break that cycle — base packages plus a Python environment at ~/venv containing ansible-core.

    ./stage-script-server.sh

    sudo will ask for the cisco account password. The script is safe to re-run, and it refuses to run on anything but Linux — so if you run it on your laptop out of habit, it stops and tells you the SSH command you actually wanted.

    It prompts once for your dCloud POD number — the integer from your lab printout — and writes it into lab.yml. That is the only lab-specific value you have to supply anywhere in this track. Pod 7 becomes SSID PSEUDOCO-POD07; the zero padding is not cosmetic, because the SSID has to match a pre-configured group policy on the shared controller.

    👀 Cannot find your POD number? Ask a proctor before guessing. Entering someone else's pod pushes your SSID onto their controller.

    It installs a pinned, reproducible set of packages so every student runs the same versions:

    PackageVersionWhy
    ansible-core2.17.14The automation engine
    paramiko3.5.1SSH transport used by other collections
    netaddr1.3.0IP address maths inside templates
    catalystcentersdk3.1.3.0.1Catalyst Center API client
    dnacentersdk2.10.6Required by the cisco.dnac collection

    The script also writes the vault password file for you, at .vault in the repository root. The lab stores its credentials in an encrypted file, Lab Topology/lab_access.yml, and .vault holds the passphrase that opens it. Confirm it works:

    cd ~/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation
ansible-vault view "Lab Topology/lab_access.yml" --vault-password-file .vault | head -4

    If that prints YAML, every playbook in the repository can authenticate, and you will never be asked for a device password again.

    🛑 Do not copy this credential pattern into production. Here the encrypted file and the passphrase that opens it both live in the repository, so the encryption protects nothing — it is a convenience for a disposable lab whose passwords are already public demo values. The mechanism itself is worth stealing; the key handling is not. In real work the passphrase comes from a secret store and never touches the repo.

    Step 4 — Bootstrap the script server

    Two playbooks, run in order. Together they take roughly ten minutes — most of that is downloading the pyats and genie libraries, which stage 11 needs to parse device output.

    Call them by their full path. ~/venv/bin is not on your PATH yet — putting it there is one of the jobs of the second playbook.

    cd ~/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation/ansible-automation/00_scriptserver_bootstrap

~/venv/bin/ansible-playbook playbooks/00_preflight.yml
~/venv/bin/ansible-playbook playbooks/01_bootstrap_script_server.yml

    🛑 If your shell says ansible-playbook: command not found and offers to install ansible-core with apt, answer no. That installs an unpinned Ansible outside the virtual environment, at a different version from every other student. Use the full path above instead.

    PlaybookWhat it does
    00_preflight.ymlRead-only. Confirms you are on the Linux script server, the checkout is complete, the vault passphrase actually decrypts, and sudo works — so a mistake is caught before anything is changed.
    01_bootstrap_script_server.ymlFixes DNS so cat-center.corp.pseudoco.com resolves, installs system packages, completes the ~/venv environment, puts the Ansible binaries on PATH, and installs the Cisco collections.
    02_sync_from_git.ymlNot needed today. Run it later if a proctor tells you a lab fix has been published — it fast-forwards your checkout. Re-run 01_bootstrap_script_server.yml afterwards, since a published fix may also add a pinned Python package or collection.

    Look for failed=0 in the PLAY RECAP at the end of each run.

    These playbooks target this same host over a local connection — there is no SSH hop and no remote password involved.

    Step 5 — Move to the pipeline directory

    Open a fresh shell so the new PATH takes effect, then change into the pipeline folder:

    cd ~/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation/ansible-automation/01_campus/evpn/ansible
ansible-playbook --version

    Ansible is now on PATH without activating anything. If that command is not found, source ~/venv/bin/activate still works.

    🛑 The working-directory rule. Every pipeline command from here on must be run from that exact directory. Ansible only reads ansible.cfg from the current directory, and that file is what supplies the inventory, the roles path, the vault password file, and the credential plugin. Run a playbook from anywhere else and you will get Lab access vault not found — that error almost always means you are in the wrong folder, not that anything is broken.

    Step 6 — Confirm your pod number

    Every student runs the same repository against a different pod, so the values that differ live in exactly one file. The staging script already wrote your pod number into it, so this is a check rather than an edit:

    grep lab_pod_id inventory/group_vars/all/lab.yml

    Edit it only if you entered the wrong pod, or later when you come back to add access point MACs:

    vi inventory/group_vars/all/lab.yml
    VariableValueNotes
    lab_pod_idYour pod numberSet for you in Step 3. It becomes your SSID — pod 7 produces PSEUDOCO-POD07. Stages that read the settings file fail if it is still REPLACE_ME, which is deliberate: it stops you pushing your SSID onto another student's controller.
    lab_ap_macs[]Leave it empty for now. You will fill this in much later, in the wireless card, once an access point has registered.

    Step 7 — Confirm the inventory loads

    ansible-inventory --graph

    You should see a single host:

    @all:
  |--@catalyst_center:
  |  |--catalyst_center_api

    That single host surprises people. It is correct — the playbooks do not SSH to the switches. Every action goes through the Catalyst Center REST API, including the verification stage, which uses Catalyst Center's Command Runner to collect show output. Catalyst Center is the only thing Ansible talks to.

    How credentials reach the playbooks

    You never type a device password, and no password appears in any playbook. Here is the chain:

    1. ansible.cfg points Ansible at your .vault passphrase file and at a custom vars plugin.
    2. That plugin, plugins/vars/lab_access.py, walks up the directory tree looking for Lab Topology/lab_access.yml, decrypts it, and injects the result as a variable called lab_access.
    3. The inventory then maps fields out of it, for example catc_username: "{{ lab_access['Catalyst Center'].username }}".

    This is a pattern worth stealing for real work: secrets encrypted at rest, committed alongside the code, decrypted only at run time by whoever holds the passphrase.

    🧭 Next up

    Your script server is ready and your pod number is set. Time to build the fabric: 2. Run the EVPN + Catalyst Center Pipeline.

    🤖

    2. Run the EVPN + Catalyst Center Pipeline

    Infrastructure as Code — Step 2 of 8

    This is the main event. Eleven numbered playbooks take your pod from an empty Catalyst Center to a fully provisioned EVPN fabric with wireless. Every command in this card runs on the script server, from the working directory you set up in the previous card.

    cd ~/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation/ansible-automation/01_campus/evpn/ansible

    The eleven stages

    Each stage is a standalone playbook. They are numbered because order matters — stage 05 cannot assign a device to a site that stage 01 has not created yet.

    The Impact column tells you what a stage can disturb if something goes wrong. Read it before you run a stage, not after.

    # Playbook What it does Impact
    0101_site_hierarchy.ymlCreates the areas, buildings, and floors that everything else hangs off. Sixteen site paths in total.Design change
    0202_network_settings.ymlSets DHCP, DNS, NTP, syslog, SNMP, and AAA servers on each site.Design change
    0303_credentials.ymlCreates the CLI, SNMP, HTTPS, and NETCONF credentials Catalyst Center uses to reach devices.Design change
    0404_device_discovery.ymlRuns two discovery jobs — one for the 9800 wireless controller, one for the three Site-105 switches.Design change
    0505_assign_to_site.ymlPlaces the discovered devices into their sites: the controller into the data centre, the switches into Site-105.Design change
    0606_template_sync.ymlUploads the 24 Jinja2 templates and the composite manifest into a Catalyst Center CLI project named Site-105. This is the fabric design, as code.Design change
    0707_network_profile.ymlBuilds four switching profiles, the wireless design including your PSEUDOCO-PODnn SSID, the FlexConnect settings, and the HQ-Wireless profile.Design change
    0808_provision_devices.ymlProvisions the switches, then the wireless controller, then any access points already known to Catalyst Center.Can reboot APs
    0909_deploy_composite.ymlRenders the templates per device and pushes the resulting EVPN configuration to the three switches.Disruptive
    1010_await_access_points.ymlWaits for the access point to reach Catalyst Center, records its MAC in lab.yml, then names, site-assigns and provisions it. Not part of 00_site_deploy.yml — it blocks on hardware booting.Reboots your AP
    1111_verify_intent.ymlCollects show output and compares it against the design. Changes nothing.Read-only

    🛑 Stage 09 pushes live configuration to the switches. That is the point of it — but be aware it is the stage that changes the network. Stage 08 can reboot access points on a controller shared with other pods. Neither is dangerous in this lab; both deserve your attention in a real one.

    Option A — Run the whole thing

    One playbook imports stages 01 through 09 in order:

    ansible-playbook playbooks/00_site_deploy.yml

    Note that it deliberately stops at 09. The two stages after it are separate, conscious acts: stage 10 blocks waiting for a physical access point to boot, which an orchestrator has no business stalling on, and stage 11 proves the result after the build. You run both yourself.

    Option B — Run stage by stage (recommended the first time)

    Going one at a time lets you watch each change land in the Catalyst Center GUI, which is by far the best way to understand what the automation is doing:

    ansible-playbook playbooks/01_site_hierarchy.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/02_network_settings.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/03_credentials.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/04_device_discovery.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/05_assign_to_site.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/06_template_sync.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/07_network_profile.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/08_provision_devices.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/09_deploy_composite.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/10_await_access_points.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/11_verify_intent.yml

    The last two behave differently from the rest, so do not be surprised by them.

    Stage 10 sits there and waits. It polls Catalyst Center for up to ten minutes while the access point boots on the trunk stage 09 just built, then records its MAC and provisions it. A long quiet pause is the playbook working, not hanging. You can start it the moment stage 09 finishes — there is no advantage to watching the controller first. Card 6. Verify Wireless & Provision the Access Point covers what it does in full.

    Stage 11 is the only read-only stage. It changes nothing and produces the evidence report, so run it as often as you like.

    After each stage, open Catalyst Center and look at what changed.

    After stageWhere to look in Catalyst Center
    01Design > Network Hierarchy — the site tree appears
    04Tools > Discovery — two completed discovery jobs
    05Provision > Inventory — devices now show a site
    06Design > CLI Templates — a CLI project called Site-105 with 25 templates
    07Design > Network Profiles and Workflows > Wireless — your SSID
    08Provision > Inventory — provisioning status turns green
    09Activities > Tasks — one deployment task per switch
    10Provision > Inventory — the access point appears, gains a name and a site, then turns green
    11Nothing changes in the GUI — read evidence/stage11-verification.html instead

    What stage 09 actually pushes

    This is the stage that creates the EVPN fabric — the same configuration the traditional path asks you to paste by hand. It does not come from the playbook. It comes from a set of Jinja2 templates that stage 06 uploaded into Catalyst Center, which Catalyst Center then renders separately for each device.

    The templates are split into three kinds of file:

    • DEFN files hold the design as data — which switches exist, which VRFs, which VLANs, which loopbacks. They contain no configuration commands at all.
    • FUNC files hold reusable macros.
    • FABRIC files are the renderers. They read the DEFN data, work out which device they are running on, and emit the IOS-XE commands for it.

    The next card but one, 4. Understanding the EVPN Template Model, takes this apart properly. For now it is enough to know that the design lives in data files, and the fabric is generated from them.

    Useful overrides

    This project has no Ansible tags. Everything is controlled with -e extra variables instead:

    OverrideEffect
    -e catc_debug=truePrints the raw API requests and responses. The first thing to try when a stage fails.
    -e state=deletedOn stages 01 and 03, removes what was created instead of creating it.
    -e wireless_provision_enabled=falseOn stage 08, skips the wireless controller. Stage 10 never touches the controller, so you do not need this flag for access-point work — see the wireless card.
    -e ap_provision_enabled=falseOn stage 08, skips access points.
    -e ap_expected_count=2On stage 10, waits for two access points rather than stopping at the first.
    -e ap_provision_after_discovery=falseOn stage 10, records the MAC in lab.yml but stops short of provisioning.
    -e force_reprovision=trueOn stage 08, re-provisions devices Catalyst Center already considers done.
    -e template_source=gitOn stage 06, pulls templates from GitHub instead of the local checkout.

    If a stage fails

    SymptomLikely causeFix
    Lab access vault not foundYou are in the wrong directorycd back to 01_campus/evpn/ansible
    A stage fails complaining about REPLACE_MElab_pod_id is still unsetEdit inventory/group_vars/all/lab.yml
    Connection or DNS errors reaching Catalyst CenterVPN dropped, or DNS was not fixedReconnect the VPN, then re-run 01_bootstrap_script_server.yml
    A stage reports changed=0 and you expected workIt is idempotent and already doneThis is normal. Re-running a stage is always safe.
    No module named 'rich', or any other missing Python moduleYour ~/venv predates a change to the pinned package listRe-run 01_bootstrap_script_server.yml — it tops up the environment in place

    Every stage is idempotent: running it twice produces the same result as running it once. When in doubt, run it again.

    🧭 Next up

    The fabric is built. Now prove it: 3. Verify Intent & Evidence Report.

    🔍

    3. Verify Intent & Evidence Report

    Infrastructure as Code — Step 3 of 8

    Deploying configuration and knowing it is correct are two different things. Stage 11 closes that gap: it reads the design out of Catalyst Center, collects live output from the switches and controller, and produces a pass/fail report you could hand to an auditor.

    ansible-playbook playbooks/11_verify_intent.yml

    This stage is read-only. It changes nothing, and you can run it as often as you like.

    How it decides what "correct" means

    The interesting design decision here is where the expected values come from. They are not hard-coded in the playbook, and they are not read from your local checkout. They are read back out of Catalyst Center — from the very DEFN templates that stage 06 uploaded.

    That has a useful consequence: if you edit a template locally and forget to sync it, verification compares against what is actually deployed, not what you meant to deploy. A forgotten sync cannot produce a false pass.

    The stage works in phases:

    1. Read the DEFN templates from Catalyst Center — this is the intended state.
    2. Read the fabric and network settings from Catalyst Center.
    3. Resolve which devices to check.
    4. Run show commands through Catalyst Center's Command Runner.
    5. Parse the output with Genie into structured data.
    6. Compare intended against observed, and write the report.

    👀 Why Genie and not text matching? Screen-scraping show output with regular expressions breaks the moment IOS-XE changes a column. Genie parses the output into structured data, so the comparison is against values, not text. Note also that show running-config is deliberately never collected — the check is on operational state, which is what actually matters.

    Reading the report

    Two files are written on the script server, into a folder called evidence/:

    FileUse it for
    evidence/stage11-verification.mdReading in the terminal, and diffing between runs
    evidence/stage11-verification.htmlA self-contained formal report — no external fonts or scripts, opens offline, prints cleanly

    Read the Markdown version in the terminal, and open the HTML one in a browser. rich is installed in your virtual environment and renders the headings and tables in colour:

    python -m rich.markdown evidence/stage11-verification.md | less -R

    Press q to quit the pager. For the raw file with no rendering, less evidence/stage11-verification.md works too — and the HTML report is complete on its own, so nothing is lost if you skip the terminal view.

    👀 If that command says No module named 'rich', your virtual environment was built before rich was added to the pinned package list. Re-running the bootstrap tops it up, along with anything else that has moved:

    cd ~/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation/ansible-automation/00_scriptserver_bootstrap
~/venv/bin/ansible-playbook playbooks/01_bootstrap_script_server.yml

    Then cd back to 01_campus/evpn/ansible and try the preview again. In a hurry, ~/venv/bin/pip install rich==15.0.0 fixes just this one thing.

    What a good run looks like

    A healthy Site-105 produces something close to this:

    ResultCountMeaning
    Pass45Observed state matches the design
    Fail0A real mismatch — investigate
    Not verified0Catalyst Center refused the command, so nothing was concluded
    Not applicable7The check does not apply to that device's role
    Total checks52, across 4 devices

    Not applicable is expected and healthy. The Border-Spine does not carry tenant VLANs or client access ports, so tenant SVI and client-port checks simply do not apply to it. The report says so explicitly rather than quietly passing them.

    Not verified is not the same as pass. Command Runner will only accept five commands per request, so the role batches them; if Catalyst Center rejects a batch, those checks are reported as not verified rather than being assumed good. A report full of "not verified" means something is wrong with Command Runner, not with your fabric.

    The report structure

    1. Result summary — the counts above
    2. Devices — what was checked, and its role
    3. Intent sources — every DEFN template used, with its Catalyst Center template ID and last-modified timestamp, so you can prove which version of the design was verified
    4. Per-device checks — intended value beside observed value, for every check

    The intent-source list is the part worth lingering on. It is the link between a Git commit and a live switch: the report names the exact template version that produced the configuration it just validated.

    👀 This card is the practical tour. 7. Intent Verification as Code takes the same stage apart properly — how a check is written, why an unevaluable check must never read as a pass, and what the report proves to an auditor. Read it once the wireless side is in place, so the report you study has everything in it.

    When something fails

    SymptomWhat to do
    A handful of checks fail on one deviceRe-run stage 09 — a deployment task may have timed out
    Everything is NOT VERIFIEDCommand Runner is unhappy. Confirm the devices are reachable and managed in Inventory.
    You want to see the raw CLI outputRe-run with -e catc_debug=true
    You want the play to stop on the first mismatchRun with -e verify_fail_on_mismatch=true
    🧭 Next up

    You have a working, verified fabric. Now find out how it was described: 4. Understanding the EVPN Template Model.

    🧩

    4. Understanding the EVPN Template Model

    Infrastructure as Code — Step 4 of 8

    You have deployed a fabric you did not type. This card explains what you actually deployed.

    If you only read one card in the automated track, make it this one. The template model is the genuinely transferable idea here — it is how you would run fifty campus sites, not three switches.

    The core idea: separate the data from the rendering

    A naïve template contains configuration with a few variables sprinkled through it. That works for one device and falls apart at scale, because the design and the syntax are tangled together.

    This project splits them into three kinds of file, each with one job:

    Kind Count Job
    DEFN11 The design, expressed as data. Which switches exist and what role each plays. Which tenants, VLANs, subnets, loopbacks, and uplinks. These files contain no configuration commands at all — only Jinja2 {% set %} statements defining lists and dictionaries.
    FUNC2 Reusable macros. Small helpers used by several renderers, so the same logic is not written out repeatedly.
    FABRIC11 The renderers. Each one pulls in the DEFN data it needs, works out which device it is currently running on, and emits the IOS-XE commands for that device.

    A twenty-fifth file, BGP-EVPN-BUILD.yml, is the manifest: an ordered list of which FABRIC templates to run and in what sequence.

    The payoff: to change the network, you change data. Adding a tenant means adding a row to a list. You never touch the rendering logic, which means you cannot break the BGP syntax while adding a VLAN.

    The join key — how a template knows which switch it is on

    Every DEFN dictionary is keyed by the device's Catalyst Center inventory hostname, which is the IOS hostname plus the configured domain name:

    Site_105-Leaf1.corp.pseudoco.com
Site_105-Leaf2.corp.pseudoco.com
Site_105-Border-Spine.corp.pseudoco.com

    Every FABRIC template starts by resolving that name from a special object Catalyst Center injects at render time:

    {% set DEVICE_HOSTNAME = __device.hostname | default('', true) %}

    __device is Catalyst Center's per-target object. One template, deployed to three switches, produces three different configurations — because DEVICE_HOSTNAME differs each time and every DEFN lookup keys off it.

    🛑 If a device's hostname or domain name does not match the DEFN key exactly, that device silently receives nothing. This is the single most common mistake when adapting these templates to a new site.

    DEFN-ROLES.j2 — the dispatch table

    This is the most important DEFN file, because every renderer consults it to decide what to emit:

    {% if DEVICE_HOSTNAME in DEFN_NODE_ROLES['CLIENT'] %}
  ... emit leaf-only configuration ...
{% endif %}

    In Site-105 there are five role buckets — SPINE, RR, CLIENT, BORDER, and MCLUSTER. The Border-Spine appears in three of them at once (spine, route reflector, and border) and is deliberately absent from CLIENT. That single omission is what stops the leaf-only configuration — tenant VLANs, anycast gateways, client ports — from being pushed to it.

    This is also why the stage 11 report shows checks as Not applicable for that device: it was never supposed to have them.

    The eleven DEFN files

    File What it declares In this lab
    DEFN-ROLES.j2Which device plays which fabric role. Consulted by every renderer.Active
    DEFN-VRF.j2The tenants — Main, PROD, IOT with ids 10, 101, 102 — plus each one's L3 VLAN and L3 VNI, and which devices carry which tenants.Active
    DEFN-OVERLAY.j2The layer-2 VLANs inside each tenant: SVI address, anycast gateway MAC, DHCP helper, BUM multicast group. Also where the BGP AS number (65535) and the OSPF underlay are defined.Active
    DEFN-LOOPBACKS.j2Loopback0 per device — the VXLAN tunnel source, the BGP router-ID, and the left half of every route distinguisher.Active
    DEFN-VNIOFFSETS.j2The arithmetic that turns a VLAN id into a VXLAN network identifier.Active
    DEFN-L3OUT.j2The VRF-Lite handoff from the Border-Spine toward the SD-WAN router on Gi1/0/48, plus a global BGP session advertising the fabric loopbacks.Active
    DEFN-CLIENT-PORTS.j2Front-panel ports on the leaves. Gi1/0/1 and Gi1/0/3 are 802.1X client ports; Gi1/0/2 is the access-point trunk.Active
    DEFN-NAC.j2802.1X scaffolding — device tracking, service templates, policy maps, critical-auth VLANs. Note that the RADIUS servers are not here; they come from Catalyst Center network settings, so no shared secret ever sits in a template.Active
    DEFN-MCAST.j2PIM, anycast rendezvous points, MSDP.Disabled — Site-105 replicates broadcast traffic with a static multicast group instead, so no RP infrastructure is needed
    DEFN-BORDER-DMZ-TUNNELS.j2GRE tunnels from the border into a DMZ fabric.Disabled — empty in this lab
    DEFN-TELEMETRY-SPLUNK.j2Model-driven telemetry subscriptions for NVE, EVPN, and BGP state.Disabled — the subscriptions are defined but the receiver address is blank

    The disabled files are worth noticing rather than skipping. They are switched off by data — an empty list, a false flag, a blank address — not by deleting code. Turning multicast on at another site is a data change, not a rewrite.

    A DEFN file, in full

    DEFN-VRF.j2 is the whole tenant design for the site. This is all of it:

    {# Site-105 tenants. RD/RT come from Loopback0:id and ASN:id (65535). #}
{% set DEFN_VRF = [
  {'id':'10',  'name':'Main', 'l3_vlan':'1010', 'l3_vni':'110010', 'mdt_default':'', 'mdt_data':''},
  {'id':'101', 'name':'PROD', 'l3_vlan':'1101', 'l3_vni':'110101', 'mdt_default':'', 'mdt_data':''},
  {'id':'102', 'name':'IOT',  'l3_vlan':'1102', 'l3_vni':'110102', 'mdt_default':'', 'mdt_data':''}
  ]
%}

{# All three VRFs on all three fabric nodes (leaves + border-spine). #}
{% set DEFN_VRF_TO_NODE = {
  'Site_105-Leaf1.corp.pseudoco.com':        ['10','101','102'],
  'Site_105-Leaf2.corp.pseudoco.com':        ['10','101','102'],
  'Site_105-Border-Spine.corp.pseudoco.com': ['10','101','102']
} %}

    No vrf definition. No route-target. Just the facts. Note that every value is a string, including the numeric ones — that is the convention throughout these files.

    And the renderer that consumes it

    FABRIC-VRF.j2 turns that data into configuration:

    {% for vrf in vrf_objs %}
vrf definition {{ vrf.name }}
description VRF {{ vrf.name }} definition
rd {{ DEFN_LOOP_UNDERLAY[DEVICE_HOSTNAME] }}:{{ vrf.id }}
!
address-family ipv4
route-target export {{ FABRIC_BGP_ASN }}:{{ vrf.id }}
route-target import {{ FABRIC_BGP_ASN }}:{{ vrf.id }}
route-target export {{ FABRIC_BGP_ASN }}:{{ vrf.id }} stitching
route-target import {{ FABRIC_BGP_ASN }}:{{ vrf.id }} stitching
exit-address-family
!
{% endfor %}

    Compare that to the block you would paste by hand in the traditional path — it is the same configuration. On Leaf1, the Main tenant renders as rd 172.30.255.1:10 and route-target 65535:10.

    Notice the two different sources: the route distinguisher uses the device's own loopback, so it differs per switch, while the route target uses the fabric AS number, so it is identical everywhere. That distinction is fundamental to EVPN, and here it is visible in one line of template.

    Numbering — what is calculated and what is not

    Layer-2 VNIs are derived, not typed:

    $$\text{L2VNI} = 100000 + \text{VLAN}$$

    So VLAN 10 becomes VNI 100010 and VLAN 101 becomes 100101. The offset lives in DEFN-VNIOFFSETS.j2 and the addition happens in FABRIC-OVERLAY.j2. Add a VLAN and its VNI appears automatically.

    Layer-3 VNIs are the opposite — they are written out explicitly on each tenant row, because the layer-3 core is not simply "one VLAN per VRF". The lab follows two conventions:

    ValueConventionExample (tenant id 101)
    L2VNIcalculated — 100000 + VLANVLAN 101 → 100101
    l3_vlan1000 + tenant id1101
    l3_vni110000 + tenant id110101

    👀 You will find L3VNIOFFSET = 50 in DEFN-VNIOFFSETS.j2. Nothing uses it — it is a leftover from an earlier numbering scheme. Don't go looking for where it is consumed.

    The manifest — order is the design

    BGP-EVPN-BUILD.yml lists the renderers in deployment order:

    templates:
  - name: "FABRIC-VRF.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-LOOPBACKS.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-BORDER-DMZ-TUNNELS.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-L3OUT.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-NVE.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-MCAST.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-EVPN.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-OVERLAY.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-NAC.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-CLIENT-PORTS.j2"
  - name: "FABRIC-TELEMETRY-SPLUNK.j2"

    That order is not cosmetic — it is the order IOS-XE needs. VRFs must exist before interfaces can be placed in them. router bgp must exist before the overlay can add an address family underneath it. Reorder this list and the deployment fails.

    Catalyst Center turns this into a composite template called BGP-EVPN-BUILD.j2 — a template whose body is empty and whose content is an ordered list of members.

    🛑 DEFN and FUNC files are never listed in the manifest. They are not deployed on their own — they are pulled in by the FABRIC templates with {% include %}. Adding one here would try to deploy a file containing no commands.

    One CLI project per site

    This is the rule to remember from this card.

    FABRIC templates pull in their data with an include that names the Catalyst Center project:

    {% include "{{ TEMPLATE_PROJECT_NAME }}/DEFN-VRF.j2" %}

    That placeholder is not resolved at render time. Stage 06 substitutes it as a plain text replacement before uploading, so what actually sits in Catalyst Center reads:

    {% include "Site-105/DEFN-VRF.j2" %}

    Includes therefore resolve as <project>/<filename> inside Catalyst Center's Template Hub. Which leads directly to the rule:

    🛑 Each campus EVPN site gets its own CLI project, containing its own complete set of DEFN, FUNC, FABRIC, and composite templates. Never share one project between two sites.

    Two sites cannot both have a file called DEFN-VRF.j2 in the same project. The second upload overwrites the first, and from that moment one site is silently configured with the other site's tenant design. Nothing errors. The verification report is the only thing that would catch it.

    Because the placeholder is substituted per project, the FABRIC and FUNC files contain no site-specific strings whatsoever. They are byte-for-byte reusable. Everything that makes Site-105 different from any other site lives in the eleven DEFN files.

    What a second site would look like

    1. Copy the template folder to a new one, for example Site 106 BGP EVPN Templates.
    2. Edit only the eleven DEFN files: new hostnames, new loopbacks, new tenants, new VLANs, new uplinks. Leave every FABRIC and FUNC file untouched.
    3. Add one row to the inventory telling stage 06 about the new folder and its project name: 
      git_repo_subfolders:
  - path: "Catalyst Center Templates/Site BGP EVPN Templates"
    project_name: "Site-105"
  - path: "Catalyst Center Templates/Site 106 BGP EVPN Templates"
    project_name: "Site-106"
    4. Add a matching entry to the settings file so stage 09 knows where to deploy it.
    5. Re-run stages 06 and 09. Both sites deploy in one pass.

    That is the scaling story. The renderers are written once; each new site is a folder of data and one inventory row.

    Quirks you will notice in the templates

    Catalyst Center's Jinja2 engine is not stock Jinja2. These workarounds are deliberate — recognise them, but don't copy them into new code without understanding why they are there.

    What you will seeWhy
    A dummy {% set vrf = {...} %} with empty values just before a loop Catalyst Center statically scans templates for variables it thinks the operator must supply. A bare {{ l3_vni }} inside a loop looks like a required input to that scanner. The dummy assignment satisfies it, and the loop overwrites the value at run time. Without it, Catalyst Center would prompt you for values at deploy time.
    Exclusions written as {% if X in A or X in B %}{# skip #}{% else %}…{% endif %} The engine does not support not in. The templates say so in their own comments.
    ! @start-ignore-compliance and ! @end-ignore-compliance markers They tell Catalyst Center's compliance engine not to flag the enclosed block as drift.
    A {## CATC: productFamily=… ##} comment on line 1 of every file Inert. Nothing reads it. The device types actually come from the Ansible inventory, which applies the same four Catalyst 9000 series to every template. Keep the line — it documents intent — but editing it changes nothing.
    FABRIC-VRF.j2 includes DEFN-OVERLAY.j2 even though it renders no VLANs The BGP AS number is defined in DEFN-OVERLAY.j2, and the VRF renderer needs it for route targets.
    🧭 Next up

    You understand the model. Now use it — add a tenant and a VLAN, and push them through the pipeline: 5. Extending the Fabric.

    🌱

    5. Extending the Fabric: Add a Tenant or VLAN via Git

    Infrastructure as Code — Step 5 of 8

    Two worked examples, then the workflow that carries them from an edit on your screen to configuration on a switch. This is where Infrastructure as Code stops being an idea and becomes a habit.

    👀 Optional but recommended. The lab works without this card. Do it anyway — making a change and watching it flow through is the most useful twenty minutes in the automated track.

    Example 1 — Add a VLAN to an existing tenant

    Goal: a voice VLAN, 111, inside the existing PROD tenant.

    One file changes: DEFN-OVERLAY.j2. Find the PROD group and add the VLAN to both the id list and the VLAN dictionary. Everything highlighted in green is new — the rest is already there:

    {
  'vrf': 'PROD',
  'vlan_ids': ['101','111'],
  'vlans': {
    '101': {'name':'PROD', 'ipaddr':'10.101.255.1 255.255.255.0', 'mac':'0001.0001.0101',
            'dhcp_helper':'198.18.5.102', 'bum_addr':'232.1.1.1', 'network':'10.101.255.0 255.255.255.0'},
    '111': {'name':'PROD-Voice', 'ipaddr':'10.111.255.1 255.255.255.0', 'mac':'0001.0001.0111',
            'dhcp_helper':'198.18.5.102', 'bum_addr':'232.1.1.1', 'network':'10.111.255.0 255.255.255.0'}
  }
}

    Note the comma that has to be added to the end of the '101' entry. It is the single easiest thing to miss here, and Catalyst Center reports it as a Jinja2 syntax error at sync time rather than as a missing VLAN.

    That is the entire change. Nothing else is required for the VLAN to forward.

    FieldWhat it becomes on the switch
    nameThe VLAN name and the SVI description
    ipaddrThe SVI address. Space-separated, not CIDR — it is pasted straight into ip address
    macThe distributed anycast gateway MAC — identical on every leaf, so a client keeps its default gateway when it roams
    dhcp_helperThe DHCP relay target, reached through the global routing table
    bum_addrThe multicast group used to flood broadcast and unknown-unicast traffic across the underlay
    networkReserved — not currently consumed by any renderer

    The VXLAN identifier is calculated for you: 100000 + 111 = 100111. You never type it, and it cannot drift.

    Two optional follow-ups, depending on what the VLAN is for:

    • If wireless clients need it, add 111 to the allowed VLAN list on the access-point trunk in DEFN-CLIENT-PORTS.j2.
    • If it needs security group tagging, add 111 to the CTS VLAN list in DEFN-NAC.j2.

    Example 2 — Add a new tenant

    Goal: a fourth tenant, DEV, with id 103.

    Step 1 — in DEFN-VRF.j2, add a row to the tenant list. Green is new:

    {% set DEFN_VRF = [
  {'id':'10',  'name':'Main', 'l3_vlan':'1010', 'l3_vni':'110010', 'mdt_default':'', 'mdt_data':''},
  {'id':'101', 'name':'PROD', 'l3_vlan':'1101', 'l3_vni':'110101', 'mdt_default':'', 'mdt_data':''},
  {'id':'102', 'name':'IOT',  'l3_vlan':'1102', 'l3_vni':'110102', 'mdt_default':'', 'mdt_data':''},
  {'id':'103', 'name':'DEV',  'l3_vlan':'1103', 'l3_vni':'110103', 'mdt_default':'', 'mdt_data':''}
  ]
%}

    Following the conventions from the previous card: l3_vlan is 1000 + 103, and l3_vni is 110000 + 103. Both must be unique across the fabric.

    Step 2 — in the same file, add the tenant to each device that should carry it:

    {% set DEFN_VRF_TO_NODE = {
  'Site_105-Leaf1.corp.pseudoco.com':        ['10','101','102','103'],
  'Site_105-Leaf2.corp.pseudoco.com':        ['10','101','102','103'],
  'Site_105-Border-Spine.corp.pseudoco.com': ['10','101','102','103']
} %}

    Step 3 — in DEFN-OVERLAY.j2, add a matching group with at least one VLAN. It goes at the end of the DEFN_OVERLAY list, after the existing IOT group:

      {
    'vrf': 'IOT',
    'vlan_ids': ['102'],
    'vlans': {
      '102': {'name':'IOT', 'ipaddr':'10.102.255.1 255.255.255.0', 'mac':'0001.0001.0102',
              'dhcp_helper':'198.18.5.102', 'bum_addr':'232.1.1.1', 'network':'10.102.255.0 255.255.255.0'}
    }
  },
  {
    'vrf': 'DEV',
    'vlan_ids': ['103'],
    'vlans': {
      '103': {'name':'DEV', 'ipaddr':'10.103.255.1 255.255.255.0', 'mac':'0001.0001.0103',
              'dhcp_helper':'198.18.5.102', 'bum_addr':'232.1.1.1', 'network':'10.103.255.0 255.255.255.0'}
    }
  }
] %}

    🛑 Step 3 is not optional. Skip it and you get a tenant that exists but does nothing: no SVI, no VLAN, and — less obviously — no address-family ipv4 vrf DEV under router bgp, because the overlay renderer only emits that when it finds a matching overlay group. The fabric will look fine and the tenant will not route.

    Steps 4 and 5, optional — add a VRF-Lite handoff in DEFN-L3OUT.j2 if the tenant needs to reach the SD-WAN, and extend DEFN-NAC.j2 or DEFN-CLIENT-PORTS.j2 if clients will be placed in it.

    Four files at most. No configuration syntax written by hand. No possibility of a typo in a route target.

    Commit the change

    In this lab the script server holds its own checkout, and stage 06 reads from the local folder by default. So edit in place, then commit:

    cd ~/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation
git checkout -b add-dev-tenant
git diff
git add "ansible-automation/01_campus/evpn/Catalyst Center Templates/Site BGP EVPN Templates/"
git commit -m "Site-105: add DEV tenant (id 103, L3VNI 110103) and PROD voice VLAN 111"

    👀 Your commit message is not just for humans. Stage 06 writes the git commit — its message, author, and date — into Catalyst Center as the template version comment, truncated at 1024 characters. Open the version history of any template in the Template Hub and you are looking at the git log. A lazy commit message becomes a lazy audit trail.

    Push it through the pipeline

    cd ansible-automation/01_campus/evpn/ansible

ansible-playbook playbooks/06_template_sync.yml     # upload the new design
ansible-playbook playbooks/09_deploy_composite.yml  # render it and push to the switches
ansible-playbook playbooks/11_verify_intent.yml     # prove it landed
    StageWhat happensImpact
    06 Uploads all 25 files. There is no create-versus-update logic — the same idempotent call handles both, and Catalyst Center commits a new version of anything that changed. Unchanged files are left alone. Design change
    09 Looks up the latest version of the composite, renders it for each of the three switches, and pushes the result. Disruptive
    10 Re-reads the design from Catalyst Center and re-checks the devices. Your new VLAN and tenant appear as new checks. Read-only

    Editing locally versus pulling from Git

    Stage 06 can take its templates from either place. The behaviour afterwards is identical.

    ModeHow to select itWhen to use it
    local The lab default Reads the folder in the checkout on the script server. Fast edit-and-test loop, which is what you want while learning.
    git -e template_source=git Reads the templates from GitHub over the API. This is the production pattern: whatever is on the branch is what gets deployed, so nobody can push an untracked local edit. Add -e git_token=… for a private repository or to avoid anonymous rate limits.

    Why this is safer than editing the switches

    • The change is reviewable. A git diff of a DEFN file shows the design change in a form a colleague can reason about — a new tenant row, not four hundred lines of CLI across three devices.
    • The change is consistent. One edit renders correctly for every device and for every role. There is no leaf that got missed.
    • The change is attributable. Catalyst Center records which commit produced the running configuration.
    • The change is provable. Stage 11 compares the devices against the design read back out of Catalyst Center — never against your local files — so an edit you forgot to sync cannot pass verification.
    • The change is reversible. Revert the commit, re-run stages 06 and 09.

    Troubleshooting

    SymptomLikely causeFix
    Stage 06 succeeds but nothing changed on the switchesYou skipped stage 09Stage 06 only uploads. Stage 09 deploys.
    New tenant appears in show vrf but does not routeNo overlay group for itAdd the group to DEFN-OVERLAY.j2 (step 3 above)
    Catalyst Center prompts for variable values at deploy timeA new variable was introduced without a dummy {% set %}See the quirks table in 4. Understanding the EVPN Template Model
    Stage 09 deploys but the old configuration is still thereAn older template version was selectedRe-run stage 06, then stage 09
    Stage 11 fails on the new VLAN onlyStage 09 partially timed outRe-run stage 09, then stage 11
    🧭 Complete

    🎉 You have built an EVPN fabric from source, proved it matches its design, and changed it through a commit. That is the whole Infrastructure as Code loop — describe, deploy, verify, evolve.

    Next, confirm the wireless service the same pipeline built for you: 6. Verify Wireless & Provision the Access Point.

    🤖

    6. Verify Wireless & Provision the Access Point

    Infrastructure as Code — Step 6 of 8

    Wireless, when the pipeline built it for you

    🛑 This card belongs to the Infrastructure as Code track. If you configured the wireless controller by hand in 4. Configure & Test WLC, you are finished with that section — skip to Macro / Micro Segmentation.

    Here is the thing that catches people out: by the time you reach this card, the wireless configuration already exists. Stage 07 built it and stage 08 provisioned it. There is no SSID to create and no policy tag to select.

    So this card is different from the manual one. Your job is to verify what the automation produced, then complete the one part that could not be done earlier — provisioning the access point.

    What the pipeline already did

    StageWhat it configured
    07 Created the SSID PSEUDOCO-PODnn from your lab_pod_id, using WPA2/WPA3 Enterprise with 802.1X pointing at ISE. Defined the Main, PROD, and IOT interfaces, configured FlexConnect, set the native VLAN, enabled AAA VLAN override, and attached it all to a wireless profile called HQ-Wireless bound to Site-105.
    08 Provisioned the 9800 controller at 198.18.5.103 into the data-centre site, pushing that wireless design onto it.
    09 Configured Gi1/0/2 on each leaf as the access-point trunk — native VLAN 10, allowed VLANs 10, 101 and 102, with a security group tag and portfast. This is the step that lets an access point come online at all.

    👀 Why the access point could not be provisioned earlier. Until stage 09 ran, the switch port the access point is plugged into was not a trunk, so the access point could not reach the controller and could not register. That is why stages 04 and 08 never discovered one. The ordering is deliberate, not a bug.

    The sequence, end to end

    Wireless is the one part of this lab that cannot be done in a single pass, because a physical device has to boot and find its controller in the middle of it. The order is fixed:

    #What happensWhy it cannot happen sooner
    1Stage 09 deploys the composite, building the EVPN fabricThis is what turns Gi1/0/2 into the access-point trunk
    2The access point boots and registers to the 9800It needs the trunk from step 1. Takes a few minutes
    3Catalyst Center learns the access point from the controllerA further sync delay — the controller knows first
    4Stage 10 waits for it, records its MAC, assigns it to Site-105 and provisions itNothing to wait for until steps 2 and 3 finish
    5Stage 11 verifies wireless alongside the fabricNeeds the access point actually serving the SSID

    The waiting is all in steps 2 and 3, and stage 10 does that waiting for you.

    Step 1 — Verify the SSID

    Log into the controller using the Chrome bookmark (C9800-WLC) or browse to https://wlc.corp.pseudoco.com with admin/C1sco12345.

    Go to Configuration > Tags & Profiles > WLANs. You should find your SSID already there, named for your pod — pod 7 gives PSEUDOCO-POD07. You did not type it; stage 07 generated it from lab_pod_id.

    Check the following:

    • The SSID name matches your pod number, zero-padded to two digits
    • Its status is Enabled
    • Security is WPA2/WPA3 Enterprise with 802.1X
    • The authentication list points at ISE

    You can confirm the same thing from the command line on the controller:

    show wlan summary

    Step 2 — Watch the access point appear, in two places

    With Gi1/0/2 now trunked, the access point can find the controller. It then has to show up twice, a few minutes apart — and it is the second one that stage 10 actually waits for. You do not have to sit and watch this happen; stage 10 polls for you. It is worth understanding, though, because it explains where the time goes.

    First, on the controller. Go to Configuration > Wireless > Access Points and wait for it to appear as Registered. This usually takes a few minutes after stage 09 completes.

    C9800#show ap summary
Number of APs: 1

CC = Country Code
RD = Regulatory Domain

AP Name                          Slots AP Model             Ethernet MAC   Radio MAC      CC   RD   IP Address                                State        Location
---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
SITE-105-AP-1                    3     AIR-AP4800-B-K9      084f.a950.f028 10b3.d66c.c860 US   -B   10.10.255.100                             Registered   Leaf1

    Note the access point's Ethernet MAC address — the wired MAC, not the radio MAC. Above, that is 084f.a950.f028 and not 10b3.d66c.c860. You will not have to type it anywhere, but seeing it here makes the next step easier to follow.

    Then, in Catalyst Center. The controller syncs its access point list up to Catalyst Center, which lags the controller by a few more minutes. Check Provision > Inventory, or filter for Unified APs, until the access point is listed there too.

    👀 This is the wait people used to get caught by. Provisioning looks the access point up in Catalyst Center by its Ethernet MAC — not on the controller. Doing it by hand while the access point is Registered on the 9800 but not yet in Catalyst Center inventory fails, even though the controller plainly shows it. Stage 10 waits for the Catalyst Center side specifically, which is why you can start it early.

    Step 3 — Let the pipeline pick up and provision the access point

    You do not copy the MAC address by hand, and you do not re-run stage 08. One command does the rest:

    cd ~/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation/ansible-automation/01_campus/evpn/ansible
ansible-playbook playbooks/10_await_access_points.yml

    It polls Catalyst Center until an access point appears — up to ten minutes by default — writes the Ethernet MAC into lab.yml in the right format, and then names the access point, assigns it to the Site-105 floor, and provisions it. Your pod number and every comment in lab.yml are left untouched. It is safe to re-run, and safe to start before the access point has registered; it simply waits.

    You can start it as soon as stage 09 finishes rather than watching the controller.

    If you have two access points, tell it so it does not stop at the first:

    ansible-playbook playbooks/10_await_access_points.yml -e ap_expected_count=2

    To stop after writing the MAC, without provisioning:

    ansible-playbook playbooks/10_await_access_points.yml -e ap_provision_after_discovery=false

    👀 Why this stage exists. Doing it by hand means reading a MAC off the controller, converting 084f.a950.f028 to 08:4f:a9:50:f0:28, and picking the Ethernet MAC rather than the Base Radio MAC that Catalyst Center shows in its inventory column. Get any of those wrong and the provision fails with an unhelpful “not found among the Unified AP devices”. The playbook removes all three traps.

    🛑 It provisions the access point only — never the controller. The 9800 is shared between pods, and re-provisioning it reboots every access point it manages, including other students'. That is why this stage calls the access-point pass directly instead of re-running stage 08, where forgetting -e wireless_provision_enabled=false would do exactly that.

    Assigning the access point to Site-105 is the step that matters most, and it is easy to underrate. An access point that is merely known to Catalyst Center still sits on default-site-tag and default-policy-tag, which map to no WLAN at all — so it broadcasts nothing. The assignment is what lets the provision step write real tags onto the controller and bind the access point to your SSID.

    The access point reboots when it is provisioned and takes 1–3 minutes to rejoin, so do not be alarmed if show ap summary is briefly empty afterwards.

    Step 4 — Confirm the tags came from Catalyst Center

    C9800#show ap tag summary
Number of APs: 1

AP Name                           AP Mac           Site Tag Name                     Policy Tag Name                   RF Tag Name                       Misconfigured    Tag Source
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
SITE-105-AP-1                     084f.a950.f028   ST_Durha_Site-105_d97a1_0         PT_Durha_Site-_MAIN_70ab7         TYPICAL                           No               Static

    Look at the Tag Source column. It should read Static, and the site and policy tag names should be Catalyst Center-generated — ST_Durha_Site-105_d97a1_0 and PT_Durha_Site-_MAIN_70ab7 above — rather than the factory default-site-tag and default-policy-tag. That is the proof that the access point is being governed by the design in Catalyst Center, not by whatever it happened to boot with.

    Step 5 — Test a client

    From here the client test is identical to the manual path. Follow Step 2: Verify wireless client connectivity in 4. Configure & Test WLC: connect the Linksys adapter on Workstation-1 through SX Virtual Link, join your SSID, and confirm the client lands in the PROD subnet 10.101.255.0/24.

    The user Pat authenticates with TEAP, and ISE returns the VLAN. Check ISE Live Logs to see the EAP chaining result.

    Step 6 — Verify wireless as part of the fabric

    There is no separate wireless verification tool, and no wireless-specific report. The stage that verified the fabric in 3. Verify Intent & Evidence Report also verifies the controller, and it now has an access point to check:

    ansible-playbook playbooks/11_verify_intent.yml
    CheckWhat it proves
    show wlan summaryThe SSID exists and is administratively up
    show ap summaryThe expected number of access points joined, each is registered, and its Ethernet MAC matches lab_ap_macs
    show ap tag summaryThe tag source is Static and the tags are Catalyst Center-generated

    Those three commands add ten checks to the same run that produces the fabric's. Switching, routing, overlay and wireless all land in one report, held to one design.

    🧭 Next up

    🎉 Fabric, templates, provisioning, and wireless — all built from source. One thing is left: understanding how the pipeline proves any of that is true.

    Continue to 7. Intent Verification as Code. If your client did not connect, detour through 8. Appendix — Wireless Troubleshooting Checklist first.

    🔬

    7. Intent Verification as Code

    Infrastructure as Code — Step 7 of 8

    Every card up to this point has been about building a fabric from source. This one is about the harder half of the problem: proving that what is running matches what you described. A pipeline that deploys confidently and verifies vaguely is worse than no pipeline, because it manufactures false confidence at scale.

    Stage 11 is the answer this lab gives. It is worth understanding properly, because the method transfers to any network you automate.

    cd ~/cisco-one-experience-lab-automation/ansible-automation/01_campus/evpn/ansible
ansible-playbook playbooks/11_verify_intent.yml

    The stage is read-only. It issues GETs and Command Runner read requests and nothing else, so run it as often as you like.

    The three rules

    Everything in the design follows from three decisions.

    RuleWhy
    Intent is read back from Catalyst Center, never from your checkoutIf the playbook read the DEFN templates from your laptop, it would verify what you meant to deploy. Reading them from Template Programmer verifies what was actually deployed. A template you edited but forgot to sync cannot produce a false pass.
    Compare parsed fields, never textA regular expression against show output breaks the day IOS-XE moves a column, and — worse — a sloppy pattern matches things it should not. The old route-distinguisher check matched :10 inside :102. Genie turns the output into structured data, so the comparison is value against value.
    Check operational state, not configurationshow running-config is deliberately never collected. A member vni line proves someone typed it; it says nothing about whether the VNI came up. show nve vni reports vni_state directly. Configuration is intent restated — only operational state is evidence.

    Where intent comes from

    Three sources, each owning a different part of the design:

    SourceOwns
    DEFN-*.j2 in Catalyst CenterVRFs, L3VNIs, loopbacks, node roles, client ports, L3OUT, overlay VLANs and SVIs
    Settings/settings.jsonWhich devices to check, the SSID, wireless VLANs, declared access points
    group_vars/all/lab.ymlYour pod number and lab_ap_macs, which resolve the {POD} and {APn_MAC} tokens in settings.json

    The DEFN files are pure {% set %} blocks, so the role concatenates the bodies it fetched, appends an epilogue that emits the lot as JSON, and renders it once. That is how a design written for humans becomes data a check can compare against.

    The anatomy of a check

    Every check lives in roles/verify_intent/vars/checks.yml and produces a list of intended / observed pairs, one set per device in scope. This is the whole of the underlay loopback check:

    - id: loopback0
  title: Underlay loopback address and state
  scope: switch
  command: show ip interface brief
  pairs: >-
    {%- set out = (_cli_output | default({})).get(host, {}).get('show ip interface brief', '') -%}
    {%- if not out -%}{{ [] }}{%- else -%}
      {%- set p = out | genie_parse('show ip interface brief') -%}
      {%- set lo = (p.interface | default({})).get('Loopback0', {}) -%}
      {{ [{'label': 'Loopback0 address',
           'intended': _intent_fabric.loop_underlay.get(host, ''),
           'observed': lo.get('ip_address', 'Loopback0 absent')},
          {'label': 'Loopback0 protocol',
           'intended': 'up',
           'observed': lo.get('protocol', 'Loopback0 absent')}] }}
    {%- endif -%}

    Note what is not there: no regular expression, no device-specific branching, and no hard-coded IP address. The same fifteen-line definition covers a leaf and a border-spine, because host is supplied per device and the intended value is looked up out of the design. Adding a fourth switch adds no check code at all.

    A check can also declare skip_when, which is how the Border-Spine correctly reports "not applicable" for tenant SVIs rather than failing them.

    The four verdicts

    This is the part most homegrown verification scripts get wrong, and it is the most important idea on this page.

    VerdictMeaning
    PASSIntended and observed are exactly equal.
    FAILBoth values are known and they differ. This is the only verdict that fails the play.
    NOT VERIFIEDCatalyst Center returned no output for the command, so the check has no verdict at all. Counting it as a pass would hide missing coverage; counting it as a failure would blame your fabric for a Command Runner restriction.
    NOT APPLICABLEThe check does not apply to that device's role — client ports on a spine, L3OUT on a leaf.

    🛑 A check that cannot be evaluated must never read as a pass. This is not theoretical. An earlier version of this role swallowed template errors and quietly reported the affected checks as "not applicable" — 45 of them — and the run finished green with a summary that said PASSED. Nothing was wrong with the fabric; nothing had been checked either. Whenever you build verification of your own, spend your effort here rather than on adding more checks. Silence is the failure mode, not mismatch.

    What the run actually does

    Seven phases, in order:

    #PhaseWhat happens
    1intent_templatesLists Template Programmer projects named in settings.json and fetches every DEFN-* body, recording each template's ID and last-modified time
    2intent_fabricAssembles those bodies plus a JSON epilogue into one template, renders it, and reads back the design as data
    3intent_settingsDerives the SSID, wireless VLANs, access points and device lists from settings.json
    4resolve_devicesTurns each management IP into a Catalyst Center device UUID and hostname — the hostname is the join key between design and device
    5run_commandsSubmits the show commands through Command Runner, five per request, polls each task, downloads the result files
    6evaluateRenders every check for every in-scope device, parses with Genie, compares, assigns a verdict
    7reportWrites Markdown and HTML, prints the summary, and only then fails

    Phase 7 failing last is deliberate. You get the whole matrix, not the first mismatch.

    👀 Why no SSH to the switches? Command Runner means the script server never needs device credentials or a route to 172.30.255.x. Verification works from anywhere Catalyst Center is reachable, and the credentials stay in one place. The cost is a five-command ceiling per request, which is why the role batches.

    A real run

    The console is quiet until the end. The last thing it prints is the summary:

    TASK [verify_intent : Verification summary] ************************************
ok: [catalyst_center_api] => {
    "msg": [
        "┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐",
        "│ Devices       : 4  (Site_105-Leaf1, Site_105-Leaf2, Site_105-Border-Spine, C9800)",
        "│ Checks        : 52",
        "│ Pass          : 45",
        "│ Fail          : 0",
        "│ Not verified  : 0",
        "│ Not applicable: 7",
        "│ Report        : .../evidence/stage11-verification.md",
        "│ HTML          : .../evidence/stage11-verification.html",
        "├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤",
        "│ Read it here  : less -R .../evidence/stage11-verification.md",
        "│ Or in Firefox : xdg-open .../evidence/stage11-verification.html",
        "│ Or on your laptop:",
        "│   scp cisco@<script-server>:.../stage11-verification.html . && open stage11-verification.html",
        "└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘"
    ]
}

TASK [verify_intent : Assert the fabric matches the declared intent] ***********
ok: [catalyst_center_api] => {
    "msg": "All 45 evaluated check(s) match the declared intent."
}

PLAY RECAP *********************************************************************
catalyst_center_api        : ok=59   changed=9   unreachable=0   failed=0

    Read the Markdown report in the terminal. rich is pinned in requirements.txt and renders the headings and tables in colour:

    python -m rich.markdown evidence/stage11-verification.md | less -R

    Press q to quit the pager. Plain less -R evidence/stage11-verification.md works too and needs nothing installed, and the HTML report is complete on its own.

    The report, section by section

    1 — Result. The headline counts, and a single PASSED or FAILED verdict.

    | Outcome        | Count |
| -------------- | ----- |
| Pass           | 45    |
| Fail           | 0     |
| Not verified   | 0     |
| Not applicable | 7     |

**PASSED** — 52 check(s) across 4 device(s).

    2 — Devices. What was checked, and the role that selected its command set.

    | Management IP | Catalyst Center hostname                  | Family              | Role   |
| ------------- | ---------------------------------------- | ------------------- | ------ |
| 172.30.255.1  | Site_105-Leaf1.corp.pseudoco.com         | Switches and Hubs   | switch |
| 172.30.255.2  | Site_105-Leaf2.corp.pseudoco.com         | Switches and Hubs   | switch |
| 172.30.255.3  | Site_105-Border-Spine.corp.pseudoco.com  | Switches and Hubs   | switch |
| 198.18.5.103  | C9800.corp.pseudoco.com                  | Wireless Controller | wlc    |

    3 — Intent sources. The section to linger on. Every DEFN template that contributed, with its Catalyst Center template ID and last-modified timestamp.

    | Template          | Project  | Template ID                          | Last updated     |
| ----------------- | -------- | ------------------------------------ | ---------------- |
| DEFN-CLIENT-PORTS.j2 | Site-105 | 3d290b65-95d3-4920-8ffb-65218d672f4b | 2026-09-16 19:12 |
| DEFN-LOOPBACKS.j2    | Site-105 | 613fe72a-fa25-4934-8fae-a9261e6c3112 | 2026-09-16 19:12 |
| DEFN-OVERLAY.j2      | Site-105 | a02e46bd-ae64-4510-91f8-193f90cb729f | 2026-09-16 19:12 |
| DEFN-ROLES.j2        | Site-105 | c9755fe0-ef1f-4a24-8d61-cfe7a9aa5b80 | 2026-09-16 19:12 |
| DEFN-VRF.j2          | Site-105 | 15b72b9a-4a62-4b11-b3e7-645952bf0f27 | 2026-09-17 11:20 |

From settings.json: SSID PSEUDOCO-POD05, wireless VLANs 10, 101, 102, 1 access point(s).

    This is the link between a Git commit and a live switch. The report does not merely say "the fabric is correct" — it names the exact version of the design it was held to. Notice that DEFN-VRF.j2 above has a later timestamp than the rest: someone edited it after the original sync, and the report says so.

    4 — Per-device checks. Intended beside observed, for every pair.

    ### Site_105-Leaf1.corp.pseudoco.com (172.30.255.1)

| Check                               | Result  | Intended                        | Observed        |
| ----------------------------------- | ------- | ------------------------------- | --------------- |
| VRFs defined                        | PASS    | VRF Main = Main                 | Main            |
| VRF route distinguishers            | PASS    | Main RD = 172.30.255.1:10       | 172.30.255.1:10 |
| L3VNI bound to its transit VLAN     | PASS    | VNI 110010 VLAN = 1010          | 1010            |
| L3VNI operationally up              | PASS    | VNI 110010 (Main) state = Up    | Up              |
| Tenant SVI addresses                | PASS    | Vlan101 address = 10.101.255.1  | 10.101.255.1    |
| DHCP helper on tenant SVIs          | PASS    | Vlan101 helper = 198.18.5.102   | 198.18.5.102    |
| Underlay loopback address and state | PASS    | Loopback0 address = 172.30.255.1| 172.30.255.1    |
| EVPN sessions established           | PASS    | peer 172.30.255.3 = established | established     |
| NVE peers up                        | PASS    | peer 172.30.255.2 = UP          | UP              |
| L3OUT BGP neighbours on the border  | SKIPPED | not applicable to this device   | —               |

    A failing row shows both sides, so the diagnosis is usually immediate:

    | VRF route distinguishers | **FAIL** | Main RD = 172.30.255.1:10555 | 172.30.255.1:10 |

  - Main RD: expected 172.30.255.1:10555, got 172.30.255.1:10

    That one is a real example from this lab: the VRF id had been changed in Catalyst Center but the fabric was still running the value it was deployed with. Design and reality had diverged, and the report said exactly where.

    Reading "not applicable" correctly

    Seven of the 52 checks do not apply, and that is healthy:

    CheckSkipped on
    Tenant VLANs, SVI addresses, SVIs up, DHCP helperBorder-Spine — the overlay template omits the L2 sections on spine and border roles
    Client-facing portsBorder-Spine — it has no client ports declared
    L3OUT BGP neighboursLeaf1 and Leaf2 — only the border peers externally

    Each of those is a deliberate skip_when, evaluated per device against the node roles in the design. The report states them rather than quietly passing them, so "not applicable" is a claim you can audit — not a gap.

    When something fails

    SymptomWhat to do
    A handful of checks fail on one deviceRe-run stage 09 — a deployment task may have timed out
    A check fails on every device with the same valueThe design changed, not the fabric. Compare the DEFN template in Catalyst Center against your checkout.
    Everything is NOT VERIFIEDCommand Runner is unhappy. Confirm the devices are reachable and managed in Inventory.
    The run aborts naming a VRF idDEFN_VRF_TO_NODE references an id that DEFN_VRF does not define. Fix the template in Template Programmer.
    You want to see the raw CLI and the parsed intentRe-run with -e catc_debug=true
    You want the report without the play failingRun with -e verify_fail_on_mismatch=false

    Why this matters beyond the lab
