    Three switches and a controller do not need automated verification; you could log in and look. Fifty sites do, and at that scale the qualities that matter are exactly the ones on this page: intent read from the system of record rather than from someone's laptop, comparison against parsed operational state, and a verdict model that refuses to turn "I could not check" into "everything is fine".

    The report is the deliverable. It is timestamped, it names the template versions it validated, it shows both sides of every comparison, and it is self-contained enough to attach to a change record. That is what separates automation from a script.

    🧭 Complete

    🎉 Built from source, deployed through a pipeline, and proven against the design with evidence you could hand to an auditor. You have completed the automated path.

    If your client did not connect, work through 8. Appendix — Wireless Troubleshooting Checklist. Otherwise continue to Macro / Micro Segmentation.

    🛠️

    8. Appendix — Wireless Troubleshooting Checklist

    When the SSID is visible but the client never connects

    This is the classic wireless failure, and it is worth knowing how to work through it methodically rather than by guessing. The checklist below is distilled from a real troubleshooting session on this lab.

    👀 Optional reference, and it applies to both tracks. Use this only if something is not working. Nothing here is required to complete the lab. The checks are the same whether you configured the controller by hand in 4. Configure & Test WLC or the pipeline built it in 6. Verify Wireless & Provision the Access Point — only the fix differs, and that is called out where it matters.

    The symptom

    The SSID broadcasts normally. The client sees it, tries to join, and never reaches the RUN state. Authentication may even succeed.

    The root cause, so you know what you are hunting for

    ISE returned a VLAN name — PROD — through AAA override. The FlexConnect profile that Catalyst Center generated only contained a mapping for PSEUDOCO-VLAN10 to VLAN 10, plus native VLAN 1. With no entry for the name ISE sent, the controller raised a VLAN Failure and excluded the client.

    The lesson generalises: when AAA override returns a VLAN name, that exact name must exist in the FlexConnect profile's VLAN mapping. Authentication succeeding tells you nothing about whether the client can be placed.

    Work the problem in order

    #CheckHow, and what good looks like
    1Access point is registered show ap summary — the access point must be Registered. If not, this is a wired problem, not a wireless one. Go to step 2.
    2The access-point trunk is correct On the leaf: show interface Gi1/0/2 trunk. Native VLAN 10, allowed 10, 101, 102.
    Manual track: re-apply the access-port configuration from 1. EVPN Fabric Provisioning.
    IaC track: stage 09 did not deploy — re-run it.
    3The SSID is enabled show wlan summary — your pod's SSID present and admin state up.
    4The access point carries the right tags show ap tag summary — tag source should be Static, not factory default.
    Manual track: the Policy tag DCLOUD-XAR-FLEX-PT and Site tag FLEX-SITE are set by hand on the Edit AP page — recheck them.
    IaC track: tags are Catalyst Center-generated; factory defaults mean stage 10 never provisioned the access point.
    5The client reaches authentication ISE Operations > Live Logs. Look for the endpoint MAC. No entry at all means the client never got as far as RADIUS — recheck steps 2 to 4.
    6What ISE actually returned Open the ISE detailed authentication report. Note the authorisation profile and, critically, the exact VLAN value — is it a number or a name?
    7The controller's view of the client show wireless client mac-address <mac> detail. Look for the state and any exclusion reason. VLAN Failure here is the smoking gun.
    8The FlexConnect VLAN mapping show wireless profile flex detailed <profile-name>. Every VLAN name ISE can return must appear in this mapping, with the right VLAN id. This is where the fault usually is.
    9Fix it in the right place Manual track: correct the VLAN mapping directly in the 9800 under Configuration > Tags & Profiles > Flex, then re-join the client.
    IaC track: correct it in the network profile, re-run stage 07, then re-provision the controller so the change reaches it \u2014 see the note below. Fixing it by hand on the controller works until the next provision overwrites it.

    🛑 Re-provisioning the controller, without rebooting everybody's access points. A FlexConnect VLAN mapping lives on the 9800, so a corrected network profile only reaches it when the controller itself is provisioned — -e wireless_provision_enabled=false would skip the very thing you are trying to fix. But the 9800 is shared across pods, and by default a controller provision re-provisions every access point it manages. Set skip_ap_provision to true in the wireless_controller block of Settings/settings.json first — that is Catalyst Center's Skip AP Provision option, and it pushes the controller configuration while leaving the access points alone. Then run:

    ansible-playbook playbooks/07_network_profile.yml
ansible-playbook playbooks/08_provision_devices.yml -e ap_provision_enabled=false

    In a production network you would schedule this in a change window rather than reach for the skip flag.

    Commands that waste your time here

    CommandWhy it misleads
    show wlan summary aloneA healthy SSID tells you nothing about client placement
    Checking RADIUS reachabilityAuthentication was succeeding the whole time — the failure was after it
    Rebooting the access pointThe mapping is wrong on the controller; the access point is fine
    show running-config on the controllerEnormous, and it shows intent rather than the operational state that is actually failing

    🛑 The general rule. Wireless client failures are usually placement failures, not authentication failures. Find out what the policy server returned, then confirm the controller knows what to do with that value.

    🧭 Next up

    Client connected? Continue to Macro / Micro Segmentation. Still stuck? Grab a proctor — and bring the output of steps 7 and 8, which is what they will ask for.

    Macro / Micro Segmentation

    📄

    1. Overview

    Enforcing Zero Trust with Macro and Micro Segmentation Using Business Security Zones

    With the campus fabric and secure wireless edge now operational, your next milestone as the lead architect for PseudoCo is validating one of the company’s most critical Zero Trust initiatives: business-aligned segmentation across wired and wireless environments.

    As part of their secure architecture transformation built on technologies from Cisco, PseudoCo has standardized on three core security segments that map directly to business risk, operational function, and access control requirements.

    These segments will serve two purposes simultaneously:

    • Macro Segmentation → Implemented using Fabric Virtual Networks (VNs)

    • Micro Segmentation → Enforced using Security Group Tags (SGTs) and TrustSec policy

    This dual-use model simplifies operations while strengthening Zero Trust enforcement across the entire environment.

    The Three Core Security Segments

    PseudoCo has defined three primary network security domains that represent the majority of business traffic and risk boundaries:

    MAIN – Corporate User and Business Operations

    This segment represents the core enterprise user environment and includes:

    • Corporate employee endpoints

    • Collaboration systems

    • Business SaaS and internal applications

    • Standard enterprise services

    Security Goal:
    Provide secure user access while enforcing least privilege between user groups and applications.

    Risk Profile:
    Medium — largest attack surface due to user activity, phishing risk, and credential compromise.

    PROD – Critical Business and Application Infrastructure

    This segment represents revenue-generating and mission-critical services such as:

    • Production application servers

    • Databases

    • Core business platforms

    • Sensitive internal services

    Security Goal:
    Strictly control which users, devices, and services can communicate with production workloads.

    Risk Profile:
    High — direct business and financial impact if compromised.

    IoT – Operational Technology and Non-User Devices

    This segment represents devices that typically cannot run endpoint security software or strong authentication, including:

    • Cameras

    • Badge readers

    • Environmental sensors

    • Manufacturing or building systems

    • Printers and facility devices

    Security Goal:
    Strictly limit communication to only required services and prevent lateral movement.

    Risk Profile:
    High — often vulnerable, difficult to patch, and attractive attacker pivot points.

    Macro Segmentation – Business Domain Isolation Using Fabric VNs

    At the macro level, these three segments are isolated using Virtual Network constructs in the fabric:

    • Main VN 

    • PROD VN

    • IOT VN

    This provides PseudoCo with:

    • Large-scale traffic isolation between business domains

    • Simplified compliance boundary enforcement

    • Reduced blast radius during security incidents

    Macro segmentation ensures that a compromise in IoT cannot directly reach Production resources, and that corporate user traffic is separated from operational systems by default.

    This directly supports PseudoCo’s goal of consistent policy enforcement across hybrid environments and user locations. 

    Micro Segmentation – Identity-Based Enforcement Using SGTs

    Where PseudoCo truly enables Zero Trust is in micro segmentation.

    The same segment definitions are also mapped directly to SGT identity context:

    • Main SGT

    • PROD SGT

    • IOT SGT

    This allows PseudoCo to enforce policy based on who or what the device is, not where it connects from.

    This policy travels with the user or device across:

    • Wired campus access

    • Wireless access (including FlexConnect branch deployments)

    • WAN transport

    • Data center firewall enforcement

    • Secure access and ZTNA workflows

    Why Reusing These SGTs Across the Entire Architecture Matters

    PseudoCo has made a strategic decision to treat SGTs as the universal policy language across the environment.

    The same Main, PROD, and IOT SGT context will be reused for:

    • Firewall policy enforcement in data center and edge security controls

    • Secure access and Zero Trust application access decisions

    • Context sharing across network and security platforms

    • Consistent policy enforcement regardless of user or device location

    This eliminates the traditional gap between network segmentation and security policy enforcement.

    Story Context for This Lab Step

    Recently, PseudoCo’s security team simulated an attack involving a compromised building sensor.

    In the legacy network:

    • The device had broad VLAN access

    • East-west traffic visibility was limited

    • Lateral movement risk was high

    In the new segmentation model you are validating:

    • The IoT SGT limits communication to only required services

    • PROD resources are protected by both VN isolation and SGT policy

    • MAIN users cannot directly reach production workloads without explicit policy

    • Security teams gain full visibility into access decisions

    This represents a shift from network-based trust to identity-based trust.

    What Success Looks Like

    By completing this section of the lab, you help PseudoCo achieve:

    • Consistent segmentation across wired and wireless endpoints

    • Business-aligned segmentation using MAIN, PROD, and IoT security zones

    • Fabric-wide macro segmentation using Virtual Networks

    • Identity-driven micro segmentation using SGTs

    • Reusable security context across firewall, secure access, and Zero Trust platforms

    📄

    2. Macro Segmentation

    Verify Macro Segmentation

    In this section, we will test macro-based segmentation utilizing a simple ping between a workstation and a test loopback interface in a different VRF.

    Workstation RDP access

    From Jumphost1, use mRemoteNG to open RDP connections to Workstation-1, Workstation-3, and Workstation-IOT. These will be all wired tests. At any time you can include any of the workstations as a wireless test as well if you would like to explore. Just connect the Wireless NIC to the workstation and include it in a test. 

    Please disconnect the wireless NIC from Workstation-1 from the last section before proceeding.

    Authenticate second NIC and test connectivity

    Connect Workstations to the Fabric network:

    First, use SX Virtual Link software to connect Wired Network Adapters in each of the (3) Workstations above. (the names start with ASIX elec. Corp).

    🛑 Note: The SX Virtual Link will initially show three adapters as "Available". Please only connect 1 "Available" adapter per Workstation. As you connect each Workstation, the state of the adapter will change from "Available" to "In use".

    Once the second NIC is connected, Workstations1-3 will perform IEEE 802.1x TEAP based Authentication. The Authentication parameters are already pre-configured on the domain Workstations and each it set to login as a specific user as shown in the table below.

    Workstation-IOT, once connected will authenticate via MAB (Mac Authentication Bypass) and be treated as a headless IOT device. We will investigate our policy structure we have implemented to accomplish this after our initial segmentation testing.

    After the successful IEEE 802.1x TEAP or MAB authentication, ISE will assign the corresponding Virtual Network (VRF) and VLAN to the device as well as the TrustSec (SGT).

    Once connected, click the ping-test app on the desktop. From the app menu enter 5 and then hit enter to run the ipconfig /all command. In this example, we are running the command on Workstation-1. Your client should have been given an IP address in the PROD network segment of 10.101.255.XXX/24, for Workstation-1.

    Next, verify the other (2) Workstations get IP addresses from the correct network range. Refer to the table below for the reference.

    Workstation User Virtual Network (VRF) SGT IP subnet
    Workstation-1 Pat PROD (VRF 101) Production (19) 10.101.255.0/24
    Workstation-2 Kit PROD (VRF 101) Production (19) 10.101.255.0/24
    Workstation-3 Nik Main (VRF 10) Main (16) 10.10.255.0/24
    Workstation-IOT - IOT (VRF 102) IoT (18) 10.102.255.0/24

    Test connectivity between network segments (using test loopback interfaces)

    Perform the following ping connectivity tests using the Ping-Test app on the Workstation being tested (Example Below).

    Workstation Ping Test Option Ping To Result
    Workstation-1 2 PROD (VRF 101) 100.100.101.105 Success 
    Workstation-1 1 Main (VRF 10) 100.100.10.105 Failure
    Workstation-1 3 IOT (VRF 102) 100.100.102.105 Failure
    Workstation-3 1 Main (VRF 10) 100.100.10.105 Success 
    Workstation-3 2 PROD (VRF 101) 100.100.101.10 Failure
    Workstation-3 3 IOT (VRF 102) 100.100.102.105 Failure
    Workstation-IOT 3 IOT (VRF 102) 100.100.102.105 Success 
    Workstation-IOT 2 PROD (VRF 101) 100.100.101.105 Failure
    Workstation-IOT 1 Main (VRF 10) 100.100.10.105 Failure
    Workstation-1 and 2 - Ping between Workstation-1 and 2 Success

    Notice the ping failures across different segments (From Workstation-1 PROD VRF to Main VRF test Loopback) as an example. This is an example of Macro Segmentation working.

    Additionally, connect both Workstation-1 and Workstation-2 (you will have to disconnect one of the other Workstations) Then run option 5 on the ping-test app to obtain both Workstation IP's and ping between them. Notice that reachability is successful between Workstation-1 and Workstation-2 because they are connected to the same VN segment (PROD VRF). We can verify this by looking at the ISE logs next. 

    ISE exploration and verification

    In this section we will investigate and verify the ISE configuration. This is the foundation for our segmentation policy.

    First, login to ISE via Jumphost1 using the Chrome bookmark. You can also access it directly by browsing to https://ise.corp.pseudoco.com/. Login with the credentials: admin / C1sco12345

    Next, navigate to the ISE Policy.  Select Policy -> Policy Sets in the left pane.

    Next, we will look at the Wired Policy Set. Select the > icon on the far right of the Wired policy set. 

    Expand the Authorization Policy section by clicking the down arrow.

    First, explore the Prod_TEAP_machine_only policy, and notice the Conditions and Results columns. We are checking the presence of certificate membership in Active Directory (a common customer deployment) and assigning a results profile (Permit-Wired-PROD or Permit-Wired-Main) respectively.

    The results profile assigns the user to a VLAN that maps to VRF PROD or Main on the switch. Let’s now examine the Results Profile. You will see as well that we are assigning the Security Groups (SGT's) Production and Main respectively. These are a key component to extending common policy throughout the infrastructure.  We will be exploring SGT capabilities throughout the rest of the lab.

    Next, navigate back to the ISE Policy.  Select Policy -> Policy Sets in the left pane.

    We will now look at the IOT Policy Set. Click the > icon on the far right of the IOT Policy Set to expand it. 

    In the Authorization Policy list, find the Wired-MAB Policy and click it to investigate. This policy is designed to authenticate our IOT devices that can't participate in our normal corporate 802.1X TEAP based authentication. This policy as you can see below is using MAB (Mac Authentication Bypass) to authenticate trusted devices that have been added to the ISE endpoint database in the Identity Group IOT.

    The policy also assigns the Security Group Tag (SGT) of IoT to the device.  In our case, this policy takes place after an endpoint times out on the 802.1X process.  MAB is a method to authenticate IOT or untrusted devices in your environment and easily wrap controls around their access.

    Next, we will investigate an Authorization Profile for the PROD network segment. You can follow the same process as below to explore both Main and IOT profiles.

    Navigate to Policy -> Policy Elements -> Results, then select Authorization -> Authorization Profiles.  Locate the Permit-Wired-PROD profile and open it to explore the configuration.

    Observe the ACCES_ACCEPT and notice a VLAN name of PROD is being assigned in the Common Tasks area. This VLAN name is pushed down to the switch in the Attributes section of the RADIUS Access Accept message. This VLAN’s SVI on the switch is assigned to the corresponding VRF (PROD or Main), thereby segmenting the device. 

    Verify Authentication Results using ISE Live Logs. On ISE, go to Operations -> RADIUS -> Live Logs.
    🛑 NOTE: For best results, Disconnect and Reconnect the Workstation-1, Workstation-3 and Workstation-IOT NIC's to get a fresh set of authentications to investigate. 

    Find and examine the RADIUS authentication of our Workstation-1, Workstation-3 and Workstation-IOT. Notice what Authorization Policy and Authorization Profile was assigned to each device, as well as things like the SGT assignment and all the other great details that are captured.

    Optionally, you can click on the Magnifying Glass icon to verify the authentication details. Try investigating one of the reports to see how our complete policy comes together. 

    Key sections to explore from the Authentication Details Report for a PROD user.

    Investigate switch authentication

    In this section we will login to the leaf switches and investigate the authentication process.

    • Login into both Leaf1 and Leaf2 switches via the mRemote client on Jumphost1.
    • The clients are connected across Gig 1/0/1 and Gig 1/0/3 on both switches.
    • Locate an authenticated client session with the following command:

    show authentication sessions interface gig 1/0/x detail

    Observe the 802.1x Username, VRF, Vlan Group, and SGT Value being assigned. Optionally, you can run the command on both switches to observe the different outputs for a PROD, Main, and IOT authentication session.

    Macro segmentation establishes the foundational security boundaries within PseudoCo’s campus fabric by isolating business domains using Virtual Networks (VNs, aka VRFs). By mapping MAIN, PROD, and IoT environments into separate logical networks, PseudoCo achieves large-scale traffic isolation, reduced attack surface, and simplified compliance enforcement. This Cisco-driven approach provides a powerful first layer of Zero Trust by containing threats and preventing unauthorized east-west movement between critical domains. The key advantage lies in its scalability and simplicity—enabling consistent segmentation across wired and wireless environments while forming a strong baseline for deeper, identity-based controls.

Let's say I wanted to implement a security policy that stops specific traffic within the same VRF. In other words big walls, big wins! 🧱🚧 You’ve locked down the neighborhood—now let’s zoom in and control who gets through the front door! 🔍🔐

    3. Micro Segmentation

    Verify Micro Segmentation

    In this section, we will test SGT-based micro-segmentation (Cisco TrustSec) utilizing simple pings between Workstation-1 and Workstation-2. As a reminder, please see the user and SGT policy assigned to each Workstation.

    Workstation User Virtual Network (VRF) SGT IP subnet
    Workstation-1 Pat PROD (VRF 101) Production (19) 10.101.255.0/24
    Workstation-2 Kit PROD (VRF 101) Production (19) 10.101.255.0/24

    Workstation RDP access / Authenticate second NIC and test connectivity

    Start by logging into Workstation-1 and Workstation-2 via the mRemote application on the Jumphost1. Use the same process as in the previous sections when working with the Workstations.

    Verify that both Workstations get a 10.101.255.x address utilizing the ping-test application and option (5) to obtain the assigned IP address on both Workstation-1 and Workstation 2

    🛑Note: This step can be skipped if already done in the Macro-segmentation section. You may also use the wireless adapter as well if you would like to see Micro-segmentation enforcement across wired / wireless.
    

    

    

    Once IP address verification is complete, issue a continuous ping between Workstation-1 and Workstation-2 (10.101.255.X). These two workstations should be on the same subnet. Verify you can ping between the two workstations.
    

    

    Leave the continuous ping running for the next step.
    

    

    Enable pre-defined group-based policy in ISE
    

    In this section, we will enable a pre-defined policy to stop lateral pings between these devices with the same SGT. This policy was created and pushed via ISE to the switches. Please note that this is a simple test, however, this policy method can be used to enforce very granular business intent. 
    

    Login to ISE utilizing the Chrome bookmark favorite or directly at https://ise.corp.pseudoco.com using the credentials below.
    

    admin / C1sco12345
    

    From the menu, select Work Centers -> TrustSec Policy to view the TrustSec Policy Matrix.
    

    

    Explore the Production SGT Matrix. Notice all present SGT's in ISE have both a Source and Destination forming a policy Matrix. For our example, we only have (1) current policy between Production and Production SGT's, this was for simplicity for the Lab.
    

    Click the intersection of Production / Production. This will launch the policy editor.
    

    

    In the Edit Permissions, click the drop-down under status and Enable the policy. Take note of the DENY_ICMP assigned Security Group ACL. When complete, click Save to save the policy.
    

    🛑 If you are unable to click to edit the Policy. Please open ISE in incognito mode to resolve the issue.
    

    🛑 NOTE: By creating additional custom SGACL's, you can customize your policy as granular as you wish between the Security Groups. 
    

    

    

    

    Back in the Production Matrix, observe that the Production to Production Policy is now enabled. Complete the Policy deployment to our infrastructure by clicking Deploy. Click to accept the information pop-up.
    

    

    Shortly after the deployment, switch back over to your continuous pings between Workstation-1 and Workstation-2 and you will see we have successfully deployed our micro-segmentatin policy and stopped all lateral communications between the Workstations. The continuous pings will now be timing out.
    

    👀 Take a moment to realize how powerful this secure networking TrustSec capability is and how easy it was to deploy!
    

    

    

    Investigate Switch Port SGT enforcement. 
    

    From Jumphost1, launch the mRemote application and log into Leaf1 and Leaf2.  The Workstations are connected across Gig 1/0/1 and Gig 1/0/3 on both switches.
    

    Locate an authenticated client session with the following command:
    

    show authentication sessions interface gig 1/0/x detail (run this until you locate the two PROD workstations)
    

    Remember, here we see the SGT value being pushed down to the switch via ISE.
    

    

    

    

    Let’s investigate this now. Issue the following command from the both Leaf switches
    

    show cts role-based permissions
    

    

    Observe the rule being installed to the switch. We are denying ICMP from group-to-group. In this case it is the same group stopping the lateral ICMP pings from occurring. This same process can be used to stop the lateral movement of malware by creating another policy denying communication between devices in the same group or across different groups. SGT’s are a powerful capability to create segmentation across the network. Notice they are IP agnostic, meaning we are not defining IP address ranges / hosts to block traffic. They can be combined with VRF's providing macro-segmentation as we just illustrated in the lab.
    

    🛑NOTE: You can now disable the Micro-Segmentation Policy if you would like and re-deploy from ISE. Re-run the above commands and you will see the SGACL is removed from the switch and your pings will re-start again.
    

    

    Micro-segmentation builds on macro-level isolation by enforcing identity-driven policy using Security Group Tags (SGTs), allowing PseudoCo to control access based on user, device, and role rather than network location. This is where Cisco’s TrustSec architecture becomes a true differentiator—enabling policy to follow the endpoint across wired, wireless, WAN, and cloud environments. By reusing SGTs like MAIN, PROD, and IoT as a universal policy language, PseudoCo eliminates policy silos and ensures consistent enforcement across all security platforms. The result is a dynamic, scalable Zero Trust model that minimizes lateral movement, simplifies policy management, and adapts in real time to changes in identity or posture.

    Enable SGTs for Consistent Identity
    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    Extending Identity and Policy Beyond the Campus: Sharing SGT Context with Security Enforcement Platforms
    

    With business-aligned segmentation now validated across wired and wireless endpoints, the next major milestone for PseudoCo is extending that same identity and policy context beyond the campus fabric and into the broader secure architecture.
    

    PseudoCo’s long-term vision has always been bigger than campus segmentation. Their goal is to ensure that identity, device posture, and business role context travel with every connection — from access layer to cloud security enforcement.
    

    To achieve this, PseudoCo is leveraging deep platform integration across their secure networking stack built on solutions from Cisco.
    

    

    The Strategic Goal
    

    PseudoCo does not want separate policy silos for:
    

      

    • 

      Network segmentation
      

      • 

    • 

      Firewall enforcement
      

      • 

    • 

      Secure access / ZTNA decisions
      

      • 

    • 

      WAN segmentation
      

      • 

    • 

      Application access policy
      

      • 

    

    Instead, they want one shared security context model that can be consumed everywhere policy is enforced.
    

    That shared context is built around:
    

      

    • 

      Identity (user and device)
      

      • 

    • 

      Security Group Tags (Main, PROD, IOT)
      

      • 

    • 

      Authentication and posture state
      

      • 

    • 

      Group and role-based policy
      

      • 

    

    This directly supports PseudoCo’s Zero Trust goal of consistent policy enforcement regardless of where users or devices connect. 
    

    

    What You Are Enabling in This Lab Section
    

    In this phase, you will extend segmentation and identity context into:
    

      

    • 

      Secure Access policy enforcement and ZTNA workflows
      

      • 

    • 

      Data center firewall policy authoring and enforcement
      

      • 

    • 

      WAN segmentation and context propagation
      

      • 

    • 

      Cross-domain security visibility and telemetry
      

      • 

    

    This is where PseudoCo transitions from segmented networks to unified Zero Trust policy enforcement.

      • 

    •

      •

      • 

    • 

      Devices changed networks
      

      •

      •

      Security policy drift occurred

    Now, PseudoCo is standardizing on identity and SGT-driven policy everywhere.

    PseudoCo’s identity team has standardized on centralized identity and policy orchestration.

      • 

    •

      •

    This is a major step in enabling identity as the universal policy source.

    Next, PseudoCo uses native integration workflows to connect identity context directly into:
    

    Secure Access Policy Enforcement
    

    This enables:

    This enables:
    

      

    • 

      Firewall rules based on SGT context instead of IP address
      

      • 

    • 

      Dynamic policy adaptation as identity or posture changes
      

      • 

    • 

      Simplified firewall rulebase management
      

      • 

    • 

      Reduced risk of policy drift
      

      • 

    

    This is especially critical for protecting PROD workloads from unauthorized lateral movement.
    

    

    Phase 4 – Verifying WAN Context Sharing
    

    PseudoCo also validates that SGT and identity context is already being shared across the WAN fabric.
    

    This ensures:
    

      

    • 

      Segmentation policy is preserved between sites
      

      • 

    • 

      Identity context survives transport across WAN overlay
      

      • 

    • 

      Branch-to-campus and branch-to-data center traffic maintains policy context
      

      • 

    

    This is critical for ensuring Zero Trust enforcement remains consistent across all locations.
    

    

    What This Means for the Business
    

    By completing this step, PseudoCo achieves something most legacy architectures cannot:
    

    One Identity → One Context → One Policy Model → Everywhere
    

    Instead of translating policy between systems, PseudoCo can now:
    

      

    • 

      Write policy once using business intent
      

      • 

    • 

      Enforce policy everywhere using shared context
      

      • 

    • 

      Audit and validate policy centrally
      

      • 

    • 

      Adapt policy dynamically as risk changes
      

      • 

    

    

    Forward Story Tie-In
    

    The Main, PROD, and IOT SGTs are no longer just campus segmentation tools.
    

    They are now:
    

      

    • 

      Firewall policy objects
      

      • 

    • 

      Secure application access context
      

      • 

    • 

      Zero Trust decision inputs
      

      • 

    • 

      Cross-domain security telemetry tags
      

      • 

    

    This is the moment where PseudoCo fully activates identity-driven security across the entire architecture.
    

    You are not just integrating platforms.
    You are enabling PseudoCo’s unified Zero Trust policy engine.

As a Cisco differentiator, pxGrid Cloud provides scalable, API-driven context exchange that ensures policy decisions remain dynamic, consistent, and identity-based across the entire architecture—significantly reducing integration friction while enhancing security visibility and responsiveness.

This integration showcases a powerful Cisco advantage: the ability to extend segmentation context directly into cloud-delivered security services. As a result, PseudoCo achieves consistent, scalable, and dynamic access control across all users and locations, simplifying policy management while strengthening security through context-aware decision making.

    Connect Infrastructure to Secure Access
    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    Section Overview – Connecting Infrastructure to Secure Access
    

    With segmentation now implemented and identity context being shared across the environment, PseudoCo is ready to take the next major step in its Zero Trust journey: securely connecting its infrastructure to the cloud-delivered security platform.
    

    PseudoCo’s leadership recognized that traditional network security models—where users connect through centralized VPN concentrators and security stacks—no longer scale for a modern workforce. Employees, contractors, and devices are accessing applications from branch offices, headquarters, and remote locations, often across multiple networks.
    

    To solve this challenge, PseudoCo has adopted a Security Service Edge (SSE) architecture built on Cisco Secure Access. This platform will become the central policy enforcement point for:
    

      

    • 

      Zero Trust Network Access (ZTNA) to private applications
      

      • 

    • 

      Secure Web Gateway and internet access control
      

      • 

    • 

      VPN-as-a-Service connectivity
      

      • 

    • 

      Identity-driven application access
      

      • 

    • 

      Context sharing across networking and security platforms
      

      • 

    

    

    The Business Objective
    

    PseudoCo’s goal is simple but powerful:
    

    Provide secure, identity-driven access to applications from anywhere while maintaining consistent policy enforcement across the entire infrastructure.
    

    To achieve this, the organization must establish secure connectivity between its infrastructure and the Secure Access cloud.
    

    This connectivity allows Secure Access to:
    

      

    • 

      Reach private applications hosted in the data center
      

      • 

    • 

      Enforce identity-based policy for remote users
      

      • 

    • 

      Inspect and control traffic leaving branch networks
      

      • 

    • 

      Apply consistent security controls regardless of user location
      

      • 

    

    

    Two Connectivity Models in the PseudoCo Architecture
    

    In this section of the lab, you will implement two complementary connectivity methods that reflect how real enterprises integrate on-premises infrastructure with cloud-delivered security.
    

    

    1. Automated Branch Integration Using SD-WAN and SSE
    

    The first integration point occurs at the branch edge, where PseudoCo has deployed a secure WAN edge router powered by Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN.
    

    Rather than manually configuring tunnels and routing policies, PseudoCo leverages the native SSE integration between SD-WAN and Secure Access.
    

    Using secure API-driven automation, this integration will:
    

      

    • 

      Automatically establish secure tunnels between the branch router and Secure Access Points of Presence
      

      • 

    • 

      Simplify large-scale branch onboarding
      

      • 

    • 

      Ensure consistent routing and security policy enforcement
      

      • 

    • 

      Reduce operational complexity and configuration overhead
      

      • 

    

    This automated integration enables PseudoCo to scale secure connectivity across dozens—or even hundreds—of branch sites without manual tunnel configuration.
    

    

    2. Manual Infrastructure Integration Using Network Tunnel Groups (NTGs)
    

    While automated integration works well for branch environments, PseudoCo also operates on-premises data center infrastructure that must securely integrate with the Secure Access platform.
    

    In this section you will configure Network Tunnel Groups (NTGs) to establish secure connectivity between Secure Access and PseudoCo’s headquarters firewall.
    

    This firewall is powered by Cisco Secure Firewall running on an FTD device and managed through Cisco Defense Orchestrator / cdFMC.
    

    These NTG tunnels will allow the firewall to serve as a secure gateway into PseudoCo’s internal application environment.
    

    Once established, these tunnels will support multiple security workflows including:
    

      

    • 

      VPN-as-a-Service connectivity for remote users
      

      • 

    • 

      Private application access for Zero Trust workflows
      

      • 

    • 

      Secure web access inspection and policy enforcement
      

      • 

    • 

      Controlled connectivity between cloud security services and on-premises resources
      

      • 

    

    

    Why This Step Matters
    

    Connecting infrastructure to Secure Access transforms the architecture from a segmented network into a globally accessible Zero Trust platform.
    

    Once these connections are established, Secure Access becomes the central point where:
    

      

    • 

      Identity is verified
      

      • 

    • 

      Device posture is evaluated
      

      • 

    • 

      Access policy is enforced
      

      • 

    • 

      Traffic is inspected and controlled
      

      • 

    

    At the same time, the infrastructure you deployed earlier—campus fabric, SD-WAN segmentation, and firewall enforcement—continues to provide local segmentation and transport security.
    

    This layered model allows PseudoCo to enforce security both locally and in the cloud, providing stronger protection without sacrificing user experience.
    

    

    Story Context
    

    PseudoCo’s IT leadership recently launched a company-wide initiative to support hybrid work and cloud application access. Employees now regularly connect from homes, branch offices, and mobile environments.
    

    Previously, remote access relied on centralized VPN infrastructure that struggled to scale and introduced latency for users far from headquarters.
    

    By integrating the network infrastructure with Secure Access, PseudoCo enables:
    

      

    • 

      Secure application access from anywhere
      

      • 

    • 

      Cloud-delivered security inspection
      

      • 

    • 

      Identity-driven policy enforcement
      

      • 

    • 

      Reduced dependency on centralized VPN infrastructure
      

      • 

    

    This approach allows PseudoCo to deliver modern secure access without sacrificing performance or operational simplicity.
    

    

    What You Will Achieve in This Section
    

    By the end of this section of the lab, you will have:
    

      

    • 

      Integrated the branch SD-WAN router with Secure Access using automated SSE integration
      

      • 

    • 

      Established secure tunnels from branch infrastructure to the Secure Access cloud
      

      • 

    • 

      Created Network Tunnel Groups connecting the on-prem HQ firewall to Secure Access
      

      • 

    • 

      Enabled connectivity required for VPNaaS, private application access, and web access enforcement
      

      • 

    

    These connections will serve as the foundation for the Zero Trust access workflows you will configure in the next sections of the lab.
    

    
  
    📄
    
    2. Automatic SSE Tunnel creation Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN
    
  
    
  
    Automatic SSE Tunnel creation with Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN
    

    Task: Explore SD-WAN Config and Policy Groups
    

    In this section, we will be using an API integration with Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN to automate the deployment of our SSE Tunnels on our Secure Router at the branch to Secure Access. First, we will explore Catalyst SD-WAN Manger since this is our first time logging into the platform. 
    

    Start by using the Chrome bookmark favorite called Cisco SD-WAN.
    

    Login using: admin / C1sco12345
    

    Observe from the Monitor -> Overview page that we see (3) reachable WAN Edges. If you do not see 3 WAN Edges, please engage the proctor to assist.
    

    

    Next, select Configuration -> Configuration Groups. Here you will see all of the Configuration Groups that drive our various Site specific configuration. In this case, our Site-105 site is using the PseudocoBranches Config Group.  Click to expand the Config Group properties. 
    

    

    Observe the Configuration Profiles here. This is where the power of Config Groups comes in - we will explore the Service Profile section here so you can see how our VN network segmentation is extended through our VPN's in our SD-WAN Fabric. To see this, click on the edit pencil of the Service Profile. 
    

    

    Here you will notice the (3) Service VPN's we are extending over the SD-WAN Fabric which match the Campus ones previously deployed: IOT / Main / PROD. Feel free to explore the configurations. Please engage the proctor if you have any questions about how we are configuring SD-WAN for our environment. 
    

    

    🛑 DO NOT UPDATE OR CHANGE  ANY CONFIGURATIONS 🛑
    

    

    Next, we will explore the Policy Groups configuration. This is what we will be configuring for our SSE tunnel automation, as well as our on-box security capabilities, later in this lab.

    To start, select Configuration -> Policy Groups. Observe the configured Policy Groups. The Policy Group we will be using is the Pseudoco-Branch-Policy. Click to expand this policy. 
    

    

    Notice the Secure Service Edge policy is blank, this is what we will be configuring. Also take note of the NGFW Policy that is configured, we will be working with this later in this Lab to configure the on-box security features such as IPS, NGFW, and URL filtering of our Secure Router at the branch. 
    

    

    🛑 DO NOT UPDATE OR CHANGE  ANY CONFIGURATIONS 🛑
    

    So far, we have familiarized ourselves with the current Configuration and Policy Groups in our SD-WAN environment. 
    

    In summary, Cisco SD-WAN Configuration Groups and Policy Groups provide a scalable and modular approach to managing network settings and traffic behavior across distributed environments. Configuration Groups allow administrators to define and apply device-specific settings (such as interfaces, routing, and system parameters) to multiple devices in a consistent and reusable way, reducing manual configuration effort. Policy Groups, on the other hand, control how traffic is handled by defining centralized rules for routing, security, and application-aware policies. Together, they enable streamlined management, improved consistency, and faster deployment, while also supporting flexibility through hierarchical design and reusability—making it easier to maintain large, complex SD-WAN deployments with minimal errors and operational overhead.
    

    

    Create the API integration between Secure Access and Catalyst SD-WAN
    

    To start the Automation of the SSE Tunnel creation to Secure Access, we must first create the API keys needed for Catalyst SD-WAN Manager and Cisco Secure Access to share information. You need to generate a key pair from Cisco Secure Access.
    

    First, if you remember from the Environment Preparation, we walked through how to access your SCC (Security Cloud Control) tenant. If you need a reminder:
    

    

    You will access through your Dashboard automation link in your Google Chrome favorites bookmark. By clicking on the Dashboard icon, you will launch the dashboard automation interface giving you access to various different accounts the lab session has automated for you. 
    

    🛑 Note: Depending on the size of your desktop you might have to expand the toolbar favorites to see the dashboard icon. It is always the last favorite in the toolbar.
    

    Once clicked, you will see all of our accounts associated with your lab session. Throughout the guide there will be specific instructions on how to use each of these when needed. To practice accessing these accounts, let's explore your "Cisco Security Cloud Control" or SCC Org by clicking View to auto-login to your  SCC Org. 
    

    

    

    

    Once logged into SCC, select Secure Access, then navigate to Admin -> Management -> API Keys.
    

    

    In the upper right, click the Add button.
    

    🛑NOTE: There could be other keys present just ignore and create a new one
    

    

    First, give the API key a name of your choice. Then from within the Key Scope section, select All of the Scopes. Set the scopes to Read/Write when possible as shown below. Leave the rest of the options defaults, then click CREATE KEY.

    

    Example API Key Name: SEC-NET-CL26-XX
    

    

    

    Copy and Save the resulting key and secret using NotePad ++ on Jumphost1. We will be using this API key next for the integration with SD-WAN Manager. 
    

    

    

    

    The last piece of information that we need is your Secure Access Org ID. To obtain this while still in Secure Access from the previous step, use the following details in the browser url below to record your Org ID alongside your API key info. If you have any questions, please engage the Proctor. 
    

    The Organization ID is the numeric identifier found in the URL of your Secure Access dashboard. For example, in the URL https://security.cisco.com/secure-access/org/8381266/overview, the Organization ID is 8381266. You can locate this number in your Secure Access dashboard URL that follows the /org/[OrganizationID]/ pattern. 
    

    🛑NOTE: THE ABOVE IS AN EXAMPLE THIS IS NOT YOUR Org ID, unless you're lucky please check 😃
    

    

    

    Next, log back into Catalyst SD-WAN Manager, and navigate to Administration -> Settings -> Cloud Credentials. 
    

    Enable Cisco SSE, paste in your Secure Access Organization ID, API key, secret, and enable Context Sharing. Then click Save.

    

    

    

    Create SSE Policy 
    

    First, navigate to Configuration -> Policy Groups -> Secure Internet Gateway/Secure Service Edge and click Add Secure Internet Access to configure the SIA connectivity policy.

    

    

    In the Secure Internet Access pop-up, enter Pseudoco-SIA for the Name and then in the Solution drop-down, select sdwan.
    

    

    Next, under SSE Provider, select Cisco SSE; under Context Sharing enable VPN and SGT for context sharing for your SSE policy. Also, under Tracker enter a source IP Address of: 10.1.1.105. Under Configuration, click +Add Tunnel to start the configuration of the First/Primary Tunnel.
    

    



    Configure the Primary Tunnel with the following configuration, then click Add.  Note that we're adding a "1" to the end of the default ipsec name.
    

    Interface Name: ipsec1
    

    Tunnel Source Interface:  TwoGigabitEthernet0/0/6
    

    Select: Primary
    

    Track this interface: Global/Disabled
    

    Click Add.
    

    

    

    Take note of your Primary Tunnel and then click +Add Tunnel to configure the Secondary Tunnel.
    

    

    Again, configure the Secondary Tunnel with the following configuration and then click Add.
    

    Interface Name: ipsec2
    

    Tunnel Source Interface:  TwoGigabitEthernet0/0/6
    

    Select: Secondary
    

    Track this interface: Global/Disabled
    

    Click Add.
    

    

    Back on the main configuration page, take note of your (2) Tunnels which are now configured. Next, we will configure these Tunnels into a High Availability interface Pair. Click + Add Interface Pair.
    

    

    Configure the Interface Pair by first selecting (ipsec1) as the Active Interface and (ipsec2) and the Backup Interface and then click Add.
    

    

    Finally, observe the configurations below and click Add.  
    

    

    We have now successfully completed all the necessary configuration for the automatic SSE Tunnel creation. In the next task, we will add this Secure Internet Access config to our main Pseudoco-Branch-Policy. Click Save
    

    

    Deploy the Policy Group
    

    First, navigate to Configuration -> Policy Groups. Next, expand our Pseudoco-Branch-Policy. In the Secure Service Edge section under Secure Internet Access, click the drop-down and select our newly created Pseudoco-SIA Policy. 
    

    

    Review and then click Save.
    

    

    To start the deployment of the Policy Group to our Branch Secure router, we first need to associate the router to this Policy Group. Start by first expanding the Pseudoco-Branch-Policy again and then by clicking the + Add next to Associated.  
    

    

    Next, click on Associate Devices to start the device association.
    

    

    Click Next after reviewing the Process Overview.
    

    

    Next, select our SITE_105 and notice that it will automatically select our Secure Router which has been provisioned to our branch site. Once verified, click Next.
    

    

    

    Review the summary and click Associate Devices.
    

    

    When asked if "you want to provision devices in Pseudoco-Branch-Policy", click Provision Devices.
    

    

    Finally, start the deployment of the Policy Group by again selecting our SITE_105 and our Branch Router. Then click Next.
    

    

    Click Next, as there are no variables to address.
    

    

    Review the summary and then complete the process by clicking Deploy.
    

    

    🎉Congratulations !! This completes the deployment. Catalyst SD-WAN Manager is now working in conjunction with Secure Access to automate and fully configure and deploy our Tunnels to Secure Access. It is also sharing VPN and SGT Context with Secure Access for identity policy enforcement. You may click View Deployment Status to monitor the deployment. 

    

    🛑 WAIT UP TO 15 MIN FOR THE DEPLOYMENT IN SECURE ACCESS 🛑
    

    👀 You may continue with the Lab and not wait for the deployment of the tunnels. You may come back later of the next section and run the Verification steps below.
    

    Verify Deployment
    

    First, within SD-WAN Manger, navigate to Monitor -> Tunnels and then click on SIG/SSE Tunnels. Observe that you have (2) Tunnels created and one is active and the other is backup. (Remember to wait 15 min for the Tunnels to be fully configured and up).
    

    

     
    

    Device Monitoring CLI
    

     Let's investigate the Branch Router. Log into your Secure Router from Jumphost1 using the mRemote app and run the command "show sse all". You should be able to see the Cisco Secure Access Service Tunnel that was created and you will be able to see "context sharing" for both VPN and SGT is enabled.
    

    Additionally, you can execute the command "show ip route vrf 10" / "show ip route vrf 101" and you should see your default route pointing to the SSE Tunnel as shown below. Please note that the IOT VRF is not configured with a service route to SSE and will be using DIA. 
    

     
    

    Device Monitoring Cisco Secure Access Dashboard
    

    Finally, lets view our Network Connections in Secure Access. In Secure Access, navigate to Connect -> Network Connections. Then click on Network Tunnel Groups. You will see your Catalyst SDWAN Tunnel Group as Connected with both Primary and Secondary Hub Data Centers. 
    

    

    

    The automated SSE integration between Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN and Cisco Secure Access demonstrates how modern enterprises can rapidly extend secure, identity-aware connectivity from the branch to the cloud without manual tunnel provisioning. By leveraging API-driven automation, SD-WAN dynamically establishes tunnels to the nearest Secure Access Points of Presence while sharing rich context such as user identity, device posture, and segmentation information. This Cisco-native integration is a key differentiator, enabling seamless policy alignment between networking and security platforms, consistent enforcement across all locations, and rapid scalability to hundreds of sites with minimal operational overhead. The result is a simplified deployment model that accelerates Zero Trust adoption while improving performance, visibility, and security consistency.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    


    

    Auto-tunnels for the win! 🤖✨ You just let SD-WAN do all the heavy lifting—spinning up secure connectivity faster than you can say “Zero Trust.” 🚀 Sit back (just for a second), admire the automation magic, and let’s keep this momentum rolling into the next section!
    

    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    3. Connect HQ-FTD Firewall to Secure Access
    
  
    
  
    Connect HQ-FTD Firewall to Secure Access 
    

    Now that we've enabled SASE via the SD-WAN integration with Secure Access, let's configure the tunnel from Secure Access down to the HQ Data Center FTD firewall to gain access to PseudoCo's private resources.
    

    Fortunately for you, we've preconfigured most of the FTD tunnel, but the Secure Access configuration was just too exciting to leave out!
    

    

    Picking up where we left off in the Network Tunnel Groups tab in Secure Access, click + Add on the right-hand side.
    

    

    

    This will take us to the Add a Network Tunnel Group workflow, which allows us to create a Secure Access tunnel in just a few simple steps.
    

    First, configure these parameters on the General Settings page.
    

      

    • Tunnel Group Name: HQ-FTD
      • 

    • Region: US (Virginia)
      • 

    • Device Type: FTD
      • 

    

    

    

    Click Next.
    

    

    On the Tunnel ID and Passphrase page, configure these paramaters.
    

      

    • Tunnel ID Format: Email (this is the default)
      • 

    • Tunnel ID: hq-secure-access-tunnel
      • 

    • Passphrase: C1scoSecureNetworking
      • 

    • Confirm Passphrase: C1scoSecureNetworking
      • 

    

    

    Click Next.
    

    

    On the Routing page, we are simply enabling Dynamic routing for BGP AS Number 65532.  This is the peer BGP ASN that Secure Access will be using to peer with the HQ FTD firewall across the tunnel to share routes.
    

    

    Click Save.
    

    

    This last page summarizes the relevant configurations used to build the tunnel from the HQ FTD firewall.  Since we've prebuilt the tunnels on FTD, we will use this information to update the Tunnel ID and Passphrase on the firewall configuration.  The Tunnel ID and Passphrase are used to securely authenticate the tunnels between the firewall and Secure Access.
    

    🛑 Note: Keep this page up for now until we copy these parameters over to the firewall.
    

    

    Open a new browser tab and navigate back to Security Cloud Control (SCC) to jump into the firewall configuration.
    

    Once on the main SCC launchpad, select Firewall on the left side.
    

    

    Let's check out the current status of the FTD firewall tunnels by selecting Security Devices on the left side.
    

    

    Next, select the hqftd firewall, which will open a pane on the right side.  Scroll down and select Interfaces under the Device Management section.
    

    

    From the Interfaces view, we can see that both tunnels to Secure Access are down, which is expected since we haven't updated the authentication parameters yet.  Let's get these tunnels up!
    

    

    On the left menu, select Secure Connections -> Site-to-Site VPN & SD-WAN.  This will take you to the VPN configuration section.
    

    

    

    First, expand the SecureAccess topology name to show additional details about the tunnels.  Next, select the pencil icon to edit the tunnels.
    

    

    This brings you to the Edit VPN Topology menu.
    

    

    🛑 Note: Remember that Secure Access browser tab with the Data for Tunnel Setup parameters?  You will need to flip back to this tab to verify and copy tunnel parameters to the FTD firewall.
    

    For reference, here's what that page looks like.
    

    

    

    From the Edit VPN Topology page, first verify that the Endpoint IP Addresses match the Secure Access Primary Data Center IP Address and Secondary Data Center IP Address (from the Secure Access Data for Tunnel Setup screen in the previous browser tab).
    

    🛑 IMPORTANT #1: The "Extranet" device could be located under Node A or Node B.  This also applies to the "hqftdv" device, which could be located under Node A or NodeB.  IT DOES NOT MATTER which Node they are under, just configure them accordingly per the image below.
    

    🛑 IMPORTANT #2: As a load-balancing mechanism, Secure Access will flip the 35.171.214.188 and 44.217.195.188 Endpoint IP Addresses (VPN Peers) between Primary and Secondary Hubs.  As a result, this makes 35.171.214.188 primary for some pods and secondary for other pods.  Please ensure that your Secure Access Primary Hub IP Address is listed first in the Endpoint IP Address field for the firewall, and your Secondary Hub IP Address is listed second.
    

    Next, copy the Primary Tunnel ID from the Secure Access browser tab and paste it into the Local Identity Configuration field.
    

    

    

    Scroll down to the Backup VTI section.  Copy the Secondary Tunnel ID from the Secure Access browser tab and paste it into the Local Identity Configuration field.
    

    

    

    Finally, click the IKE tab and scroll to the bottom.  Enter C1scoSecureNetworking into both the Key:* and Confirm Key:* fields.
    

    

    

    Click Save.
    

    

    Before deploying these VPN tunnel changes, we need to verify that the BGP ASN provisioned by Secure Access is configured correctly on the firewall.
    

    

    Navigate back to the Secure Access browser tab that contains the VPN parameters and select Done.
    

    

    

    This will take you back to the Network Tunnel Groups homepage. Click HQ-FTD to view the tunnel details.
    

    

    

    This will present you with the BGP parameters that were provisioned in Secure Access at the top of the page.  Take note of your Peer (Secure Access) BGP AS, as you will need to copy this into the firewall configuration in order to BGP peer with Secure Access to exchange routes.
    

    

    

    Now that we know the BGP ASN provisioned by Secure Access, let's go verify it's correct in the firewall.  From cdFMC, navigate to Devices -> Device Management.
    

    

    

    Click the hqftdv firewall.  Don't worry if you see a critical warning - this is because the firewall isn't receiving traffic on one of its interfaces.
    

    

    

    On the next page, you will see configurations specific to this firewall.  Navigate to the Routing tab at the top, then IPv4 on the left column, then the Neighbor tab, and finally review the BGP Remote AS Number for both BGP peers.
    

    

    

    If these match what is displayed in Secure Access, there is no need to change them.  If they don't match, click the pencil to the right, update the Remote AS* to match Secure Access, and click Save. Make sure to do this for BOTH BGP Peers.
    

    

    

    Now it's time to deploy these configuration changes to the firewall. First click Save to save your changes.
    

    Then select Deploy in the top banner, check the box next to hqftdv, then click Deploy.  This config deployment will take just a couple minutes to complete.
    

    

    

    You should see that the deployment Completed successfully.
    

    

    

    Navigate back to Secure Connections -> Site-to-Site VPN & SD-WAN to verify the tunnel status. These tunnels may take a couple of minutes to come up.
    

    Congratulations, your tunnels should now be up!!
    

    

    

    

    Now back in Secure Access, from the Connect -> Network Connections -> Network Tunnel Groups page, you can see that we have two tunnels up to Secure Access, one from the SD-WAN Branch Router and one from the FTD firewall at the HQ Data Center.
    

    

    

    Finally, let's quickly take a look at the BGP route status.  Click the HQ-FTD link and then scroll down to the bottom and click the Primary 1 tunnel link.
    

    

    

    

    In the slider window that pulls out, scroll down to the Routing section and notice the BGP routing type and that we're receiving a route from the FTD firewall for 198.18.5.0/24, which is the prefix used to reach HQ Data Center private resources.
    

    

    Click Close to exit this window.  You have completed the HQ FTD tunnel configuration to Secure Access!
    

    

    The HQ-FTD integration using Network Tunnel Groups (NTGs) highlights a flexible approach for securely connecting on-premises data center environments to the Secure Access cloud when automation is not available or when more granular control is required. By manually establishing tunnels from the Cisco Secure Firewall (FTD), organizations can extend Secure Access policy enforcement directly into their internal application environment, enabling use cases such as VPN-as-a-Service, private application access, and secure internet inspection. This model showcases Cisco’s strength in unifying cloud-delivered security with traditional firewall infrastructure, providing deep control, interoperability, and consistent policy enforcement across hybrid environments, while allowing organizations to incrementally adopt Zero Trust without disrupting existing architectures.
    

    

    🧭 Complete
    


    

    Manual tunnels, maximum control! 🔐💪 You’ve locked in secure connectivity from HQ like a true network craftsman. Now that your firewall is officially part of the Zero Trust squad, let’s keep building—onward to the next challenge!
    

    

    

    

    

    
  
    Connect DUO IDP
    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    Section Overview – Integrating Identity: Connecting Duo, Active Directory, and Secure Access
    

    With the infrastructure now securely connected to the cloud security edge, PseudoCo is ready to enable the most important component of its Zero Trust architecture: identity-driven access control.
    

    PseudoCo’s leadership understands that modern security cannot rely solely on network location or IP address. In today’s distributed workforce, users may connect from corporate offices, branch locations, home networks, or mobile devices. As a result, the organization must make access decisions based on who the user is, the device they are using, and the context of the connection.
    

    To accomplish this, PseudoCo has standardized on Cisco Duo as its Identity Provider (IdP), integrated with the company’s existing Microsoft Active Directory environment.
    

    This integration will allow PseudoCo to bring together enterprise identity, strong authentication, and cloud access policy enforcement through Cisco Secure Access.
    

    

    The Role of Identity in PseudoCo’s Zero Trust Architecture
    

    Up to this point in the lab, you have built the network foundation of PseudoCo’s secure architecture:
    

      

    • 

      Campus segmentation using Virtual Networks and SGTs
      

      • 

    • 

      Context sharing across infrastructure and security platforms
      

      • 

    • 

      Secure infrastructure connectivity to the Secure Access cloud
      

      • 

    

    Now, PseudoCo must ensure that every access request is tied to a verified identity.
    

    This is where the identity platform becomes the decision engine for Zero Trust access.
    

    By integrating Duo with Active Directory and Secure Access, PseudoCo enables:
    

      

    • 

      Strong multi-factor authentication for all application access
      

      • 

    • 

      Centralized user identity management
      

      • 

    • 

      Dynamic access policy based on user and device context
      

      • 

    • 

      Secure application access without traditional VPN dependencies
      

      • 

    

    

    Identity Architecture in the PseudoCo Environment
    

    In PseudoCo’s design, Active Directory remains the authoritative identity store, containing:
    

      

    • 

      User accounts
      

      • 

    • 

      Group membership
      

      • 

    • 

      Organizational roles
      

      • 

    • 

      Enterprise identity attributes
      

      • 

    

    However, authentication and identity verification are handled by Duo, which adds modern security capabilities such as:
    

      

    • 

      Multi-factor authentication
      

      • 

    • 

      Device trust verification
      

      • 

    • 

      User risk evaluation
      

      • 

    • 

      Conditional access enforcement
      

      • 

    

    Duo becomes the Identity Provider for Secure Access, allowing Secure Access to delegate authentication decisions to a trusted identity authority.
    

    

    Establishing Identity Federation with Secure Access
    

    To enable secure application access, Secure Access must trust Duo as its Identity Provider. This trust relationship is established using Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML).
    

    SAML enables Secure Access to:
    

      

    • 

      Redirect users to Duo for authentication
      

      • 

    • 

      Validate successful authentication events
      

      • 

    • 

      Receive identity attributes and group membership information
      

      • 

    • 

      Apply access policies based on that identity context
      

      • 

    

    This process allows PseudoCo to enforce identity verification before a user is granted access to private applications.
    

    

    Automating Identity Provisioning with SCIM
    

    In addition to authentication, PseudoCo must ensure that user identities and group memberships remain synchronized across systems.
    

    To accomplish this, the organization uses System for Cross-domain Identity Management (SCIM) to automatically provision users and groups between Duo and Secure Access.
    

    SCIM enables:
    

      

    • 

      Automatic user provisioning and lifecycle management
      

      • 

    • 

      Synchronization of group membership and attributes
      

      • 

    • 

      Reduced administrative overhead
      

      • 

    • 

      Consistent identity policy enforcement across platforms
      

      • 

    

    This automation ensures that when employees join, move within, or leave the organization, access policies update automatically across the security architecture.
    

    

    Why This Matters to PseudoCo
    

    PseudoCo previously relied on a combination of VPN authentication and network-level access controls. This approach created several operational and security challenges:
    

      

    • 

      Users often had broad network access after VPN login
      

      • 

    • 

      Authentication policies varied between applications
      

      • 

    • 

      User provisioning across systems required manual configuration
      

      • 

    • 

      Access policies were difficult to audit and maintain
      

      • 

    

    By integrating Duo, Active Directory, and Secure Access, PseudoCo achieves a modern identity-first security model.
    

    Now, access decisions are based on:
    

      

    • 

      Verified user identity
      

      • 

    • 

      Device trust and security posture
      

      • 

    • 

      User group membership and role
      

      • 

    • 

      Organizational policy
      

      • 

    

    This approach ensures that authentication, authorization, and access control operate as a unified system across the enterprise.
    

    

    What You Will Achieve in This Section
    

    In this section of the lab, you will:
    

      

    • 

      Integrate Duo with PseudoCo’s Active Directory environment
      

      • 

    • 

      Configure Duo as the Identity Provider for Secure Access
      

      • 

    • 

      Establish SAML-based authentication between Duo and Secure Access
      

      • 

    • 

      Configure SCIM provisioning to synchronize users and groups
      

      • 

    • 

      Validate identity federation and access workflows
      

      • 

    

    These steps enable Secure Access to enforce identity-driven application access policies, a critical component of PseudoCo’s Zero Trust architecture.
    

    

    What Comes Next
    

    Once identity integration is complete, PseudoCo will be ready to define application access policies that determine:
    

      

    • 

      Which users can access specific private applications
      

      • 

    • 

      What device posture requirements must be met
      

      • 

    • 

      When additional authentication controls are required
      

      • 

    • 

      How access should be monitored and logged
      

      • 

    

    This brings the organization one step closer to delivering secure, seamless access to applications from anywhere—without relying on traditional VPN connectivity.
    

    
  
    📄
    
    2. DUO IDP Integration
    
  
    
  
    IDP Integration
    

    

    

    In this first section, we will be configuring Active Directory to sync Users and Groups with Duo. These will be used later in policy configuration. The steps in this section will be completed on AD1 and Duo.
    

    

    Starting from Jumphost1, open your Chrome browser and click the Dashboard bookmark favorite.  Locate the Duo app and click Login. Login using your suggested email and password.   
    

    When prompted, finish authenticating using your mobile app pin code.
    

    

    

    Once logged into the Duo dashboard, from the left-hand navigation, click Users -> External Directories.
    

    

    

    Click Add External Directory -> Active Directory.
    

    

    

    Select Add new connection and click Continue.
    

    

    

    Since the Duo Auth Proxy is already downloaded and installed on AD1, go to Step 2 of Configuration metadata and click download a pre-configured file.  
    

    You may be prompted to verify the download in the Chrome browser. If so, click Download unverified file.
    


    🛑 Note: To learn more about the Duo Auth Proxy and how it can be used to enable multi-factor authentication with various authentication protocols, including Radius and LDAP, please see:  https://duo.com/docs/authproxy-reference
    

    

    From Jumphost1, login to AD1 from mRemoteNG. 
    

    

    From AD1, click the Start Menu, select Duo Security, then select Duo Authentication Proxy Manager.
    

    

    

    In the Duo Authentication Proxy Manager, there will be two panes.  In the left pane, select all and delete the [sso] and [cloud] configuration, if they exist.
    

    

    

    Navigate to the Downloads folder on Jumphost1 and open authproxy.cfg in Notepad.
    

    Select the entire [cloud] configuration and copy it.
    

    

    

    Next, navigate back to the Duo Auth Proxy Manager configuration and paste the [cloud] text from authproxy.cfg (right-click doesn’t work, to paste simply press ctrl+v).
    

    

    Click Validate, then in the output pane it should state “There are no configuration problems” and “No issues detected”.
    

    🛑 Note: it may mention that the Duo Auth Proxy has updates available. This can be ignored.
    

    Finally, be sure to click Save and then Start Service.
    

    

    

    The Authentication Proxy is now running!
    

    

    

    Back in the Duo Admin Console, scroll down to the bottom of the page and click Test connection.  The status in the Duo dashboard will show Not connected, but will now have a blue check mark next to Add Authentication Proxy.
    

    

    

    Scroll back up to the Directory Configuration section and enter the following Domain controller(s) values.
    

      

    • Hostname or IP address (1): 198.18.5.102
      • 

    • Port (1): 389
      • 

    • Base DN: DC=corp,DC=pseudoco,DC=com
      • 

    

    Click Save in the lower left hand corner.
    

    

    

    The Status will change to Connected to Duo.
    

    

    Under Groups, add IoT, MAIN and PROD.  This will pull users contained within these Active Directory Groups into Duo.
    

    

    

    Under Synced Attributes, click the Add Attribute button to add the following: 
    

      

    • First Name: givenname
      • 

    • Last Name: sn
      • 

    

    

    Click Complete Setup on the right side of the screen.
    

    

    

    You should now see Directory sync setup completed successfully at the top of the screen.
    

    Click Sync Now.
    

    

    

    You will now see users and groups imported from Active Directory into Duo.
    

    

    

    Users and Groups will begin to populate under Users -> Users. Make note of the e-mail domain here as you will need this later.
    

    

    

    Next, configure the Duo Authproxy for SSO Authentication by navigating to Applications -> SSO Settings.
    

    

    

    Select the External Authentication Sources tab.
    

    

    

    On the next page, click + Add Source in the upper right hand corner, then click + Add Active Directory.
    

    

    

    Accept the Privacy Statement and click Configure Active Directory.
    

    

    

    On the next page, click + Add Authentication Proxy.
    

    Scroll down to step 1.2 Add SSO section and service account credentials to the configuration file.
    

    Select Copy.
    

    

    

    Navigate back to the Duo Auth Proxy Manager configuration on AD1, then paste in what you copied below the [cloud] configuration from the previous steps.
    

    Click Validate and verify that validation was successful.
    

    

    

    Next click Save, and finally click Restart Service in the upper right hand corner.
    

    

    

    Back in the Duo Admin Console, scroll down to step 2. Connect the Authentication Proxy to Duo, click Generate Command and then click Copy.  We will run this on AD1 to trigger the Duo Auth Proxy to register with Duo.
    

    

    

    Back on AD1, open a Command Prompt by clicking the shortcut in the taskbar.
    

    Right-click in the command prompt window to paste the Authentication Proxy Enrollment Code. Hit Enter.
    

    

    

    Back in the Duo Admin Console under step 3. Verify the proxy is connected, click Run test.
    

    Verify that the Status updates to reflect Connected to Duo.
    

    

    

    Scroll to the top of the page and return to the configuration page by clicking External Authentication Sources.
    

    

    Click Active Directory.
    

    

    

    Scroll down to the Active Directory server configuration section and enter the following values.
    

    

      

    • Hostname or IP address (1): 198.18.5.102
      • 

    • Port (1): 389
      • 

    • Base DN(s): DC=corp,DC=pseudoco,DC=com
      • 

    

    

    Click Save and enable.
    

    

    

    Return to Applications -> SSO Settings.
    

    

    Scroll down to Permitted Domains at the bottom of the page and click + Add email domain.
    

    

    

    Enter the email domain corp.pseudoco.com and click Add.
    

    

    

    You will now see this email domain listed at the bottom of the page.
    

    

    

    Finally, Active Directory needs to be set as the primary authentication source. Scroll to the top of the Single Sign-On page and click the Routing Rules tab.
    

    

    

    In the Default rule section, select Active Directory in the Use this authentication source drop-down and click Save.
    

    

    

    Navigate back to the External Authentication Sources tab and select Active Directory.
    

    

    

    Verify that Active Directory is Enabled at the top of the page.  If not, select Enable Source on the right side.
    

    

    

    Integrating Cisco Duo as the Identity Provider establishes the foundation for identity-driven access control within PseudoCo’s Zero Trust architecture. By connecting Duo with Active Directory, PseudoCo combines authoritative identity data with modern authentication capabilities such as multi-factor authentication, device trust, and risk-based access evaluation. This is a key Cisco differentiator—bringing strong, cloud-delivered identity security into the access workflow while seamlessly integrating with existing enterprise identity stores. The result is a centralized, secure authentication model that ensures every access request is verified, contextual, and aligned with organizational policy.
    

    

    

    

    🧭 Next up
    


    

    Identity = unlocked! 🔓🧠 You’ve just put Duo in charge of “who gets in”—now let’s make sure everyone’s invited correctly with automated provisioning! 📋✨ 
    

    

    

    Now that the Users and Groups are synced to Duo, it is time to sync them from Duo to Secure Access using SCIM and then configure SAML authentication between Duo and Secure Access for Proxy and ZTA!
    

    

    

    

    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    3. SCIM and SAML Integration
    
  
    
  
    SCIM and SAML Integration
    

    

    

    

    

    In order for Secure Access to authenticate users, they must be synced from Duo. Fortunately, the Duo outbound SCIM and SAML configuration are part of the same application.
    

    

    In the left-hand Duo Dashboard navigation, click Applications -> Manage -> Applications. 
    

    

    

    Click Add application. 
    

    

    

    In the application search bar type secure access. Click Add for the first option of Cisco Secure Access, this is the base integration for SCIM and SAML auth with ZTA and web. The Cisco Secure Access VPN application will be configured later when configuring the VPN profile.
    

    

    

    Select the Provisioning tab.  The Authentication Base URL and Token will be collected from Secure Access next.
    

    

    

    Now it's time to jump into Secure Access.  We will be accessing Secure Access from Security Cloud Control, which again is accessible from the Chrome browser Dashboard bookmark favorite on Jumphost1.
    

    Click View to launch SCC if it's not already open.
    

    

    

    This will take you to the Security Cloud Control (SCC) landing page.  Notice the various different security solutions available from this SCC landing page.
    

    Select Secure Access to launch into the Secure Access dashboard.
