    This is the Secure Access dashboard landing page you will be presented with next.
    

    

    

    From this Secure Access dashboard, navigate to Connect -> User, Groups, and Endpoint Devices.
    

    

     
    

    Click Configuration management in the top right-hand corner.
    

    

     
    

    In the Directories section, click Integrate directories.
    

    

    

    Secure Access supports many different provisioning methods including SCIM and Active Directory LDAP. The Provisioning Method that Duo uses is SCIM. Select Identity provider (IdP) to configure this now.
    

    The IdP directory name will be Duo and the Identity Provider is Duo. Click Next.
    

     
    

    

    In the Start Provisioning section, click Generate Token.
    

    

    

    You will now be presented with both a Token and Provisioning URL.  These will be used to connect Duo to Secure Access.
    

    Open Notepad from the jumphost, then click Copy token and Copy URL to copy each of these into Notepad.  You will be pasting these into Duo in the next step.
    

    Finally, click Done.
    

    

    

    Navigate back to Duo.  Copy and paste the Base URL (Base URL is the same as the Provisioning URL) and Token from the Secure Access SCIM integration into the Authentication section in the Duo Admin Portal.
    

    Click Connect to application.
    

    

    Duo is now connected to Secure Access to share Identity!
    

    

    

    Next, scroll down to the Attribute mapping section.
    

    This is the list of attributes that will get passed to Secure Access.  Click + Edit mappings to add more attributes.
    

    

    

    Select the additional optional attributes. Then click Save mapping.
    

      

    1. displayName
      2. 

    2. emails
      3. 

    3. name.familyName
      4. 

    4. name.givenName
      5. 

    

    

    

    Edit the userName Application attribute to be Email Address.
    

     
    

    Scroll down to the Groups section next.  There are 3 AD groups that will be used in the lab that will need to be mapped:
    

    

    Active Directory Groups: IoT, MAIN and PROD
    

    

     
    

    Finally, click Save and enable to complete this integration.
    

    

    Configuring SCIM and SAML integration enables seamless identity federation and lifecycle management between Duo and Secure Access. SAML establishes the trust relationship for secure authentication, allowing users to be redirected to Duo for verification, while SCIM automates the provisioning and synchronization of users and groups across platforms. Together, these standards-based integrations highlight a major Cisco advantage: simplifying identity management while ensuring real-time consistency and reduced administrative overhead. This ensures that access policies remain accurate, dynamic, and aligned with user roles as they change within the organization.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    


    

    No more manual user juggling! 🤹‍♂️❌ Your identities are now synced and flowing—next up, let’s put that authentication power to work for real app access! 🚀. While the users and groups sync, it is time to configure the SAML integration in the application.
    

    

    

    

    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    4. SAML Integration for Proxy and ZTA
    
  
    
  
    SAML Integration for Proxy and ZTA
    

    

    

    

    

    In this next step, Duo SAML authentication will be configured in Secure Access. The steps in this section will be completed on Jumphost1, Duo and Secure Access.
    

    Starting in Duo, scroll to the top of the the Cisco Secure Access – Single Sign-On page from the last section and click the Single Sign-On tab.
    

    

    

    Return to the Secure Access dashboard.
    

    Navigate to Connect -> Users, Groups, and Endpoint Devices.
    

     
    

    

    The users and groups may not be synced under the Users and Groups and Organizational Units tabs yet.  If they are, it will look similar to below.
    

    Click Configuration management.
    

    

    

    This next step is to integrate with a SAML provider for authentication of our users. This SAML integration will provide authentication for Client-Based ZTA, Clientless ZTA and Secure Web Gateway users.
    

    🛑Note: Secure Access supports any SAMLv2 or OIDC provider for authentication. Workflows for Duo and many other providers are available. For this lab, Duo is the chosen SAML provider.
    

    

    In the SSO authentication section of the Configuration Management page, click Configure.
    

     
    

    

    This will bring you to the SSO Authentication configuration workflow.
    

    Enter DuoSSO as the SSO Authentication Name, choose SAML for the Authentication Method, and Duo for the User Directory.
    

    Click Next.
    

    

    

    In the IdP Authentication section, select Duo as the Provider.  The Entity ID section can be left at default.
    

    Click Next.
    

    

    Deep Dive: If Organization-specific Entity ID is enabled, then the SAML integration would be assigned an Entity ID that is unique to this Secure Access org. This would allow an administrator to integrate Duo with multiple Secure Access orgs. Since every pod has a unique Duo Org, it is unnecessary to use Organization-specific Entity ID, so we will leave this option Disabled.
    

    

    On the final configuration page, there are two options provided for linking Secure Access to the SAML provider using either SAML Metadata XML Configuration, which involves three steps and is largely preconfigured when using Duo, or Manual Configuration, where administrators can type in the integration values.
    

    

    Click Download Service Provider XML file - this will be used in the Duo App configuration. 
    

    🛑 Note: If the button opens the file in a new browser tab, you can close that and right click the Download Service Provider XML file button, then use the Save link as option to save the file to the Downloads folder.
    

    You may be prompted to verify the download. If so, click Download unverified file.
    

     
    

    

    Now it is time to go back to the Duo Admin Portal. From the Single Sign-On tab on the Cisco Secure Access - Single Sign-On page, leave the default Application name of Cisco Secure Access - Single Sign-On.
    

    In the User access section, click the radio button to Enable for all users.
    

    

    

    Scroll down to the Service Provider section and click Choose File.  Navigate to the Downloads folder and open Cisco_SSE_SP_Metadata.xml.
    

    

    

    Scroll down to the bottom of the configuration and click Save.
    

    

    Once saved, scroll down to the Downloads section and click Download XML.
    

    

    🛑 Note: If the button opens the file in a new browser tab, you can close that and right click the Download XML button, then use the Save As option to save the file.
    

    You may be prompted to verify the download. If so, click Download unverified file.
    

    

    Back in the Secure Access dashboard, continue the Configure SSO Authentication flow by navigating to step 3. Upload your IdP Security Metadata XML file.
    

    Click on the Upload area and select the Cisco Secure Access - Single Sign-On - IDP Metadata.xml file that you downloaded from Duo.
    

    Click Done.
    

    

     
    

    The final step in the Duo Admin Portal is to make testing easier in the lab environment. By default, MFA is required for all users authenticating through Duo.  To turn off this requirement, click on Policies -> Policies in the left-hand navigation
    

     
    

    

    Click the Actions menu on the right for the Global Policy, then click Edit.
    

    DO NOT accidentally edit the default policy under the Global Policy.
    

      
    

    Under New user policy, select Allow access without MFA. 
    

    

    

    Select the Authentication policy section and select Skip MFA.
    

    

    Click Save.
    

    

    This concludes the SAML configuration - it's time to test authentication!
    

    Return to the Secure Access Configuration Management page. Under SSO Authentication, expand DuoSSO and click Test Configuration.
    

    

    Duo will prompt for a username and password from the web browser next.  Use the credentials below to test authentication.  Enter the credentials from your lab printout - it will be a format similar to below depending on where your pod is located and which Pod # it is.
    

    RTP Username: kit@rtp.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    SJC Username: kit@sjc.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    Password: C1sco12345
    

    🛑 Note: If you get a prompt to Update Chrome, click the Skip for now option.
    

    

    After the Duo Authentication, a successful configuration test page should load.
    

    

    Close the SSO Authentication browser tab.
    

    

    Navigate back to Users, Groups and Endpoint Devices by clicking Users and Groups.
    

    

    

    By now the Users and Groups should be fully synced.  You should expect to see 8 Users and 3 Groups and Organizational Units.
    

    

    This completes the Active Directory and Duo integration with Secure Access. 
    

    

    Extending SAML integration to Secure Access Proxy and Zero Trust Access (ZTA) enables identity-driven application access without reliance on traditional VPNs. With Duo acting as the trusted Identity Provider, Secure Access can enforce authentication, evaluate context, and grant application-specific access based on user identity, device posture, and policy. This showcases Cisco’s strength in delivering a unified, cloud-based Zero Trust framework where identity becomes the primary control point. The result is secure, seamless access to applications from anywhere, with consistent policy enforcement and enhanced user experience.
    

    

    🧭 Complete
    
    

    VPNs? That’s old news. 📼❌ You’re now delivering app access the Zero Trust way—secure, seamless, and seriously cool. 😎🔥 Let’s keep building! Now that tunnels have been built, and users have a way to login, it is time to configure Secure Internet Access (SIA) and Secure Private Access (SPA)!
    

    

    

    

    

    

    
  
    Secure Private Access (SPA) Configuration
    

    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    Section Overview – Enabling Secure Private Application Access
    

    Historically, organizations relied on traditional VPN technologies to allow remote users to connect to internal networks. While VPNs provide encrypted connectivity, they often grant users broad network access once connected, which increases the risk of lateral movement if a device or account is compromised.
    

    PseudoCo’s security team is modernizing this model by implementing identity-driven private access workflows using Cisco Secure Access. Rather than providing full network access by default, Secure Access allows PseudoCo to grant precise, application-level access based on identity, device trust, and policy.
    

    This approach enables multiple access methods that support different user needs while maintaining consistent security enforcement.
    

    

    Review User Personas in the PseudoCo Environment
    

    Throughout this section of the lab, several user personas will be used to simulate real-world enterprise access scenarios. These personas may appear again in later sections of the lab as additional security capabilities are introduced.
    

    Kit – Production Employee
    

    Kit represents a full-time production employee at PseudoCo. As a trusted internal user, Kit has access to business-critical applications and internal resources.
    

    However, even with elevated application access, Kit must still comply with corporate internet usage policies and data protection controls.
    

    Pat – Production Employee
    

    Pat also represents a production employee within PseudoCo’s corporate environment. Like Kit, Pat has access to internal applications but remains subject to web security policies designed to reduce risk from malicious or inappropriate internet content.
    

    Nik – Corporate  Employee
    

    Nik represents a standard corporate user within PseudoCo’s Main user segment. Unlike production users, Nik has limited and controlled access to sensitive production applications and resources.
    

    Nik remains subject to strict web security and segmentation policies designed to reduce risk from unauthorized access and inappropriate internet usage. His role demonstrates how identity-based policy and segmentation enforce business-intent-driven access controls, ensuring users only access what is necessary for their role.
    

    Lee – Contractor
    

    Lee represents an external contractor who requires limited access to PseudoCo resources. Contractors often work alongside internal teams but must be restricted to only the applications and resources required for their role.
    

    Lee’s access will demonstrate how identity-based policies can enforce stricter controls for third-party users.
    

    IoTUser – Non-802.1X Device
    

    IoTUser represents a device that cannot perform standard user authentication, such as cameras, sensors, or operational equipment. These devices typically authenticate using MAC Authentication Bypass (MAB) and are assigned segmentation policies that restrict their behavior.
    

    This scenario illustrates how PseudoCo applies security policy to devices that cannot participate in traditional identity workflows.
    

    

    The Private Access Strategy at PseudoCo
    

    PseudoCo’s architecture supports several secure private access models that can be applied depending on the use case:
    

      

    • 

      Browser-based access for contractors or unmanaged devices
      

      • 

    • 

      VPN-as-a-Service (VPNaaS) for users requiring broader network access
      

      • 

    • 

      Client-based Zero Trust Network Access (ZTNA) for identity-driven application connectivity
      

      • 

    • 

      Hybrid private access that allows direct connectivity to internal infrastructure when appropriate
      

      • 

    

    All of these access methods are integrated with the identity platform configured earlier in the lab using Cisco Duo and Microsoft Active Directory, ensuring that authentication, authorization, and policy enforcement remain consistent.
    

    This flexibility allows PseudoCo to provide the right level of access for each user type without sacrificing security.
    

    

    Private Access Use Cases
    

    In this section of the lab, you will validate several real-world access scenarios using the user personas introduced earlier.
    

    Each scenario demonstrates how Secure Access can enforce granular, identity-driven access policies for different users and applications.
    

    

    Use Case 1 – Browser-Based Contractor Access
    

    The first scenario focuses on providing limited access for external users.
    

    Lee, a contractor working with PseudoCo, needs access to a dedicated contractor workstation located in the company’s data center environment. However, because Lee is using an unmanaged device, PseudoCo does not want to require a full VPN client or grant network-level access.
    

    Using Secure Access browser-based private access, Lee can authenticate through the identity provider and launch a secure session directly from a web browser.
    

    Once authenticated, Lee will be granted access to a remote desktop session hosted on the contractor workstation in the data center. The connection is proxied through Secure Access, ensuring that:
    

      

    • 

      Lee can reach only the approved system
      

      • 

    • 

      No direct network access is granted
      

      • 

    • 

      The session remains fully monitored and controlled
      

      • 

    

    This model allows PseudoCo to securely enable contractor productivity without exposing the internal network.
    

    

    Use Case 2 – VPN-as-a-Service for Remote Employees
    

    While Zero Trust application access is preferred, some employees still require broader connectivity for certain workflows such as administrative tasks, troubleshooting, or legacy application access.
    

    For these scenarios, PseudoCo provides VPN-as-a-Service (VPNaaS) through Secure Access.
    

    In this test case, Kit will connect from a remote workstation and establish a secure VPN connection through Secure Access.
    

    Once authenticated and authorized, Kit will gain access to internal network resources including:
    

      

    • 

      Internal network reachability using ICMP (ping)
      

      • 

    • 

      Access to the corporate intranet hosted within the data center
      

      • 

    

    This demonstrates how Secure Access can provide modernized VPN connectivity without relying on traditional VPN concentrator infrastructure.
    

    

    Use Case 3 – Client-Based Zero Trust Network Access
    

    PseudoCo’s long-term goal is to minimize reliance on full network-level VPN connectivity by shifting toward application-level Zero Trust access.
    

    In this scenario, Kit will enroll a device using the Secure Access client and establish a client-based Zero Trust Network Access (ZTNA) connection.
    

    Once enrolled and authenticated, the Secure Access client will automatically establish secure connectivity to authorized applications without granting access to the entire internal network.
    

    Kit will then access the intranet application again, but this time the connection will occur through ZTNA application access rather than traditional VPN routing.
    

    The application will be accessed through its public IP address, but Secure Access ensures that only authenticated and authorized users can reach the service.
    

    This demonstrates how PseudoCo can securely expose private applications without exposing the internal network itself.
    

    

    Use Case 4 – Hybrid Private Access (In Bonus Tasks)
    

    Finally, PseudoCo supports a hybrid access model that allows Secure Access to work alongside existing security infrastructure.
    

    In this scenario, Kit will access a private resource that is hosted behind the organization’s internal firewall infrastructure powered by Cisco Secure Firewall.
    

    Using the hybrid private access architecture, Secure Access will direct authorized traffic through the firewall infrastructure to reach the protected application.
    

    This allows PseudoCo to combine the benefits of cloud-delivered Zero Trust access with the existing security inspection and policy enforcement capabilities of its on-premises firewall environment.
    

    

    What You Will Achieve in This Section
    

    By completing these exercises, you will validate several secure private access workflows used by PseudoCo.
    

    You will:
    

      

    • 

      Provide browser-based access for contractors without requiring a VPN client
      

      • 

    • 

      Establish VPN-as-a-Service connectivity for remote employees
      

      • 

    • 

      Enroll devices for client-based Zero Trust Network Access
      

      • 

    • 

      Test hybrid access models that integrate Secure Access with on-premises firewall infrastructure
      

      • 

    

    These scenarios demonstrate how PseudoCo can provide secure, flexible access to private applications while enforcing Zero Trust principles across different user types and access methods.
    

    

    Why This Matters to PseudoCo
    

    Modern enterprises must support a variety of user types and access requirements while maintaining strong security controls.
    

    By implementing these private access workflows through Secure Access, PseudoCo gains:
    

      

    • 

      Granular application-level access control
      

      • 

    • 

      Reduced reliance on traditional VPN infrastructure
      

      • 

    • 

      Secure contractor access without exposing internal networks
      

      • 

    • 

      Flexible integration with existing security platforms
      

      • 

    

    This allows the organization to deliver secure access to applications from anywhere while maintaining full visibility and control over who can access what resources.
    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    2. Private Resources
    
  
    
  
    Configuring Private Resources
    

    

    In this section, the primary intranet website and RDP access for a contractor will be defined. The intranet website will be used to test VPN and Zero Trust Access. The Contractor RDP access will be used to test browser-based access from a non-corporate asset.
    

    Return to the Jumphost1 and launch the Secure Access dashboard from the Dashboard automation tile in the Chrome bookmarks.
    

    Before configuring Private Resources, we will configure DNS Servers to resolve private resource hostnames. Select Resources -> DNS Servers in the left hand navigation.
    

     
    

    

    Click Add DNS Servers.
    

     
    

    

    In the DNS server group name field, enter PseudoCo DNS. In the DNS server 1 field, enter 198.18.5.102.
    


    

    Click Save.
    

    

    From the left menu, navigate to Resources -> Private Resources.
    


    

    

    Click Add a Private Resource to create a new Private Resource
    

    

    Enter the following information into the fields that are displayed:
    

      

    • Private Resource Name: Production - Intranet App Server
      • 

    • Internally Reachable Address: intranet.corp.pseudoco.com
      • 

    • Protocol: TCP - (HTTP/HTTPS)
      • 

    • Port / Ranges: 80
      • 

    

    

    Scroll down to Endpoint Connection Methods.
    

    Check the Zero-trust connections checkbox.  Client-based connection and Browser-based connection will both be enabled by default. In the Client-based connections sub-section, check the box Change the remotely reachable addresses, and make sure the field below now reads:  intranet.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    In the Browser-based connection section, make sure Use a URL in a Cisco domain is selected with the the Protocol set to HTTP.
    

    Check the box to enable VPN connections.
    


    

    

    

    Deep Dive: The Use a URL in your domain is available to provide an easy way to customize the domain used. This means that all that is needed to make an application available publicly with an existing or new custom domain is a DNS entry with the correct CNAME and a valid certificate for that domain.
    

    

    Continue scrolling to the very bottom and observe the only associated rule is the default Block rule that is applied automatically to any private access traffic not matching an administrator defined rule.
    


    

    Click Save and Test. This will verify that the intranet web application is reachable on TCP port 80.
    

    On the Private Resources page, there is now an option to Test Reachability to the newly created Intranet Private Resource.  
    

    Click Test Reachability.  Note, you may need to wait a minute for the cloud security policy to be deployed in Secure Access.  If you get an error, wait a minute and try again.
    

     
    

    Testing should be successful as shown below.
    

    

    Deep Dive: Private Resource reachability can be tested for any app that is defined by a specific IP or domain, port and TCP protocol. Testing can be done at time of creation like here and it can be tested after creation by clicking the name of the Private Resource and clicking Test Reachability in the drawer that pops out on the right. 
    

    This completes the definition of the first application to be used for the Intranet App.
    

    

    Next, let's setup another Private Resource, this time for Contractor RDP access into a workstation.
    

    Click + Add to add the Contractor RDP Private Resource.
    

    

    Enter the following information into the fields that are displayed:
    

      

    • Private Resource Name: Contractor - RDP Workstation
      • 

    • Internally Reachable Address: contractor-wkst.corp.pseudoco.com
      • 

    • Protocol: TCP - RDP
      • 

    • Port / Ranges: 3389
      • 

    


    

    

    Scroll down to Endpoint Connection Methods.
    

    Check the Zero-trust connections checkbox.  Client-based connection and Browser-based connection will both be enabled by default.
    

    Because the contractor using this Private Resource does not have a corporate laptop, they will be connecting via Browser-based connection.  As a result, deselect the Client-based connection radio button.
    

    

    Continue scrolling to the very bottom and once again observe the only associated rule is the default Block rule that is applied automatically to an any private access traffic not matching an administrator defined rule.
    

    

    Click Save and Test.  Let's test to see if we can reach the Contractor Workstation via RDP.
    

    Testing should be successful as shown below.
    

    

    Deep Dive: Notice that a single Private Resource can have multiple access methods. This streamlines rule creation and gives administrators the ability to migrate from a VPN based architecture to a more secure ZTA solution. All applications can be made available through all access methods such as Client-based ZTA, Clientless ZTA, Remote Access VPN, and Branch. This provides a very flexible, single definition for all forms of access.
    

    

    

    Configuring private resources in Cisco Secure Access defines the foundation for identity-driven application access by securely publishing internal applications without exposing the underlying network. Instead of relying on IP-based access or inbound firewall rules, resources are abstracted and made accessible through Secure Access based on identity and policy. This highlights a key Cisco differentiator: the ability to decouple application access from network access, enabling Zero Trust principles where users connect only to what they are authorized to use. The result is reduced attack surface, simplified application publishing, and secure access to internal resources from anywhere.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    
    

       Apps are live, but locked down tight! 🔐📲 You’ve exposed the right things—now let’s decide who gets in! 🎯
          
    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    3. Access Policy
    
  
    
  
    Access Policy
    

    We will start by building an Endpoint Posture Profile that will be used in the Access Policy.  The Endpoint Posture Policy allows you to define various criteria on the endpoint before being allowed to connect.
    

    Navigate to Secure -> Endpoint Posture Profiles.
    

    

    

    From the Endpoint Posture Profiles page, select the Add posture profile button on the right and then click Client-based.
    

    

    

    Name the profile PseudoCo Windows, select Windows as the Operating system, and Update is required: Within 365 days.
    

    

    

    Next, select (3) Endpoint security agents to proceed to the next setting option.
    

    Select Windows under Operating systems requiring endpoint security agents.  Leave all other options at the default and click the Save and Exit button.
    

    

    

    Let's move on to configuring the Access Policy.
    

    From the left menu, navigate to Secure -> Access Policy.
    

    

    

    

    Scroll down to the bottom of the page and notice the two default rules:
    

    Private Access Default Deny:  Denies all traffic destined to private resources by default, meaning an explicit allow must be configured for any users to be able access defined internal applications and networks.
    

    Internet Access Default Allow: Allows users to access all internet destinations by default. This provides maximum connectivity with minimum monitoring for internet-bound traffic. Rules will need to be created to restrict internet-bound traffic based on company policy.
    

    

    Now let's create some rules!
    

     
    

    Click Add Rule and then select Private Access.
    

    

    Input the following rule configuration parameters:
    

    

      

    • Rule name: Production Intranet App
      • 

    • Action: Allow
      • 

    • From: AD groups > PROD
      • 

    • To: Private Resources > Production - Intranet App Server
      • 

    

    

    

    

    Scroll down to the Endpoint Requirements.
    

    For Zero-Trust Client-based Posture Profile, select the newly created PseudoCo Windows profile.
    

    For Zero-Trust Browser-based Posture Profile, leaves the default System provided option selected.
    

    

    

    Scroll down to the bottom and click Next.
    

    In the Configure Security section, IPS profiles with SNORT rules can be applied. Start by Enabling IPS, set the Profile to Connectivity over Security, and leave the Security Profile at the default of System Provided - Private Access. 
    


    

     
    

    Click Save to complete the rule creation.
    

    

    

    Next, we'll create the rule to get the contractor access to the Contractor workstation via RDP.
    

    Click Add Rule in the upper right hand corner and then select Private Access.
    

    

    

    Input the following rule configuration parameters:
    

    

      

    • Rule name: Contractor RDP Access
      • 

    • Action: Allow
      • 

    • From: AD groups > IoT
      • 

    • To: Private Resources > Contractor - RDP Workstation
      • 

    

    

    

    

    Leave the Zero Trust Browser-based Posture Profile at the default setting and click Next.
    

    

    

    In the Configure Security section, enable IPS again, but this time set the profile to Security over Connectivity because the contractor has a lower level of trust. Leave the Security Profile at the default of System Provided - Private Access. 
    

    

    

    We now have both of our Private Access rules created to allow the proper level of access to our internal resources!
    

    Deep Dive: Notice that the new Contractor RDP Access rule is added after Production Intranet App. These rules are processed in order from the top down, stopping when there is a match. Since both are allow rules and specific to a single application, their order does not matter. Rule order is important though, and rules can easily be moved up and down as needed if policies change or unexpected behavior is discovered.
    

    

    

    By default, logging will be enabled on any new custom rule, however, logging is not enabled on the default Internet Access rule.
    

    Click the 3 dots on the right side of For all Internet access and select Edit.
    

    

    

    Enable Logging and click Save.
    

    

    Authorized Access to the Intranet and contractor access has been configured. This gets the basis in place for accessing an application from an infrastructure standpoint.
    

    

    Finally, we need to create an Access Policy for Client VPN that we will be configuring in the next VPNaaS Provisioning task.
    

    From the Access Policy page, select Add Rule -> Private Access.
    

    

    

    Input the following rule configuration parameters:
    

    

      

    • Rule name: VPN Allow All
      • 

    • Action: Allow
      • 

    • From: AD groups > PROD
      • 

    • To: Any
      • 

    

    

    

    

    Scroll down to the Endpoint Requirements.
    

    Select PseudoCo Windows for the Zero-Trust Client-based Posture Profile.
    

    Leave the Zero Trust Browser-based Posture Profile at the default setting and click Next.
    

    

    

    In the Configure Security section, enable IPS and set the profile to Connectivity over Security. Leave the Security Profile at the default of System Provided - Private Access. Click Save.
    

    

    

    You have now completed configuring the Private Access Policies allowing access to specific Private Resources, as well as full VPN as a Service for Remote Access Client VPN users.
    

    

    

    Defining access policies in Secure Access allows PseudoCo to enforce granular, identity-driven controls based on user identity, group membership, and device posture. Policies determine exactly who can access specific applications and under what conditions, ensuring least-privilege access across all user types. As a Cisco differentiator, these policies integrate natively with Duo and identity context, enabling dynamic, real-time decision-making rather than static rule enforcement. This results in consistent, scalable Zero Trust policy enforcement that adapts to user risk and context across all access methods.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    

    

    No more “all access passes”! 🎟️❌ You’re calling the shots now—only the right users, on the right devices, at the right time. Let’s bring connectivity into the mix! 🌐🔥
    
      
    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    4. VPNaaS Provisioning
    
  
    
  
    VPN Service Provisioning
    

    In this section we’ll configure the VPN IP Pools and a VPN Profile. The steps in this section will be performed from Jumphost1, Secure Access and Duo.
    

    Stay in Secure Access and navigate to Connect -> End User Connectivity.
    


    

    Click the Virtual Private Network tab.
    

    The first thing that needs to be done to Setup VPN is to add an IP Pool. This provisions a Region for the VPN Clients to connect with Client IP addresses, Management IP Addresses and DNS Server(s). Click Add IP Pool.
    

    

    Deep Dive: Secure Access requires 2 IP pools per VPN Profile, a System IP Pool and Client IP Pool. System IP Pool is used for control traffic including NTP and VPN Headend assignment. Client IP Pool are the range(s) used for your end users.
    

    IP pools need to be non-overlapping private addresses from the internal network. Client IP Pools can be as large as a /16 or as small as a /28. The range for System IP Pools is /21 to /28. Both pools can accept up to five non-contiguous subnets. The ratio of Client IPs to System IPs is 4096:1, meaning for every 4096 or /20 Client IP Pool, a single IP in the System IP Pool is required, so the 10.100.2.0/28 can be paired with up to 65536 Client IPs. The IP Pool configuration will provide an error if the System IP Pool is not large enough for the configured Client IP Pool. If using BGP for private access Network Tunnel Groups, these pools will be advertised as reachable over all connected
    

    Enter the following values:
    

      

    • Region: US (Virginia)
      • 

    • Display name: PseudoCo RA VPN System Pool
      • 

    • DNS Servers: PseudoCo DNS (198.18.5.102)
      • 

    • System IP pool: 198.18.21.0/24

      • 

    

    

    

    


    

    In the IP Pools section, click Add IP Pool.
    

     
    

    

    Enter the following values:
    

      

    • IP Pool name: PseudoCo RA VPN User Pool
      • 

    • IPv4 subnets: 198.18.22.0/24
      • 

    

    

    

    Click Save.
    

    

    In the Edit IP Pool Window, click Save.
    

    

    

    Now that an IP Pool has been configured for the VPN, it's time to create a VPN Profile.
    

    Click the End User Connectivity button in the top left corner.
    

    

    

    Scroll down to VPN Profiles and click + VPN profile.
    

    

    

    On the General Settings page, enter the following values:
    

      

    • VPN Profile Name: PseudoCo_RA_VPN_Profile
      • 

    • Display name: PseudoCo User VPN
      • 

    • Default Domain: corp.pseudoco.com
      • 

    • IP Pools: Click Assign IP Pools then select Pseudoco Labs Client IP Pool, click Save.
      • 

    

     
    

    Click Next.
    

    On the Authentication, Authorization, and Accounting page, enter the following values:
    

      

    • Check External browser authentication
      • 

    • SAML Metadata XML Configuration: 
      • 

    • Click Download service provider XML file
      • 

    

    You may be prompted to verify the download. If so, click Download unverified file.
    

    

    

    Return to the Duo Admin Portal in another browser tab. A new protected application must be created in Duo for each VPN profile.
    

    In the Duo Admin Portal navigate to Applications -> Application Catalog.
    

     
    

    

    Type “Secure Access” in the Filter by keywords field. Click Add below the Cisco Secure Access VPN application.
    

    

    

    Enter the following values:
    

      

    • Application name: Cisco Secure Access VPN - Single Sign-On
      • 

    • User access: Select Enable for all Users
      • 

    • Under Service Provider, select Choose file.
      • 

    • Navigate to the Downloads folder and select PseudoCo_RA_VPN_Profile_saml_metadata.xml and click Open.
      • 

    


    

    Scroll down and click Save.
    

    Scroll down again to the Downloads section and click Download XML.
    

    You may be prompted to verify the download. If so, click Download unverified file.
    

    

    Back in the Secure Access dashboard VPN Profile configuration, continuing the SAML configuration, click Upload on step 3. Upload IdP security metadata XML file.  Select the downloaded XML from the Duo Admin Portal, Cisco Secure Access VPN - Single Sign-On - IDP Metadata.xml, click Open.
    

    

    Click Next. 
    

     
    

    On the Traffic Steering (Split Tunnel) page, notice that there are already exceptions in the VPN Traffic Steering list. These are added by default to prevent the VPN tunnel from interfering with the ZTA module enrollment and connectivity.
    

    Important, two more exceptions need to be added for this lab to maintain Web RDP access.
    

    Click Add.
    

    In Add Destinations, select Add a source: 10.16.0.0/15,198.19.255.0/24,198.18.133.0/24
    

    

    Click Save.
    

    

    Click the Proxy and DNS Steering Settings tab, and adjust the following DNS configuration:
    

    DNS Mode: Tunnel All DNS
    

    

     
    

    Click Next.
    

    

    From the Cisco Secure Client Configuration Session Settings tab, configure the following:
    

      

    • Banner Message: Enable
      • 

    • Banner Text: Authorized Users Only
      • 

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    Click the Client Settings tab.
    

    Check the box to enable Local LAN Access.  This will allow you to stay connected to RWST-2 after connecting to the VPN.
    

    

    

    Scroll down and expand the Administrator Settings section.  Adjust the following options:
    

      

    • Windows Logon Enforcement: Single Logon
      • 

    • Windows VPN Establishment: All Remote Users 
      • 

    

    Click Save.
    

    Note: Because of the resolution of the Jumphost, it may be necessary to zoom out of the page in the browser before clicking save in this section. Zooming out to 70-80% should make the save button visible.
    

    

    The VPN Profile is complete! Remote access and all its components are now fully configured.  The next section will perform the final configuration steps to enable Zero Trust Access.
    

    

    Provisioning VPN-as-a-Service (VPNaaS) through Secure Access modernizes traditional remote access by delivering secure connectivity from the cloud without requiring on-prem VPN concentrators. Users can establish encrypted tunnels while still benefiting from centralized identity-based policy enforcement and cloud-delivered security inspection. This is a major Cisco advantage—combining the familiarity of VPN with the scalability and intelligence of Secure Access. The result is simplified infrastructure, improved user experience, and consistent security policy enforcement regardless of user location.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    

    

    VPN… but make it cloud ☁️😎 You’ve upgraded remote access to the modern era—now let’s put some Zero Trust in! 🚀
    

    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    5. ZTA Provisioning
    
  
    
  
    ZTA Provisioning
    

    In this section we’ll configure the ZTA Profile required to enable Zero Trust Access. The steps in this section will be performed from Secure Access.
    

    Stay in Secure Access and navigate to Connect -> End User Connectivity.
    


    

    Click the Zero Trust Access tab followed by + ZTA Profile.
    

    

    

    On the Edit ZTA profile page, name the ZTA profile and then select + Users and Groups.
    

    ZTA Profile Name: PseudoCo ZTA Profile
    

    

    

    Expand Groups and then AD Groups.
    

    Select MAIN and PROD, followed by Save.
    

    

    We now have 2 groups of users allowed for Zero Trust Access.
    

    Click Next.
    

    

    

    Click + Destinations.
    

    

    

    Note: If you see a blank menu like below, simply click into the upper portion of the blank menu to proceed with adding the private resource.
    

    On the Add private resources page, select the Production - Intranet App Server private resource that we created earlier, then click Save.
    

    

    We now have the Intranet App enabled for Zero Trust Access, which allows authorized users to access this application without a VPN!
    

    Click Next to proceed to the Secure Internet Access section.
    

    

    

    On the Secure Internet Access page, select Use ZTA to secure all internet destinations.  As the name implies, this will cause the ZTA Module inside Secure Client to intercept all internet-bound traffic and send it to Secure Access for inspection.
    

    Click Close to complete the ZTA Profile setup.
    

    

    

    

    🧭 Next up
    

    

    Application access without the hassle of VPN! ☁️😎 Your applications are now accessible and secure—now let’s test it like a real user would! 🚀
    

    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    6. Testing End to End Policy
    
  
    
  
    Testing Use Case 1: Browser-based Contractor Access
    

    This use case demonstrates how PseudoCo provides secure, limited access for external contractors using unmanaged devices. Lee, a contractor, authenticates through the identity provider and uses browser-based Secure Access to launch a proxied remote desktop session to a designated workstation—without requiring VPN or direct network access. This approach ensures access is restricted, monitored, and controlled while enabling contractor productivity.
    

    

    Test Access Type: Browser-based Access
    

    Test Device: RWST-1 (Remote Workstation 1)
    

    Test User: Lee (our contractor)
    

    

    Starting in Secure Access, navigate to Resources -> Private Resource.
    

    

    

    On the Private Resources window, click the Contractor - RDP Workstation private resource, which opens a slider pane on the right side.
    

    Scroll down to the Browser-based ZTA section.  Also, notice that the Client-based ZTA and VPN options are OFF because we did not configure those options.
    

    Under the Browser-based ZTA section, click the button to the right of the External URL.  This is the URL we will use to test Browser-based Access from RWST-1.
    

    

    

    From Jumphost1, open mRemoteNG.  Under the Workstations - Remote section, double click RWST-1 to connect to Remote Workstation 1.
    

    Once connected to RWST-1, open the Chrome browser.
    

    

    Paste the URL that you copied from Secure Access into your browser window and hit enter.
    

    Note: This URL will be different for each pod.
    

    

    

    You will be prompted for authentication by Duo.  Enter the credentials from your lab printout - it will be a format similar to below depending on where your pod is located and which Pod # it is.
    

    RTP Username: lee@rtp.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    SJC Username: lee@sjc.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    Password: C1sco12345
    

    

    Click Next and supply the password specified above.
    

    You will now be presented with a login page from Secure Access.  You will use this to login to the contractor workstation via RDP within the browser.  Login with the credentials below.
    

    Username: lee
    

    Password: C1sco12345
    

    

    

    After authenticating, you will be logged into the workstation within the PseudoCo internal environment via RDP.  Now, Lee the contractor can get to work and disconnect when complete!
    

    

     
    

    This concludes the Contractor Browser-based Access use case.  Disconnect from RWST-1 in mRemoteNG to proceed to the next use case - VPN as a Service!
    

    

    🎉 This concluded the first use case testing.
    

    

    This use case demonstrates how Cisco Secure Access enables secure, browser-based access for contractors without requiring a VPN client or direct network connectivity. By proxying access through the cloud and enforcing identity-based authentication via Duo, PseudoCo ensures that contractors like Lee can only access specific approved resources. A key Cisco differentiator is the ability to deliver application-level access without exposing the underlying network, reducing attack surface while maintaining full session visibility and control. This provides secure, frictionless access for unmanaged devices while enforcing strict Zero Trust principles.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    


    

    No client, no problem! 🌐🔐 Contractors get just enough access—now let’s level up to full connectivity for power users! 💪
    

    

    

    

    Testing Use Case 2: VPN-as-a-Service for Remote Employees
    

    This use case shows how PseudoCo supports employees who need broader network access for tasks like administration or legacy applications using Secure Access VPN-as-a-Service. Kit connects from a remote workstation, authenticates, and establishes a secure VPN session to reach internal resources such as the intranet and network services. This approach delivers modern VPN connectivity without relying on traditional VPN infrastructure.
    

    

    Test Access Type: VPN-as-a-Service
    

    Test Device: RWST-2 (Remote Workstation 2)
    

    Test User: Kit (our production user)
    

    

    Starting in Secure Access, navigate to Connect -> End User Connectivity.  Click the Virtual Private Network tab.
    

    

    

    In the VPN Profiles section under the Profile URL column, copy the URL. It will look like 87c5f7.vpn.sse.cisco.com/PseudoCo_RA_VPN_Profile, with the first six characters being pod-specific.
    

    

    🛑 Note: An XML file can also be downloaded and distributed via SCCM or an MDM.
    


    

    Return to the Jumphost and open mRemoteNG.  Double-click RWST-2 on the left side to connect to Remote Workstation 2. 
    

    Once connected to RWST-2, double-click the Cisco Secure Client shortcut on the desktop, paste the VPN URL from Secure Access into the Anyconnect VPN connection field, and click Connect. 
    

    

    

    You will be prompted for authentication by Duo.  Enter the credentials from your lab printout - it will be a format similar to below depending on where your pod is located and which Pod # it is.
    

    RTP Username: kit@rtp.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    SJC Username: kit@sjc.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    Password: C1sco12345
    

    

    Click Next and supply the password specified above.
    

    You will now be presented with the banner you configured previously.  Click Accept to continue and be connected to the VPN.
    

    

    

    Now that you're connected to the VPN, open a command prompt and type nslookup intranet.corp.pseudoco.com.  It resolves to 198.18.5.15, which is an internal IP address in the PseudoCo HQ Data Center.
    

    

    

    Next, open Chrome and click the Intranet App shortcut in the bookmark bar.  This shows that there is network connectivity across the VPN to intranet.corp.pseudoco.com.
    

    

    

    🎉 Congratulations! this concluded the VPN-as-a-Service testing.
    

    

    This scenario highlights how Cisco Secure Access delivers VPN-as-a-Service (VPNaaS), modernizing traditional remote access by shifting it to a cloud-delivered model. Users like Kit can securely connect to internal resources with encrypted tunnels, while still benefiting from centralized identity-based policy enforcement. A major Cisco differentiator is eliminating the need for on-prem VPN concentrators while integrating VPN access into a broader Zero Trust framework. This provides scalability, simplified operations, and consistent security policy enforcement across all remote access sessions.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    


    

    VPN, but smarter! ☁️🧠 You’ve got full access when needed—now let’s dial it back and go application-first with Zero Trust! 🎯
    

    

    

    

    Testing Use Case 3: Client-Based Zero Trust Network Access
    

    This use case highlights PseudoCo’s shift toward client-based Zero Trust Network Access to reduce reliance on full VPN connectivity. Kit enrolls a device with the Secure Access client and, after authentication, gains seamless access to authorized applications without network-level exposure. This approach allows secure access to private applications while keeping the internal network fully protected.
    

    

    

    Next, we will connect RWST-2 to Client-based ZTA to show how this connectivity is different than a full VPN.  VPN can remain connected when we connect to Zero Trust Access (ZTA). If both VPN and Client-based ZTA are enabled for a Private Resource, Client-based ZTA will take precedence and intercept the interesting traffic.
    

    For ZTA enrollment, open the Cisco Secure Client application on RWST-2.
    

    Click Enroll to enroll in the Zero Trust Access module.
    

    

    

    At the username prompt, it's the same username that we used to login to the VPN (see below for format reminder and on your lab printout for credentials).
    

    RTP Username: kit@rtp.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    SJC Username: kit@sjc.corp.pseudoco.com

    

    

    

    You will be redirected to Duo to complete the authentication.  Enter the password below to complete the enrollment.
    

    Password: C1sco12345
    

    

    

    This message will flash up in the lower right hand corner confirming the successful enrollment in ZTA.  How simple was that?!
    

    

    

    Now that we are enrolled in ZTA, go back to the Command Prompt and enter the same nslookup command again. While accessing the Intranet web app will look the same to the end user, DNS for intranet.corp.pseudoco.com is now resolving to different IPs.
    

    nslookup intranet.corp.pseudoco.com
    

    Now the DNS query is being intercepted by the ZTA module and resolving to an IP that is only reachable over the tunnel that is created by the ZTA module towards the Secure Access cloud.
    

     
    


    

    

    Back in Chrome, refresh the intranet web portal to change the connection from VPN to client-based ZTA.  Notice that there is no noticeable difference to the user, which is exactly the outcome we are looking for. 
    

    Feel free to disconnect from the VPN and see that the Intranet web app is still reachable over the ZTA module.
    

     Now, users don't need to worry about connecting to the VPN - their ZTA module is always connected and securely intercepting traffic towards the private applications that have been defined!
    

    

    

    After successfully reaching the intranet web portal from both VPN and ZTA, go back to the Secure Access dashboard on Jumphost1 and in the left-hand navigation, click Monitor -> Activity Search.
    

    Type Kit into the search field, then click the Kit user that pops up in order to filter on all events tied to this identity.
    

    

    

    Scroll down until you see the ZTA CLIENT-BASED events triggered from accessing the Intranet App over the ZTA module.
    

    Click one of these ZTA CLIENT-BASED events to learn more.
    

    Note: Click in the white space next to the blue bubble.
    

    

    

    This will open a slider window to the right with additional details regarding the connection made over the ZTA module.  Investigate the identities and other attributes relevant to the access request.
    

    

    

    Feel free to investigate other events within the Activity Search to learn more about access requests happening on the network!
    

    🎉 Nice Job - you completed all of the Secure Private Access use case testing! 
    

    

    This use case showcases client-based Zero Trust Network Access (ZTNA), where Secure Access enables application-level connectivity without granting full network access. Using the Secure Client, users authenticate and are automatically connected only to authorized applications based on identity and device posture. This is a key Cisco differentiator—shifting from network-centric to identity-centric access control, where policies follow the user regardless of location. The result is reduced lateral movement risk, simplified access management, and a more secure, seamless user experience.
    

    

    🧭 Complete
    

    It was the moment of truth! 🎬🔍 You built it—now you proved it works. Secure, seamless, Zero Trust in action… and looking good doing it! 😎🔥 . Now lets take it a step further and get Secure Internet Access (SIA) working!! 
    

    

    

    
  
    Secure Internet Access (SIA) Configuration
    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    Section Overview – Enforcing Secure Internet Access and Data Protection
    

    With identity integrated and infrastructure connected to the cloud security edge, PseudoCo is ready to implement the next critical layer of its Zero Trust architecture: secure and policy-driven access to the public internet.
    

    In today’s enterprise environment, the majority of user activity involves interacting with internet-hosted services—ranging from SaaS applications to collaboration platforms and AI tools. As users operate across branch offices, remote locations, and unmanaged environments, PseudoCo must ensure that security policies follow users and devices everywhere, not just when they are connected to the corporate network.
    

    To accomplish this, PseudoCo has deployed cloud-delivered Secure Internet Access (SIA) through Cisco Secure Access—enabling consistent, identity-driven security enforcement for all internet-bound traffic.
    

    

    User Personas in the PseudoCo Environment (Review)
    

    Throughout this section of the lab, several user personas will be used to simulate real-world enterprise access scenarios. These personas may appear again in later sections of the lab as additional security capabilities are introduced.
    

    Kit – Production Employee
    

    Kit represents a full-time production employee at PseudoCo. As a trusted internal user, Kit has access to business-critical applications and internal resources.
    

    However, even with elevated application access, Kit must still comply with corporate internet usage policies and data protection controls.
    

    Pat – Production Employee
    

    Pat also represents a production employee within PseudoCo’s corporate environment. Like Kit, Pat has access to internal applications but remains subject to web security policies designed to reduce risk from malicious or inappropriate internet content.
    

    Nik – Corporate  Employee
    

    Nik represents a standard corporate user within PseudoCo’s Main user segment. Unlike production users, Nik has limited and controlled access to sensitive production applications and resources.
    

    Nik remains subject to strict web security and segmentation policies designed to reduce risk from unauthorized access and inappropriate internet usage. His role demonstrates how identity-based policy and segmentation enforce business-intent-driven access controls, ensuring users only access what is necessary for their role.
    

    Lee – Contractor
    

    Lee represents an external contractor who requires limited access to PseudoCo resources. Contractors often work alongside internal teams but must be restricted to only the applications and resources required for their role.
    

    Lee’s access will demonstrate how identity-based policies can enforce stricter controls for third-party users.
    

    IoTUser – Non-802.1X Device
    

    IoTUser represents a device that cannot perform standard user authentication, such as cameras, sensors, or operational equipment. These devices typically authenticate using MAC Authentication Bypass (MAB) and are assigned segmentation policies that restrict their behavior.
    

    This scenario illustrates how PseudoCo applies security policy to devices that cannot participate in traditional identity workflows.
    

    

    The Secure Internet Access Strategy at PseudoCo
    

    PseudoCo’s Secure Internet Access architecture is designed to deliver consistent, identity-aware protection across all users, devices, and locations—without relying on traditional perimeter-based controls.
    

    To achieve this, PseudoCo applies multiple policy enforcement models depending on how users and devices connect:
    

      

    • SGT-based policy enforcement for branch users, extending campus identity into secure internet access
      • 

    • Client-based Zero Trust  Access (ZTA) for securing remote users with the ZTA module, eliminating the need for backhauling traffic
      • 

    • Data Loss Prevention (DLP) and AI guardrails to protect sensitive information and control interactions with generative AI platforms (ChatGPT, Gemini, etc.)
      • 

    • Unified policy enforcement across branch, remote, and unmanaged environments through a single cloud-delivered platform
      • 

    

    All internet-bound traffic—regardless of origin—is evaluated using a combination of:
    

      

    • User identity and group membership
      • 

    • Security Group Tags (SGTs) and segmentation context
      • 

    • Device type and authentication method
      • 

    • Organizational security and data protection policies
      • 

    

    This approach ensures that every request is validated, inspected, and enforced consistently—extending Zero Trust principles to the internet edge.
    

    

    Secure Internet Access Use Cases
    

    The following test scenarios will demonstrate how Secure Access enforces web security and data protection policies using identity and segmentation context.
    

    

    Use Case 1 – SGT-Based Internet Policy Enforcement (Branch)
    

    In the first scenario, Secure Internet Access policy will be enforced based on network segmentation using Security Group Tags (SGTs).
    

    A workstation located in the branch campus environment is assigned the PROD SGT, representing a production user segment within the organization.
    

    When this workstation attempts to access a website categorized under gambling, Secure Access will evaluate the request using the SGT context provided by the network infrastructure.
    

    Because this segment is restricted from accessing non-business web categories, Secure Access will block the request.
    

    This test demonstrates how PseudoCo can enforce internet security policies based on network segmentation and device classification, even when user identity may not be available.
    

    

    Use Case 2 – Identity-Based Web Filtering (Remote Users)
    

    The second scenario demonstrates how Secure Access applies web filtering policies based on user identity and directory group membership.
    

    When Kit logs in from a remote workstation, authentication occurs through the identity platform integrated earlier in the lab. Secure Access receives identity attributes and group membership information from Microsoft Active Directory through Cisco Duo.
    

    If Kit attempts to access a website categorized as gambling, Secure Access will enforce the organization’s acceptable-use policy and block the request.
    

    This same test can also be performed using Lee, the contractor persona, demonstrating how contractors receive the same or stricter internet usage restrictions.
    

    This scenario highlights how PseudoCo enforces identity-driven web security policies for remote users without requiring traditional VPN connectivity.
    

    

    Use Case 3 – Data Loss Prevention and AI Guardrails
    

    The final scenario introduces data protection and AI governance controls.
    

    As generative AI tools become increasingly common in the workplace, organizations must ensure that sensitive corporate information is not inadvertently exposed when users interact with public AI platforms.
    

    Using Secure Access Data Loss Prevention capabilities, PseudoCo has implemented AI guardrails to prevent sensitive data and malicious activity from being submitted to external AI services such as ChatGPT.
    

    In this test scenario, Kit will attempt to submit malicious  activity to ChatGPT from both:
    

      

    • 

      A campus workstation
      

      • 

    • A remote workstation
      • 

    

    Secure Access will inspect the request and detect the presence of malicious activity
    

    When the data / activity matches PseudoCo’s DLP policy definitions, the request will be blocked, preventing the sensitive information and or activity from leaving or executing in the organization.
    

    This demonstrates how Secure Access provides modern data protection controls for emerging technologies such as generative AI.
    

    

    What You Will Achieve in This Section
    

    By completing these exercises, you will validate how PseudoCo enforces consistent internet security policies across multiple environments.
    

    You will:
    

      

    • 

      Verify SGT-based internet policy enforcement for branch users
      

      • 

    • 

      Validate identity-based web filtering for remote employees and contractors
      

      • 

    • 

      Test DLP policies that prevent sensitive data exposure to AI services
      

      • 

    • 

      Observe how Secure Access applies policy regardless of user location
      

      • 

    

    These capabilities allow PseudoCo to maintain strong security controls while supporting a flexible and distributed workforce.
    

    

    Why This Matters to PseudoCo
    

    The public internet is one of the largest attack surfaces facing modern organizations. At the same time, employees must interact with cloud services, research platforms, and AI tools to perform their work effectively.
    

    By implementing Secure Internet Access through Secure Access, PseudoCo ensures that:
    

      

    • 

      Security policies follow users everywhere
      

      • 

    • 

      Internet threats and risky content are blocked
      

      • 

    • 

      Sensitive corporate data is protected
      

      • 

    • 

      Emerging technologies such as generative AI are used safely
      

      • 

    

    This allows PseudoCo to balance productivity, innovation, and security while maintaining a strong Zero Trust security posture.
    

    
  
    📄

    📄
    
    2. Access Policy (SIA / DLP)
    
  
    
  
    Configuring SIA Access Policy
    

    With identity and connectivity in place, this section focuses on enforcing secure, policy-driven internet access using Cisco Secure Access. You will apply identity and segmentation-based controls to protect users from web threats, enforce acceptable use policies, and prevent sensitive data loss. This is where Zero Trust extends to the internet—ensuring every request is inspected, validated, and controlled regardless of user location.
    

    Return to Jumphost1 and launch the Secure Access dashboard from the Dashboard automation tile in the Chrome browser.
    

    Navigate to Secure Internet Access Policy Configuration by returning to Jumphost1 and launching your SCC Dashboard from the Dashboard automation tile. Start by clicking on Secure Access.
    

    Navigate to Secure -> Access Policy. Review existing policy structure (from the previous section).
    

    

    

    Configure Web Policy for SGT and Identity Based Enforcement
    

    Click on Add Rule, then click Internet Access.
    

    

    Start by giving your rule a name of Block-Gambling, and optionally give the rule a description.
    

    

    Next, scroll down to the Specify Access section and set the Action to Block. In the From field, we will be specifying (2) different Identity Sources for both Main and Production. 
    

    Start by clicking the drop down for Select Sources, then select Security Group Tags.
    

    Select the Main and Production SGT group tags.
    

    

    Select the second Identity source by Selecting Groups and Organizational Unit -> AD Groups.
    

    Make sure your don't have "Any AD Group" checked to allow you to make specific selections.
    

    Select the MAIN and PROD AD groups.
    

    This concludes setting both our SGTs and AD Group Identities for the rule.
    

    

    Next, we will set the To destination to configure our Gambling Content Category we want to block. 
    

    Start by clicking the To field and then select Content Categories -> Gambling.
    

    

    We now have completed all of the details to specify our desired access. Click Next to go to the second step of configuring Security.

    

    In the Configure Security section, leave the "System Provided - Internet Access (Rule Default)".
    

    The Security Profile configures security requirements that must be met before traffic is allowed. Observe that since this is a Block rule, not all security functions are valid. Feel free to browse all the Security capabilities which can be set for rules that are inspecting traffic.
    

    Click Save to save the rule.
    

    

    Finally, once your rule is saved, click on the rule name in the slide out and review the details of the rule. Verify you see our business intent like below.  We are blocking Gambling for both Main and Production users by matching SGT identities as well Active Directory group identities.
    

    

    

    

     This completes the SIA rule creation. Next, we will configure our AI Guardrails DLP policy.
    

    

    

    Configuring DLP and AI Guardrails Policy
    

    With Secure Internet Access policies in place, this section focuses on protecting sensitive data and governing the use of emerging technologies like Generative AI. You will configure Data Loss Prevention (DLP) and AI Guardrails to detect and block the exposure of critical information across web traffic. This is where Zero Trust evolves beyond access control—ensuring not just who can connect, but what data can be shared and where it is allowed to go.
    

    Still in Secure Access, navigate to Secure -> Data Loss Prevention Policy.
    

    

    Start by clicking ADD RULE, then select AI Guardrails Rule.
    

    

    Start the configuration by entering a Rule Name and setting the Severity to Medium. Optionally, you can give the rule a description.
    

    

    Next, in the Data Classification section under Content, select all (3) Guardrails (Privacy, Safety, Security). You can optionally click Preview to observe the details of the Guardrail. 
    

    

    Scroll down to the Identities section. Under AD Groups, select the Main and Prod AD Groups.
    

    

    Back in the Identities, select Tunnels to add our Network Tunnel Groups (NTG's) as identity sources as well. This will allow us to enforce this rule not only to AD groups, but also to traffic coming from our SIA connection from the Branch SDWAN router. 
    

    

    Next, in the Destinations section, select the Generative AI Application Category. Then click the drop-down to expand and configure the category settings.
    

    

    Scroll down to OpenAI ChatGBT and select "Prompt & Response" in the drop-down. This will configure both Prompt & Response inspection in the ChatGBT session with the user. 

    Observe all the different Generative AI applications included in our rule.
    

    

    Next, in the Action section, select Block. Leave all other sections set to the defaults and then click Save.
    

    

    Finally, review the Data Loss Prevention Policy AI Guardrails Rule details that we just configured.
    

    

    This concludes the creation of our DLP AI Guardrails policy, and completes the Secure Internet Access section. 
    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    Configuring Secure Internet Access (SIA) policies in Cisco Secure Access enables PseudoCo to enforce consistent identity and context-driven security controls for all internet-bound traffic. These policies leverage user identity, Security Group Tags (SGTs), device posture, and organizational rules to determine what users can access and how traffic is inspected. A key Cisco differentiator is the convergence of networking, security, identity, and data protection into a single cloud-delivered platform—extending beyond web filtering to include integrated Data Loss Prevention (DLP) and AI guardrails. In this lab, these capabilities were used to detect and block sensitive data from being shared with external services such as generative AI platforms, ensuring safe adoption of modern tools. The result is simplified policy management, consistent enforcement, and comprehensive protection against web threats, data exfiltration, and emerging AI-related risks across all users and locations.
    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    🧭 Next up
    
    

    Policies are set, guardrails are up! 🛡️🌐 You’re officially in control of the internet—now let’s see how it all plays out in the real world! 🎬
    

    

    
  
    📄
