    3. Testing End-to-End Policy
    
  
    
  
    Testing End-to-End Policy 
    

    With configuration complete, it’s time to put your security policies to the test. You will validate how Secure Access enforces controls across branch and remote users, while protecting sensitive data. Let’s confirm everything is working exactly as designed.
    

    For reference, review our policy we are looking to validate.
    

    

    Branch:
    

    


    
Workstation
User
Virtual Network (VRF)
SGT
IP subnet
 SIA Policy  
DLP - AI Guardrails (NTG ID)

    
    


    
Workstation-2
Kit
PROD (VRF 101)
Production (19)
10.101.255.0/24
Block Gambling
ChatGPT - Prompt & Response

    

    
Workstation-3
Nik
Main (VRF 10)
Main (16)
10.10.255.0/24
Block Gambling
ChatGPT - Prompt & Response

    
    

    

     
    

    Remote:
    

    


    
Workstation
User
AD-Group / Identity 
 SIA Policy     
DLP - AI Guardrails

    
    


    
RWST-2 
Kit
PROD 
Block Gambling
ChatGPT - Prompt & Response

    
    

    

    

    

    Validate SGT-Based Policy (Branch Use Case)
    

    In the first scenario, our Secure Internet Access policy will be enforced based on network segmentation using Security Group Tags (SGTs). Workstation-2 is logging in as Kit, a production user located in the branch campus environment that is assigned the Production SGT (19).
    

    When this user/workstation attempts to access the website (gambling.com) categorized under Gambling, Secure Access will evaluate the request using the SGT context provided by the network infrastructure. Because this segment is restricted from accessing this non-business web category, Secure Access will block the request. The user be returned a custom SSE Block page from Secure Access ensuring the user knows they violate a corporate policy.
    

    This test demonstrates how PseudoCo can enforce internet security policies based on network segmentation and device classification, even when user identity may not be available.
    

    From Jumphost1, use MRemote to login to Workstation-2. Remember that the RDP sessions are set to automatically log you in as the users defined above. In this case, the user will be Kit (a production user). Once logged in, launch SX Virtual Link and attach an available NIC (if it's not already connected). Use the Ping-Test app option (5) to verify that you authenticated successfully and obtained an IP address in the PROD Network.  
    

    

    Next, from the authenticated Workstation-2, launch the Chrome browser from the desktop. From the browser in the bookmarks tool bar, click the favorite for Gambling. You will be returned our custom SSE Block Page. Next, browse to a website of your choice outside of the gambling content category, such as cisco.com, and you will be allowed to browse unrestricted.
    

    

    

    Now, let's investigate our connection in Secure Access to see the logging and visibility of Secure Access. 
    

    Start within Secure Access by navigating to Monitor -> Activity Search to begin our investigation.
    

    

    Next, within the Activity Search, refresh the activity log.
    

    Filter on Blocked and pick a block log entry that includes gambling.com to analyze.
    

    Click on the entry to open the Event Details slide out. Review the details of the block and notice that it's using our Identity Source of Production (SGT-19).
    

    

    

    Optionally, you can now choose to repeat the process with Workstation-3, which is set to login with the user Nik as a Main user. You will see the same results in the activity log, except the Block will be with Identity Source Main (SGT-16).
    

    

    This use case demonstrates how Secure Access enforces internet policies based on Security Group Tags (SGTs), even without direct user identity. A branch user assigned the Production SGT attempts to access restricted web content, and Secure Access blocks the request based on the defined policy. This highlights a powerful Cisco differentiator: the ability to extend segmentation context (SGTs) beyond the network into cloud-delivered security enforcement. The benefit is consistent policy across the infrastructure.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    
   

    

    Segment says “nope!” 🚫🏷️ Your SGTs are pulling their weight—now let’s see what happens when user identity steps into the spotlight! 🎭
    
   
    

    

    

    Validate Identity-Based Web Filtering (Remote Use Case)
    

    The second scenario demonstrates how Secure Access applies web filtering policies based on user identity and Active Directory group membership.
    

    When Kit logs in from RWST-2, authentication occurs through the identity platform integrated earlier in the lab. Secure Access receives identity attributes and group membership information from Microsoft Active Directory through Cisco Duo. If Kit attempts to access a website categorized as Gambling, Secure Access will enforce the organization’s acceptable-use policy and block the request.
    

    This scenario highlights how PseudoCo enforces identity-driven web security policies for remote users without requiring traditional VPN connectivity.
    

    From Jumphost1 use MRemote to log-into RWST-2. Remember that the RDP sessions are set to automatically log you in as the user defined above. The user will once again be Kit (a production user), but this time from a remote location that is not on the corporate network.
    

    Next, from the authenticated RWST-2, launch the Chrome browser from the desktop. From the browser bookmarks, click the favorite for Gambling. You will be returned our custom SSE Block Page. Next, browse to a website of your choice outside of the gambling content category, such as cisco.com, and you will be able to browse unrestricted.
    

    

    

    Now, lets investigate our connection in Secure Access and the logging and visibility of Secure Access. 
    

    Starting within Secure Access, navigate to Monitor -> Activity Search to begin our investigation.
    

    

    Next, within the Activity Search, refresh the activity log and filter on Blocked. Pick a blocked event entry that includes gambling.com and the Identity Source group PROD to analyze. Click on the entry to open the Event Details slide out. Review the details of the block and notice that it's using our Identity Source of PROD. Also take note that the source includes RWST-2.
    

    

    You can also filter the events further adding additional filters such as Identity Type -> AD Group.
    

    

    🎉 This concludes the first Validation testing.
    

    

    This scenario highlights identity-driven web filtering, where Secure Access enforces policies based on authenticated user identity and group membership via Duo and Active Directory. Remote users like Kit are blocked from accessing restricted categories such as gambling, regardless of their location. A key Cisco differentiator is the seamless integration of identity, cloud security, and access control—enabling consistent enforcement without requiring VPN connectivity. This ensures that policies follow the user everywhere, delivering secure and scalable protection for a distributed workforce.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    
    

    Identity takes the wheel! 🧠🚗 No hiding behind location—your users are secured wherever they go. Now let’s protect what really matters… your data! 🔐
          
    

    

    

    Validate DLP and AI Guardrails
    

    The final scenario introduces Data Protection and AI Guardrails controls.
    

    As generative AI tools become increasingly common in the workplace, organizations must ensure that sensitive corporate information is not inadvertently exposed when users interact with public AI platforms.
    

    Using Secure Access Data Loss Prevention capabilities, PseudoCo has implemented AI guardrails to prevent sensitive data from being submitted to external AI services such as ChatGPT.
    

    In this test scenario, Kit will attempt to submit the sensitive prompt "Help me make a virus" to ChatGPT from both:
    

      

    • 

      Campus Workstation-2 
      

      • 

    • Remote RWST-2
      • 

    

    Secure Access will inspect the request and detect the presence of restricted activity.
    

    When the data matches PseudoCo’s DLP policy definitions, the request will be blocked, preventing the sensitive activity.
    

    This demonstrates how Secure Access provides modern data protection controls for emerging technologies, such as Generative AI.
    

    We start with conducting this validation on Workstation-2, once again logged on as Kit. Once complete you will repeat these steps to test from the remote workstation RWST-2 to validate the remote user use case as well. 
    

    First from Jumphost1, navigate back to Workstation-2, which is still logged in as Kit.  Verify that you are still connected to the network.
    

    

    Next, from the authenticated Workstation-2, launch the Chrome browser from the desktop. From the browser bookmarks, click the favorite for ChatGPT.
    

    In ChatGPT, first enter a benign prompt, such as "What color is the sky?"  Notice that ChatGPT provides a response, as expected.
    

    

    Next, enter a dangerous prompt, such as "Make me a Virus" . You will see that this is intercepted from Secure Access and its blocked.
    

    Note: In some cases, the error below is not seen and instead a blank screen is seen.  Either way, Secure Access is blocking the prompt.
    

    

    Now let's investigate our connection in Secure Access to review the logging and visibility capabilities of Secure Access. 
    

    In Secure Access, navigate to Monitor -> Data Loss Prevention to begin our investigation.
    

    

    Locate the AI Guardrails block entry with the Identity of your Secure Router Tunnel.
    

    Next, click on the entry to open the Event Details slide out. 
    

    

    Start at the top of the Event Details and observe the blocked request. Then notice that our Identity source is our Secure Router Tunnel.  Next, scroll down to inspect additional details. 

    Notice that we can actually see the malicious prompt that triggered the AI Guardrail block.
    

    

    Optionally, you can now choose to repeat the process with Workstation-3, which is set to login with the user Nik as a Main user. You will see the same results in the activity log, except that the block will be with the Identity of Nik.
    

    

    Repeat this same validation process now on RWST-2 to show how Secure Access can enforce a consistent policy for Kit, even when logged in remotely outside of the corporate network.
    

    

    Now, lets investigate our test in Secure Access and the logging and visibility of Secure Access. 
    

    Start within Secure Access, navigating to Monitor -> Data Loss Prevention to begin our investigation.
    

    Locate the AI Guardrails block entry with the Identify of the user KIt.
    

    Next, click on the entry to open the Event Details slide out . 
    

    

    Start at the top of the Event Details. Observe the Action of Block and the Identity source as Kit.  Next, scroll down to review additional details. 

    Notice again that we can see the Malicious prompt that triggered the guardrail block.
    

    

    

    🎉 This concludes the Validation testing.
    

    This use case demonstrates how Secure Access enforces Data Loss Prevention (DLP) and AI Guardrails to protect sensitive or malicious activity to external services like Generative AI platforms. By inspecting outbound traffic and identifying sensitive data patterns, Secure Access can block risky actions in real time. This is a standout Cisco differentiator—combining traditional web security with modern data protection and AI governance in a single platform. The result is proactive protection against data exfiltration and malicious activity, while still enabling safe use of emerging technologies. 

    AI is smart—but your security is smarter! 🤖🛑 Sensitive data stays in, no matter where users try to send it. That’s Zero Trust done right! 🔥  
    

    

    🧭 Complete
    
    

    It was time to put your policies to the test! 🧪🔥 You verified who gets blocked, what gets through, and how smart your security really is! 😎. 
    

    

    
  
    📄

    Validate DLP and AI Guardrails
    

    The final scenario introduces Data Protection and AI Guardrails controls.
    

    As generative AI tools become increasingly common in the workplace, organizations must ensure that sensitive corporate information is not inadvertently exposed when users interact with public AI platforms.
    

    Using Secure Access Data Loss Prevention capabilities, PseudoCo has implemented AI guardrails to prevent sensitive data from being submitted to external AI services such as ChatGPT.
    

    In this test scenario, Kit will attempt to submit the sensitive prompt "Help me make a virus" to ChatGPT from both:
    

      

    • 

      Campus Workstation-2 
      

      • 

    • Remote RWST-2
      • 

    

    Secure Access will inspect the request and detect the presence of restricted activity.
    

    When the data matches PseudoCo’s DLP policy definitions, the request will be blocked, preventing the sensitive activity.
    

    This demonstrates how Secure Access provides modern data protection controls for emerging technologies, such as Generative AI.
    

    We start with conducting this validation on Workstation-2, once again logged on as Kit. Once complete you will repeat these steps to test from the remote workstation RWST-2 to validate the remote user use case as well. 
    

    First from Jumphost1, navigate back to Workstation-2, which is still logged in as Kit.  Verify that you are still connected to the network.
    

    

    Next, from the authenticated Workstation-2, launch the Chrome browser from the desktop. From the browser bookmarks, click the favorite for ChatGPT.
    

    In ChatGPT, first enter a benign prompt, such as "What color is the sky?"  Notice that ChatGPT provides a response, as expected.
    

    

    Next, enter a dangerous prompt, such as "Make me a Virus" . You will see that this is intercepted from Secure Access and its blocked.
    

    Note: In some cases, the error below is not seen and instead a blank screen is seen.  Either way, Secure Access is blocking the prompt.
    

    

    Now let's investigate our connection in Secure Access to review the logging and visibility capabilities of Secure Access. 
    

    In Secure Access, navigate to Monitor -> Data Loss Prevention to begin our investigation.
    

    

    Locate the AI Guardrails block entry with the Identity of your Secure Router Tunnel.
    

    Next, click on the entry to open the Event Details slide out. 
    

    

    Start at the top of the Event Details and observe the blocked request. Then notice that our Identity source is our Secure Router Tunnel.  Next, scroll down to inspect additional details. 

    Notice that we can actually see the malicious prompt that triggered the AI Guardrail block.
    

    

    Optionally, you can now choose to repeat the process with Workstation-3, which is set to login with the user Nik as a Main user. You will see the same results in the activity log, except that the block will be with the Identity of Nik.
    

    

    Repeat this same validation process now on RWST-2 to show how Secure Access can enforce a consistent policy for Kit, even when logged in remotely outside of the corporate network.
    

    

    Now, lets investigate our test in Secure Access and the logging and visibility of Secure Access. 
    

    Start within Secure Access, navigating to Monitor -> Data Loss Prevention to begin our investigation.
    

    Locate the AI Guardrails block entry with the Identify of the user KIt.
    

    Next, click on the entry to open the Event Details slide out . 
    

    

    Start at the top of the Event Details. Observe the Action of Block and the Identity source as Kit.  Next, scroll down to review additional details. 

    Notice again that we can see the Malicious prompt that triggered the guardrail block.
    

    

    

    🎉 This concludes the Validation testing.
    

    This use case demonstrates how Secure Access enforces Data Loss Prevention (DLP) and AI Guardrails to protect sensitive or malicious activity to external services like Generative AI platforms. By inspecting outbound traffic and identifying sensitive data patterns, Secure Access can block risky actions in real time. This is a standout Cisco differentiator—combining traditional web security with modern data protection and AI governance in a single platform. The result is proactive protection against data exfiltration and malicious activity, while still enabling safe use of emerging technologies. 

    AI is smart—but your security is smarter! 🤖🛑 Sensitive data stays in, no matter where users try to send it. That’s Zero Trust done right! 🔥

    It was time to put your policies to the test! 🧪🔥 You verified who gets blocked, what gets through, and how smart your security really is! 😎. 
    

    

    

    
  
    Secure SD-WAN Local Enforcement 
    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    Section Overview – Secure SD-WAN Local Enforcement at the Branch Edge
    

    As PseudoCo continues building its Zero Trust architecture, the next step is ensuring that security enforcement exists not only in the cloud but also directly at the network edge.
    

    Up to this point in the lab, most user traffic—particularly from the MAIN and PROD segments—has been forwarded to the cloud security platform Cisco Secure Access for inspection and policy enforcement. This model works well for users and corporate devices that require full Secure Internet Access and private application access workflows.
    

    However, not every type of traffic or device requires inspection through the cloud security edge.
    

    PseudoCo’s branch environments contain a growing number of IoT and operational devices, including sensors, cameras, and facility systems. These devices typically generate predictable traffic patterns and often require local internet breakout for operational efficiency.
    

    Sending all IoT traffic to the Secure Access cloud could introduce unnecessary latency and increase operational overhead. At the same time, PseudoCo’s security team cannot allow these devices to access the internet without protection.
    

    To solve this challenge, PseudoCo leverages the local security capabilities built directly into the SD-WAN edge router, powered by Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN and its integrated on-box Next-Generation Firewall (NGFW) and Unified Threat Defense (UTD) engine.
    

    This architecture allows PseudoCo to apply local threat protection and policy enforcement at the branch edge while still maintaining consistent segmentation and security controls across the environment.
    

    

    Local Enforcement in the PseudoCo Architecture
    

    In the branch design, traffic handling follows a hybrid security model:
    

      

    • 

      MAIN and PROD segments forward internet-bound traffic to Secure Access for cloud-based inspection and policy enforcement.
      

      • 

    • 

      IoT segment traffic uses Direct Internet Access (DIA) locally at the branch but is protected by the router’s built-in NGFW and UTD capabilities.
      

      • 

    

    This approach allows PseudoCo to maintain strong security controls without unnecessarily routing all traffic through cloud inspection points.
    

    The SD-WAN edge router acts as a local enforcement point, capable of applying:
    

      

    • 

      Stateful firewall policy
      

      • 

    • 

      Intrusion Prevention System (IPS) inspection
      

      • 

    • 

      Malware protection
      

      • 

    • 

      URL filtering and web reputation controls
      

      • 

    • 

      Security Group Tag (SGT)-based policy enforcement
      

      • 

    

    By combining these capabilities, the router becomes a powerful security control point for edge environments.
    

    

    IoT Security Challenges
    

    IoT devices present unique challenges for enterprise security teams.
    

    These devices often:
    

      

    • 

      Cannot run endpoint security agents
      

      • 

    • 

      Do not support strong authentication methods
      

      • 

    • 

      Use proprietary or outdated operating systems
      

      • 

    • 

      Communicate with external cloud services
      

      • 

    

    In the PseudoCo environment, these devices authenticate using MAC Authentication Bypass (MAB) and are assigned the IoT Security Group Tag through the identity infrastructure.
    

    Once tagged, the network can apply consistent segmentation and security policy enforcement.
    

    This ensures that even devices with limited security capabilities are still governed by Zero Trust network controls.
    

    

    Use Case – IoT Threat Protection with Local Enforcement
    

    In this section of the lab, you will configure and validate local threat protection for IoT devices connected to the branch network.
    

    An IoT device will generate internet-bound traffic using Direct Internet Access from the branch router.
    

    Rather than forwarding this traffic to Secure Access, the SD-WAN edge router will inspect the traffic locally using the UTD security engine.
    

    A prebuilt NGFW security policy will be applied that includes:
    

      

    • 

      SGT-based policy enforcement
      

      • 

    • 

      Intrusion Prevention System (IPS) inspection
      

      • 

    • 

      Malware detection
      

      • 

    • 

      URL filtering controls
      

      • 

    

    This policy will ensure that IoT traffic is still protected even though it bypasses the cloud security edge.
    

    

    Policy Enforcement Example
    

    To demonstrate this capability, the IoT segment will be subject to a security rule that blocks access to specific web categories.
    

    Using the same segmentation strategy applied earlier in the lab, devices assigned the IoT SGT will inherit a security policy that blocks access to gambling websites.
    

    This demonstrates how PseudoCo can enforce consistent acceptable-use policies across multiple enforcement points, whether traffic is inspected in the cloud or locally at the branch.
    

    

    Protecting Internal Resources
    

    In addition to controlling internet access, the branch router will also enforce local segmentation controls using SGT context.
    

    The IoT device will attempt to access internal resources such as:
    

      

    • 

      The corporate intranet
      

      • 

    • 

      Application servers hosted in the data center
      

      • 

    

    Using the on-box firewall and IPS policies, the router will detect the unauthorized request and block the traffic before it reaches sensitive resources.
    

    This demonstrates how local enforcement can prevent east-west movement and unauthorized access attempts from less trusted device segments.
    

    

    What You Will Achieve in This Section
    

    In this portion of the lab, you will validate how PseudoCo protects edge environments using local SD-WAN security services.
    

    You will:
    

      

    • 

      Apply a prebuilt NGFW security policy on the SD-WAN edge router
      

      • 

    • 

      Enable UTD threat protection services including IPS, malware protection, and URL filtering
      

      • 

    • 

      Enforce SGT-based web filtering policies for IoT devices
      

      • 

    • 

      Block unauthorized access attempts to internal applications
      

      • 

    • 

      Verify that IoT traffic remains protected even when using Direct Internet Access
      

      • 

    

    These tests demonstrate how PseudoCo can maintain consistent security policy enforcement across both cloud and local security controls.
    

    

    Why This Matters to PseudoCo
    

    Modern enterprise networks require security enforcement across multiple layers of the architecture.
    

    By combining cloud-delivered security through Secure Access with local enforcement at the SD-WAN edge, PseudoCo achieves:
    

      

    • 

      Flexible traffic steering based on device type and risk profile
      

      • 

    • 

      Local threat protection for IoT and operational devices
      

      • 

    • 

      Reduced latency for edge traffic flows
      

      • 

    • 

      Consistent segmentation and policy enforcement using SGT context
      

      • 

    

    This layered model ensures that every device, user, and network segment remains protected—whether traffic is inspected in the cloud or directly at the branch edge.
    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    2. Configure and Test - NGFW Secure Router Policy 
    
  
    
  
    Configure NGFW Secure Router Policy
    

    The first step will be to configure an existing NGFW policy to protect the IoT segment traffic that uses Direct Internet Access (DIA) locally. This will provide us with flexibility regarding what traffic we send to Secure Access and what traffic we enforce locally. These enhanced capabilities allow for a consistent experience and policy while controlling costs where it makes sense. In our case, we don't want to use Secure Access to inspect all of our IoT device traffic; we prefer to handle that with the on-box security features.
    

    Start on Jumphost1 by using the Chrome bookmark favorite called Cisco SD-WAN.
    

    To start, select Configuration -> Policy Groups and review the configured Policy Groups. The Policy Group we will be using is the Pseudoco-Branch-Policy. Click to expand this policy. 
    

    

    Notice that the Secure Service Edge Policy has our Pseudoco-SIA policy that we already configured. Take note of the NGFW Policy that is configured. This is what we will be working with in this section to configure the on-box security features such as IPS, NGFW, and URL filtering of our Secure Router at the branch. Click on the NGFW Policy.
    

    

    Click the (...) next to our NGFW-Policy-Branches and then click Edit to adjust the policy based on our business intent. 
    

    

     There are currently (2) Zone Pairs configured in the NGFW Policy:

    

        


    
Source Zone
Destination Zone
Traffic Interception

    

    
IOT_zone
IOT_zone
Local IOT Network Traffic

    

    
IOT_zone
NAT-DIA
Internet Bound Traffic

    
    

    

    

    These are used to enforce policy on both the local IOT network segment traffic and internet-bound DIA traffic that originates from the IOT network segment. Remember that our other network segment traffic from PROD and Main are forced to a service route towards Secure Access for cloud-delivered SIA. We will be replicating the same policy structure directly on the branch Secure Router for this use case.
    

    

    We will now be configuring policy for the use cases below:

    1. Control IOT network traffic from reaching our Intranet Server: intranet.cop.pseudoco.com using SGT enforcement.
    

    2. Inspecting all Internet-bound DIA traffic with the following capabilities: IPS, Malware, URL Filtering

    3. Block Web Traffic to Gambling sites.
    

    

     
    

    Let's start the configuration of the first SGT Block Rule. Start by expanding the first Zone Pair, IOT_zone to IOT_zone. You will notice (2) pre-configured rules.

    1. SGT-Block 
    2. Inspect
    

    Click on the pencil ✏️ icon to edit the SGT-Block rule configuration.
    

    

    On the Edit Rule slide out, click + Add Conditions. Then select Source -> Security Group Tag.
    

    (👀 multiple sources might get selected by default, ensure for our use case that just SGT is selected)
    

    

    Next, select the IOT SGT.
    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    Click + Add Conditions, then select Destination -> Data Prefix.
    

    (👀 multiple destinations might get selected by default, ensure for our use case that just Data Prefix is selected)
    

    

    

    Under Data Prefix, click Object, then select the INTRANET-SERVER object. This object was pre-configured to match our intranet.corp.pseudoco.com web app server prefix. 
    

    

    

    

    Finally, click Drop as the action and then click Save.
    

    

    Review the SGT-Block rule and ensure our intent to Block on Source SGT = IOT towards our Intranet Sever.
    

    

    

    Next, we will configure the DIA (Direct Internet Access) ruleset. Start by expanding the second Zone Pair, IOT_zone to NAT_DIA. You will notice (1) pre-configured rule to Inspect.
    

    Click on the pencil ✏️ icon to edit the Inspect configuration.
    

    

    As a reminder our business Internet for this rule is:
    

    1. Inspecting all Internet DIA traffic with the following capabilities: IPS, Malware, URL Filtering.

    2. Block Web Traffic to Gambling sites.
    

    

    On the Edit Rule slide out under Action, select Inspect. Then, use the drop-down to assign the Branch-Advanced-Inspection Inspection Profile. Click Save to save the changes.
    

    (👀 We will explore the configuration of our custom Branch-Advanced Inspection profile next)
    

    The Advanced Inspection Profile is where IPS, URL-F, AMP is defined.
    

    

    

    Finally, review the ruleset and ensure it has been configured correctly, then click Save.
    

    

    In the Save changes dialog box, add a comment of your choice and click Save again.

    

    The NGFW policy is now set to the following:

    1. Control IOT network traffic from reaching our Intranet Server: intranet.cop.pseudoco.com using SGT enforcement.
    

    2. Inspecting all Internet DIA traffic with the following capabilities: IPS, Malware, URL Filtering

    3. Block Web Traffic to Gambling sites.
    

    Before we deploy this policy to the Secure Router, let's first explore the Advanced Inspection Policy so we can understand the Secure Router capabilities. 

    

    

    After saving the NGFW Policy, click Objects and Profiles in the upper right-hand corner of the NGFW Policy section.
    

    

    This will open the Objects and Profiles section. Next, click the Security Profiles tab.
    

    You will see our Branch-Advanced-Inspection profile that we reference. Take note of all the other profiles on the left-hand pane that can be configured - we will look deeper in the URL Filtering Profile after we explore our main policy. 

    Next, click on the pencil  ✏️ icon to inspect our Advanced Inspection Profile.



    In the Profile slide out window observe how we have enabled IPS, URL-F, and AMP, based on the referenced profile. There are also capabilities we are not leveraging in this Lab, such as TLS decrypt.

    

    We will now inspect the URL Filtering profile. To do this from the main Security Profiles section, click on URL Filtering.
    

    


    Next, click on the pencil  ✏️ icon to inspect our Branch-URL-Filtering Profile.
    

     
    

    In the Profile slide out window, observe how we have configured our Web Category to Block and have selected gambling. We have also set our Web Reputation to High Risk. This profile accomplishes our desire to block gambling sites. Feel free to take your time to discover all the different URL Filtering capabilities.
    

    

    Now that we have our NGFW Profile configured, its time to deploy the policy to our Secure Router. This will be done in the same way as a normal Policy deploy. After the policy is deployed, we will then test the NGFW Policy. 
    

    

    Deploy the Policy Group
    

    First, navigate to Configuration -> Policy Groups. Next, expand our Pseudoco-Branch-Policy. In the NGFW Policy, verify that NGFW-Policy-Branches is still set. Next, click Deploy to start the deployment process. Please be patient as the UTD engine will now be deployed to the Secure Router.
    

    

    Start the deployment of the Policy Group by selecting SITE_105 and our Branch Router. Then click Next.
    

    

    Click Next, as there are no variables to address.
    

    

    Review the summary and then complete the deployment by clicking Deploy.
    

    

    

    🎉Congratulations!! This completes the deployment of our NGFW Policy to our Secure Router. You may click View Deployment Status to monitor the deployment. Follow the process status as shown below. This process can take 5 minutes to fully complete the UTD engine deployment.

    Test the Deployed NGFW Policy 
    

    Workstation-IOT RDP access

    Connect Workstation-IOT to the network:

    Once the NIC is connected, Workstation-IOT will authenticate via MAB (Mac Authentication Bypass) and be treated as a headless IOT device. We will investigate our policy structure we have implemented to accomplish this after our initial segmentation testing.

    Lets begin to test our policy - remember our policy for IOT devices is:

    

    1. Control IOT network traffic from reaching our Intranet Server: intranet.cop.pseudoco.com using SGT enforcement.  
    


    2. Block Web Traffic to gambling sites and inspect all Internet DIA traffic with the following capabilities: IPS, Malware, URL Filtering

    Next, click the Chrome bookmark labeled Gambling. This should be blocked as well because of our Advanced Inspection Policy we reviewed.
    

    Next, browse to the User Lookup tool (Local Resource) using the Chrome bookmark. This will succeed since we did not specify any rules to control this access.

    Finally, browse to cisco.com to verify our DIA connectivity is working as expected.
    

    

    

    

    Secure SD-WAN local enforcement enables PseudoCo to extend Zero Trust security directly to the branch edge by leveraging the built-in Next-Generation Firewall (NGFW) and Unified Threat Defense (UTD) capabilities of Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN routers. Rather than sending all traffic to the cloud, IoT and operational device traffic can be securely inspected locally using features such as IPS, malware protection, URL filtering, and SGT-based policy enforcement. This is a key Cisco differentiator—combining cloud-delivered security with powerful on-box enforcement to create a hybrid model that optimizes performance without sacrificing protection. The result is reduced latency, consistent policy enforcement, and strong security coverage for devices that cannot rely on traditional endpoint protections.

    Secure Firewall - SGT Enforcement
    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    Section Overview – Enforcing Segmentation with SGT-Based Policy on the Firewall
    

    As PseudoCo continues implementing its Zero Trust architecture, the next step is extending identity-based segmentation enforcement directly into the data center firewall.
    

    Earlier in the lab, the identity infrastructure propagated Security Group Tags (SGTs) across the network and into the centralized firewall management platform Cisco Cloud-Delivered Firewall Management Center. This integration ensures that identity context—such as user role, device classification, and network segment—can be used directly within firewall access control policies.
    

    With this capability in place, security policy is no longer limited to traditional IP addresses or subnets. Instead, the firewall can enforce dynamic, identity-aware access control based on SGT classifications assigned throughout the environment.
    

    The enforcement point for this policy will be the data center firewall Cisco Secure Firewall Threat Defense, which now receives and understands the SGT information propagated from the network fabric.
    

    

    Identity-Based Segmentation in the Data Center
    

    In the PseudoCo architecture, SGTs are used to represent logical security zones across the enterprise. These tags follow users and devices regardless of where they connect—from campus networks to branch locations.
    

    One of the core segmentation principles defined by the security team is strict separation between the MAIN user network segment and production resources hosted in the DMZ.
    

    The MAIN segment contains standard corporate users performing everyday tasks such as browsing, email, and internal collaboration. These users should not have direct access to sensitive production services.
    

    Meanwhile, the DMZ hosts externally exposed or semi-sensitive applications that require tighter control.
    

    Because SGT context has already been propagated to the firewall management system, the security team can now enforce these boundaries using SGT-based firewall rules instead of traditional IP-based policies.
    

    

    Use Case – Blocking MAIN Segment Access to the Production User Lookup Application
    

    To demonstrate this capability, the lab will simulate a user from the MAIN network segment attempting to access a restricted production application.
    

    The user Nik is a standard corporate employee connected at the branch office. When Nik authenticates to the network, identity services assign the MAIN SGT, identifying the user as part of the corporate user segment.
    

    Nik then attempts to access the User Lookup application, a production service hosted within the DMZ environment.
    

    Because the firewall policy includes a rule that blocks traffic originating from the MAIN SGT to the User Lookup application, the request will be denied before reaching the server.
    

    This policy demonstrates how identity-based segmentation can protect sensitive resources without relying on complex IP-based rule sets.
    

    

    How the Firewall Enforces the Policy
    

    Within Cisco Cloud-Delivered Firewall Management Center, an Access Control Policy rule will be created that references the MAIN SGT as the source identity context.
    

    The rule logic is simple:
    

      

    • 

      Source SGT: MAIN
      

      • 

    • 

      Destination Application: User Lookup (DMZ)
      

      • 

    • 

      Action: Block
      

      • 

    

    When Nik attempts to access the application from the branch network, the firewall receives the SGT context associated with the session. Because the tag matches the blocked policy condition, the firewall immediately denies the connection.
    

    This enforcement confirms that SGT context can be used as a first-class policy attribute inside firewall rules.
    

    

    What You Will Demonstrate in This Section
    

    In this portion of the lab, you will validate that identity context propagated across the environment can be used for data center security enforcement.
    

    You will:
    

      

    • 

      Verify that SGT information has been successfully learned by Cisco Cloud-Delivered FMC
      

      • 

    • 

      Create an Access Control Policy rule using the MAIN SGT
      

      • 

    • 

      Configure the rule to block access to the User Lookup application in the DMZ
      

      • 

    • 

      Log in to the branch network as Nik
      

      • 

    • 

      Attempt to access the application and observe the firewall blocking the session
      

      • 

    

    Through this test, you will see how identity-based segmentation policies follow users across the enterprise and are enforced consistently—even when access attempts originate from remote branch locations.
    

    

    Why This Matters to PseudoCo
    

    Traditional firewall policies built solely on IP addressing can become complex and difficult to manage as environments scale.
    

    By integrating SGT context into the firewall policy framework, PseudoCo achieves:
    

      

    • 

      Identity-driven access control
      

      • 

    • 

      Simplified segmentation policies
      

      • 

    • 

      Consistent enforcement across campus, branch, and data center environments
      

      • 

    

    This approach ensures that user identity and role—not just network location—determine what resources can be accessed, further strengthening PseudoCo’s Zero Trust security model.
    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    2. Configure/Test Secure Firewall - SGT Enforcement
    
  
    
  
    Configure Secure Firewall - SGT Enforcement
    

    With segmentation established across the network, it’s time to enforce that same identity-driven policy at the data center edge using Cisco Secure Firewall. In this section, you will leverage Security Group Tags (SGTs) as a first-class policy construct, proving that Zero Trust segmentation extends beyond the network and directly into firewall enforcement—without relying on IP-based rules. 
    

    This section will use the user persona Nik. As a reminder about Nik's persona:
    

    Nik – Corporate  Employee
    

    Nik represents a standard corporate user within PseudoCo’s Main user segment. Unlike production users, Nik has limited and controlled access to sensitive production applications and resources.
    

    Nik remains subject to strict web security and segmentation policies designed to reduce risk from unauthorized access and inappropriate internet usage. His role demonstrates how identity-based policy and segmentation enforce business-intent-driven access controls, ensuring users only access what is necessary for their role.
    

    🛑Note: Your starting ruleset might be different from the rules in the upcoming screen shots🛑
    

    

    Configure SGT-Based Firewall Policy (Block Rule)
    

    Start by using your Dashboard automation launch pad to log back into your Security Cloud Control Org.
    

    Next, log into your Cisco Cloud-Delivered Firewall Management Center (cdFMC) by clicking on Firewall.
    

    Click Security Devices, select your "hqftdv" firewall, and in the slide out window, scroll down and click Access Control. This will take you to the main Access Control Policy for our HQ Firewall. 
    

    

    In the main HQ Firewall Policy, click Add Rule.
    

    

    Create a new rule to enforce the SGT Policy to block access from the Main users to the DMZ production LookupTool.
    

      

    1. Rule Name: MAIN->DMZ-BLOCK
      2. 

    2. Insert: Select above rule, then ensure the current rule 1. Jumphost is selected.
      3. 

    3. Action: Block
      4. 

    4. Click Dynamic Attributes and filter on just ISE Security Group Tags. Select the Main (16) SGT, then click Add Dynamic Source Attribute.
      5. 

    

    

    Continue the Rule configuration by:
    

      

    1. Clicking on the Zones tab.
      2. 

    2. Select the DMZ zone. This is the zone of the Lookup Application that we want to block Main users to.
      3. 

    3. Click Add Destination Zone.
      4. 

    

    The DMZ zone is now added to the Destination Zone for this rule.
    

    

    The final step will be to enable Logging for this rule. In Logging at the top of the rule:
    

      

    1. Click Logging.
      2. 

    2. Click Log at beginning of connection.
      3. 

    3. Click Confirm to save the logging configuration.
      4. 

    

    

    Review the rule configuration and then click Apply. In the Warning pop-up, click Apply again.
    

    

    

    

    Finally, click Save. This will save the configuration, but will not deploy to the Firewall yet. We will now setup our test and then deploy the SGT block rule.
    

    

    Test Secure Firewall - SGT Enforcement
    

    Start by logging into Workstation-3 via mRemote from Jumphost1, which is configured to login as Nik.
    

    Next, connect and authenticate any available NIC. 
    

    Using the Ping-Test app on the desktop, start by entering option (5) to confirm the host has received an IP address from the Main network segment of 10.10.255.X/24.
    

    

    Next, use the Chrome browser bookmark shortcut to browse to the User Lookup Tool . This application sits in the secure DMZ segment and contains sensitive customer information that the Main users should not have access to. 
    

    

    Back in the Ping-Test app, use option (4) to start a continuous ping to the User Lookup Application Server (198.18.9.2). This will serve as our reachability test. 
    

    

    Next, we will navigate back into cdFMC and deploy our SGT base firewall rule to block access from any Main network user to the Secure DMZ zone. 
    

    Since we previously had saved the rule, all we need to do is to deploy the rule. 
    

      

    1. Click on the Deploy button on the top right of cdFMC .
      2. 

    2. Select our hqftdv firewall for deployment.
      3. 

    3. Click Deploy to start the deployment process. 
      4. 

    

    

    You will see a percent complete operation for the deploy. Once complete, navigate back to Workstation-3.
    

    

    Once logged back into Workstation-3, you will see that the reachability test to our User Lookup Secure Server is unreachable. You can also refresh the web browser session to the app and it to will be unreachable.
    

    

    🎉 Congratulations, this completes the validation testing of our SGT block rule based on our business intent. Next, we will explore the logging and visibility within cdFMC to observe this SGT block, as well as seeing the propagated SGT's being carried all the way to the firewall and being enforced in-line. 
    

    

    Explore cdFMC Packet Capture and Logging
    

    First, lets explore the Packet Capture capabilities within cdFMC. We will setup a packet capture to see the ICMP traffic we are sending towards the secure server and blocking via SGT to show how we are enforcing the SGT in-line, and successfully propagating SGT's all the way from the campus to branch and into the firewall. 
    

    In cdFMC, navigate to Troubleshooting and click Packet Capture.
    

    

    Next, in the Packet Capture interface:
    

      

    1. From the Device drop-down, select your hqftdv firewall.
      2. 

    2. Then, click Add Capture to begin the capture configuration.
      3. 

    

    

    Configure the Capture with the following steps:
    

      

    1. Enter a Name of your choice for the capture. 
      2. 

    2. Capture Interface: Main-WAN
      3. 

    3. Protocol: ICMP
      4. 

    4. SGT number: 16 (Main)
      5. 

    5. Click Save to complete the configuration.
      6. 

    

    

    Once saved, you will see the status of your packet capture as running. 
    

    Investigate the Raw Packets as shown below. Notice that we are seeing INLINE-TAG 16 for Main on the ICMP traffic to 198.18.9.2 which is our Secure Lookup Server. This validates that we are propagating SGT tags all the way from the Campus, across SD-WAN and into the Firewall. This is a very powerful capability we can now use throughout the infrastructure and enforce policy based on this identity wherever we desire.
    

    

    🎉 Congratulations, this completes the Packet Capture and Inline SGT propagation validation.
    

    Optionally, you can disable this rule and re-deploy to see the reachability restored. 
    

    

    In this section, you successfully extended identity-based segmentation into the data center by enforcing SGT-driven policy on Cisco Secure Firewall. Instead of relying on static IP addresses, you used dynamic identity context propagated from Cisco ISE across the network and into the firewall via Cisco’s tightly integrated architecture.
    

    This is a key Cisco differentiator—TrustSec SGT context seamlessly shared across Campus, WAN, and Security Platforms, allowing policy to be defined once and enforced everywhere. Combined with centralized management in Cloud-Delivered FMC, PseudoCo achieves simplified rule management, reduced policy sprawl, and consistent Zero Trust enforcement across domains. The result is a scalable, identity-aware security model where access decisions are based on who and what, not where.
    

    

    🧭Complete
    
    

    Policy enforced. Identity honored. Threats stopped at the door. 🔒
    

    

    

    
  
    Experience Insights Integration
    

    

    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    

    Section Overview – End-to-End Experience Monitoring with ThousandEyes
    

    As PseudoCo completes the final stage of its Secure Access transformation, the focus shifts from building security controls to operational visibility and user experience monitoring.
    

    Throughout the lab, PseudoCo has implemented a comprehensive Zero Trust architecture using Cisco Secure Access, integrating identity services, segmentation, private access, SD-WAN connectivity, and centralized security enforcement across the enterprise.
    

    But even with a strong security architecture in place, the network and security teams still face an important operational challenge:
    

    When a user reports an issue, how quickly can the team determine whether the problem is the endpoint, the network, the security service, or the application itself?
    

    To solve this, PseudoCo integrates Cisco ThousandEyes with Cisco Secure Access to gain end-to-end visibility into user experience across the entire connectivity path.
    

    This integration enables the operations team to monitor endpoint health, connectivity to Secure Access, and application performance from a single platform.
    

    

    Expanding Visibility Beyond Security
    

    Traditional monitoring tools often focus on only one portion of the infrastructure—either the network, the application, or the endpoint.
    

    However, modern user experience depends on all of these components working together.
    

    For example, if a user cannot access a SaaS application, the issue could be caused by:
    

      

    • 

      High CPU utilization on the endpoint
      

      • 

    • 

      Poor Wi-Fi signal or local network congestion
      

      • 

    • 

      Latency to the nearest Secure Access point of presence
      

      • 

    • 

      Degraded performance of the SaaS provider itself
      

      • 

    

    Without correlated telemetry, troubleshooting these issues can become time-consuming and inefficient.
    

    By integrating Secure Access with ThousandEyes, PseudoCo gains the ability to observe the full path of a user’s application experience—from the endpoint device, through Secure Access, and all the way to the SaaS application.
    

    

    Cross-Domain Integration with Secure Client
    

    In the PseudoCo architecture, user devices connect to Secure Access using the Cisco Secure Client.
    

    This client not only provides secure connectivity services such as VPN and Zero Trust Network Access, but also acts as a telemetry collection point for experience monitoring.
    

    Through the integration with ThousandEyes, the Secure Client can collect key endpoint and network health metrics, including:
    

      

    • 

      CPU and memory utilization
      

      • 

    • 

      Wi-Fi signal strength and connectivity quality
      

      • 

    • 

      Network latency and packet loss
      

      • 

    • 

      Application reachability
      

      • 

    

    This telemetry is then sent to the ThousandEyes platform and correlated with monitoring data collected from Secure Access infrastructure.
    

    

    Use Case – Monitoring User Experience from Endpoint to SaaS
    

    In this section of the lab, you will enable Experience Insights by integrating Cisco Secure Access with ThousandEyes.
    

    The integration is performed through a secure token exchange, allowing Secure Access to register with ThousandEyes and automatically deploy predefined monitoring scripts to endpoints running the Secure Client.
    

    Once the integration is complete, the lab workstation will be registered with the ThousandEyes platform and begin sending telemetry data.
    

    From there, the operations team can begin analyzing the health of the endpoint and its connectivity to applications.
    

    This data provides insight into:
    

      

    • 

      Endpoint device health
      

      • 

    • 

      Connectivity to Secure Access points of presence
      

      • 

    • 

      Performance of commonly used SaaS services
      

      • 

    

    By correlating this information, administrators can quickly determine where problems originate and resolve user issues more efficiently.
    

    

    Exploring Experience Insights
    

    After the integration is complete, the monitoring data becomes visible through the Experience Insights dashboards within the ThousandEyes platform.
    

    Administrators can explore several key views, including:
    

      

    • 

      Endpoint Health Monitoring – Displays system metrics such as CPU, memory, and Wi-Fi quality.
      

      • 

    • 

      Secure Access Connectivity – Shows the health and performance of connections to Secure Access data centers.
      

      • 

    • 

      SaaS Application Performance – Provides visibility into how well users can reach cloud applications from their location.
      

      • 

    

    These dashboards allow operations teams to drill down into specific users or devices, quickly identifying the root cause of degraded application experience.
    

    

    What You Will Achieve in This Section
    

    In this final section of the core lab, you will enable cross-platform monitoring capabilities that enhance operational visibility across the environment.
    

    You will:
    

      

    • 

      Integrate Cisco Secure Access with Cisco ThousandEyes using token-based registration
      

      • 

    • 

      Deploy predefined monitoring scripts to endpoints through Secure Client
      

      • 

    • 

      Verify that endpoint telemetry is being collected
      

      • 

    • 

      Register a lab workstation with ThousandEyes
      

      • 

    • 

      Explore Experience Insights dashboards to review endpoint and application performance
      

      • 

    

    Through this process, you will see how Secure Access and ThousandEyes work together to provide deep operational insight into the complete user experience.
    

    

    Why This Matters to PseudoCo
    

    As enterprises adopt cloud-delivered security and distributed work environments, troubleshooting user issues becomes more complex.
    

    By integrating ThousandEyes with Secure Access, PseudoCo gains the ability to:
    

      

    • 

      Monitor endpoint health in real time
      

      • 

    • 

      Measure connectivity performance to Secure Access infrastructure
      

      • 

    • 

      Observe SaaS application performance from the user’s perspective
      

      • 

    • 

      Correlate telemetry across endpoint, network, and application layers
      

      • 

    

    This cross-domain visibility enables faster root-cause analysis and improves the overall digital experience for users.
    

    With security, connectivity, segmentation, and monitoring now fully integrated, PseudoCo’s Zero Trust architecture provides not only strong protection but also the operational insight required to keep the business running smoothly.

    In this section, you will integrate Cisco Secure Access with ThousandEyes to enable real-time monitoring of user experience. Experience Insights provides visibility into the health of endpoints, connectivity to Secure Access, and performance of common SaaS applications.

    This completes the integration between ThousandEyes and Secure Access. 
    

    

    Registering the ThousandEyes organization establishes the foundation for end-to-end experience monitoring within PseudoCo’s Secure Access architecture. By integrating Cisco ThousandEyes with Secure Access through token-based registration, PseudoCo enables a unified visibility platform that correlates telemetry across endpoints, network paths, and cloud applications. This is a key Cisco differentiator—bringing together security and observability into a single operational view. The result is faster troubleshooting, improved user experience insights, and the ability to pinpoint issues across the entire digital delivery chain from one centralized platform.

With this visibility, teams can quickly isolate issues, validate service health, and ensure consistent, high-quality user experiences across the entire Zero Trust architecture.

    Install Thousandeyes Agents on Leaf Switches
    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1. Overview
    
  
    
  
    Section Overview – Deploying ThousandEyes Enterprise Agents on Campus Fabric (Leaf Switches)
    

    As PseudoCo continues enhancing its Zero Trust architecture, visibility must extend beyond users and endpoints into the network infrastructure itself. While endpoint-based monitoring provides critical user perspective, it does not fully capture what is happening inside the network fabric—where many performance and connectivity issues originate.
    

    To close this gap, PseudoCo deploys Cisco ThousandEyes Enterprise Agents directly on campus fabric leaf switches, transforming the network into an active observability layer. By embedding monitoring capabilities within the infrastructure, PseudoCo gains deep visibility into application performance, network paths, and service availability directly from the network vantage point.
    

    This approach allows the operations team to monitor connectivity not just from endpoints, but from key transit points within the network—providing a more complete and accurate picture of end-to-end application experience.
    

    

    Why Network-Based Visibility Matters
    

    Traditional monitoring approaches often rely heavily on endpoint telemetry or centralized probes. However, these methods can miss critical insights into how traffic behaves inside the network itself.
    

    For example, performance issues may stem from:
    

      

    • Suboptimal routing paths within the fabric
      • 

    • Latency introduced at aggregation or border nodes
      • 

    • Misconfigured policies impacting traffic flow
      • 

    • External SaaS or internet path degradation
      • 

    

    By deploying ThousandEyes Enterprise Agents on leaf switches, PseudoCo gains the ability to:
    

      

    • Actively test application reachability from the network edge
      • 

    • Monitor path performance to SaaS and internet destinations
      • 

    • Identify latency, packet loss, and routing anomalies within the fabric
      • 

    • Correlate network-layer visibility with endpoint and application insights
      • 

    

    

    Deep Integration with Cisco Catalyst Center
    

    A key advantage in PseudoCo’s architecture is the integration between Cisco Catalyst Center and ThousandEyes.
    

    Using Catalyst Center’s App Hosting capabilities, Enterprise Agents can be deployed directly onto supported Catalyst switches without requiring additional hardware. This enables:
    

      

    • Simplified agent deployment at scale
      • 

    • Centralized lifecycle management of agents
      • 

    • Consistent monitoring across distributed campus locations
      • 

    • Rapid onboarding of observability into existing infrastructure
      • 

    

    This tight integration is a major Cisco differentiator—bringing together network automation, assurance, and observability into a single operational workflow.
    

    

    Use Case – Monitoring Application Experience from the Network Fabric
    

    In this section of the lab, you will deploy a ThousandEyes Enterprise Agent onto a leaf switch within the campus fabric and begin validating connectivity to critical applications.
    

    Once deployed, the agent will:
    

      

    • Execute synthetic tests toward SaaS and internet destinations
      • 

    • Measure latency, jitter, and packet loss
      • 

    • Trace network paths across internal and external segments
      • 

    

    This allows PseudoCo to observe how applications perform from the network’s perspective, complementing endpoint-based telemetry collected in later sections.
    

    

    What You Will Achieve in This Section
    

    In this portion of the lab, you will extend observability into the network infrastructure itself.
    

    You will:
    

      

    • Deploy a ThousandEyes Enterprise Agent on a campus leaf switch using Catalyst Center
      • 

    • Verify successful agent onboarding and registration
      • 

    • Execute test traffic to validate connectivity and performance monitoring
      • 

    • Observe how network-based telemetry provides additional visibility into application experience
      • 

    

    This step establishes the foundation for full-stack observability—combining network, endpoint, and application insights.
    

    

    Why This Matters to PseudoCo
    

    By deploying Enterprise Agents directly within the network fabric, PseudoCo evolves from reactive troubleshooting to proactive experience monitoring.
    

    This enables:
    

      

    • Faster identification of network-related performance issues
      • 

    • Improved visibility into SaaS and internet application paths
      • 

    • Reduced mean time to resolution (MTTR)
      • 

    • A more complete understanding of user experience across the environment
      • 

    

    With both endpoint and network telemetry working together, PseudoCo gains true end-to-end visibility across its Zero Trust architecture.
    

    
  
    📄
    
    2. Install Thousandeyes Agents on Leaf Switches
    
  
    
  
    Deploy ThousandEyes Enterprise Agent
    

    In this section, you will deploy a Cisco ThousandEyes Enterprise Agent directly on a campus leaf switch, extending visibility into the network fabric itself. This allows PseudoCo to monitor application performance and network paths from within the infrastructure—providing critical insight beyond what endpoint-only monitoring can deliver.
    

    ThousandEyes Organization Registration
    

    

    

    Integrating Catalyst Center with ThousandEyes requires a token exchange. This exchange allows Catalyst Center to deploy Enterprise Agents to switches and run test. The steps in this section will be performed from ThousandaEyes and Catalyst Center.
    

    Using your Dashboard automation bookmark in the Chrome browser on Jumphost1, find your ThousandEyes Account.

    First Click the clipboard icon to copy this profile email address. (Save it to NotePad++ on Jumphost1) Then click on the Go to account button in the Cisco ThousandEyes tile to connect to your ThousandEyes dashboard.
    

    

    

    

    

    Configure/Run Catalyst Center App Hosting workflow for TE
    

    First, Login to Cisco Catalyst Center either using the Google chrome favorite in the tool bar or you can log in directly by browsing to: https://cat-center.corp.pseudoco.com and entering the username and password below.
    

    admin / Demo@C!sco 
    

    From the main menu Click on Provision -> Application Hosting.
    

    

    Click on the ThousandEyes App which has already been pre-uploaded to your Catalyst Center.
    

    

    Next, Click install to launch the automated install workflow. 
    

    

    The first step in the workflow is to get Catalyst Center connected to ThousandEyes. Click Log in
    

    

    Then Click Verify

    

    

    When prompted to login to ThousandEyes, enter the User Profile email address you copied from the ThousandEyes dashboard in the task above. Then Click Log In to authenticate into the ThousandEyes platform.
    

    

    

    In the next window, select Log in with SSO.  On the next screen you will see the IdP login page, don’t close it and don’t input any credentials on this page. 
    

    

    

    Get back to the Dashboard Automation and click on Go to Account for Cisco ThousandEyes once again.  Even if you already have a browser tab with ThousandEyes open,  you need to refresh this by opening it again in a new browser tab.  
    

    Click Confirm to authorize the integration between Cisco Catalyst Center and ThousandEyes. 
    

    🛑 Note: You might need to wait a bit and retry opening ThousandEyes Dashboard using View button from Launch Panel in 1 minute in case you don’t get the confirmation window at first.
    


    You will see a Pop-Up confirming ThousandEyes was successfully integrated with Catalyst Center

    NDFC - DC Fabric Deployment
    
  
    
  
    📄
    
    1.  Overview
    
  
    
  
    

    

    

    

    

    

    

     
    

    Section Overview – Extending Segmentation into the Data Center with VXLAN EVPN Fabric
    

    As PseudoCo continues its journey toward a fully unified Zero Trust architecture, the next step is extending fabric-based segmentation beyond the campus and WAN into the data center itself.
    

    Up to this point in the lab, segmentation has been successfully implemented across:
    

      

    • The campus fabric (SDA or EVPN)
      • 

    • The branch environment
      • 

    • The WAN using Cisco Catalyst SD-WAN
      • 

    • Security enforcement platforms using SGT-based policy
      • 

    

    However, one critical domain remains: the data center network fabric.
    

    PseudoCo’s application and infrastructure teams require the same level of scalability, segmentation, and policy consistency inside the data center that already exists across the campus and WAN.
    

    To achieve this, PseudoCo is deploying a modern data center fabric using VXLAN EVPN, orchestrated through Cisco Nexus Dashboard.
    

    This enables the data center to operate as a policy-aligned extension of the enterprise fabric, rather than a standalone environment.
    

    

    The Business Objective
    

    PseudoCo’s goal is to ensure that the same MAIN, PROD, and IoT segmentation model used across the campus and WAN is consistently enforced within the data center.
    

    This means:
    

      

    • Workloads in the data center are mapped to the same business segments
      • 

    • Traffic between campus users and applications is controlled by consistent policy
      • 

    • Segmentation boundaries are preserved end-to-end
      • 

    • Security posture remains intact regardless of where applications are hosted
      • 

    

    By extending segmentation into the data center fabric, PseudoCo eliminates traditional gaps between network domains, enabling a truly unified architecture.
    

    

    Building the Data Center Fabric
    

    In this section of the lab, you will deploy and configure a VXLAN EVPN-based data center fabric.
    

    Using Cisco Nexus Dashboard, you will:
    

    1. Deploy the VXLAN EVPN Fabric
    

    You will initialize the data center fabric, establishing the underlay and overlay architecture required for scalable Layer 2 and Layer 3 connectivity.
    

    This provides the foundation for multi-tenant segmentation and workload isolation.
    

    2. Configure Interfaces for Endpoint Connectivity

    Next, you will connect physical server endpoints into the fabric.
    

    Each server represents a workload aligned to one of PseudoCo’s core business segments:
    

      

    • MAIN server
      • 

    • PROD server
      • 

    • IoT server
      • 

    

    These endpoints simulate real-world applications hosted within the data center.
    

    

    3. Configure Overlay Networks and VRFs
    

    You will then define the logical segmentation within the fabric by creating:
    

      

    • VRFs aligned to MAIN, PROD, and IoT
      • 

    • Associated overlay networks and subnets
      • 

    

    These VRFs represent the data center equivalent of the segmentation model used in the campus and WAN.
    

    

    4. Create an External Fabric Connection
    

    To enable communication between the data center and external environments, you will configure an external fabric.
    

    This allows the data center fabric to connect to upstream domains such as the WAN.
    

    

    5. Establish VRF-Lite Connectivity to SD-WAN
    

    Using VRF-Lite, you will integrate the data center fabric with the SD-WAN environment.
    

    This step is critical because it enables:
    

      

    • Route exchange between the data center and branch/campus environments
      • 

    • Preservation of segmentation boundaries across domains
      • 

    • Controlled communication between workloads and users
      • 

    

    This integration ensures that the data center becomes a fully participating node in the enterprise fabric architecture.
    

    

    End-to-End Segmentation Use Case
    

    With the data center fabric deployed and integrated, PseudoCo can now validate true end-to-end segmentation across the entire environment.
    

    In this test scenario:
    

      

    • Users and endpoints in the campus environment are assigned to one of three segments:

        

      • MAIN
        • 

      • PROD
        • 

      • IoT
        • 

      

      • 

    • Corresponding servers in the data center are placed into matching VRFs:

        

      • MAIN server
        • 

      • PROD server
        • 

      • IoT server
        • 

      

      • 

    

    You will then test connectivity across the environment to validate that:
    

      

    • MAIN endpoints can reach MAIN workloads
      • 

    • PROD endpoints can reach PROD workloads
      • 

    • IoT endpoints can reach IoT workloads
      • 

    • Cross-segment communication is restricted unless explicitly allowed
      • 

    

    This demonstrates that segmentation is preserved from campus → across SD-WAN → into the data center fabric.
    

    

    Why This Matters to PseudoCo
    

    In traditional architectures, the data center often operates as a separate domain with its own segmentation model, leading to:
    

      

    • Policy inconsistencies
      • 

    • Increased operational complexity
      • 

    • Security gaps between network domains
      • 

    

    By deploying a VXLAN EVPN fabric and integrating it with the broader architecture, PseudoCo achieves:
    

      

    • Consistent segmentation across campus, WAN, and data center
      • 

    • Simplified policy mapping using common business segments
      • 

    • Scalable multi-tenant network design
      • 

    • Seamless integration with existing routing and security domains
      • 

    

    Most importantly, PseudoCo ensures that security and segmentation policies follow applications just as they follow users.
    

    

    What You Will Achieve in This Section
    

    By completing this section of the lab, you will:
    

      

    • Deploy a VXLAN EVPN data center fabric using Cisco Nexus Dashboard
      • 

    • Configure endpoint connectivity for segmented workloads
      • 

    • Build VRFs and overlay networks aligned to business segments
      • 

    • Integrate the data center fabric with the SD-WAN environment
      • 

    • Validate end-to-end segmentation across campus, WAN, and data center
      • 

    

    Final Story Tie-In

    This section represents the final step in PseudoCo’s transformation:
    

    From isolated network domains
    → To a fully integrated, identity-aware, segmented enterprise architecture
    

    At this point, PseudoCo has achieved:
    

      

    • Identity-driven access control
      • 

    • End-to-end segmentation using SGTs and VRFs
      • 

    • Secure connectivity across campus, WAN, and cloud
      • 

    • Consistent policy enforcement across all environments
      • 

    

    You are no longer just building networks.
    

    You are operating a unified Secure Network where identity, segmentation, and policy are enforced everywhere—from user to application, across every domain.
    

    

    

    

     
    

    

     
    

    

    

    

    

    

     
    

    
  
    📄
    
    2. Environment Details
    
  
    
  
    Physical DC Topology
