    📄
    
    3. Getting Started
    
  
    
  
    In this section you will be using Nexus Dashboard and mRemote to configure then test a Secure DC Fabric that extends Segmentation from the Campus -> through SD-WAN -> to a VXLAN EVPN Data Center Fabric for the Main / PROD / IOT network segments. 
    


    To get started you will access Nexus Dashboard from Jumphost1 using the same Favorites Bar in your Google Chrome browser.  Once you launch it,  you may log-in using:
    

    Username:  admin
    

    Password: C1sco12345 
    

    

    

    

    Lastly we will be using the Remote-DC folder in the mRemote app on Jumphost1. This is where all of our DC device infrastructure ssh sessions are for this section, including the (3) Servers aligned to Main / PROD / IOT network segments as shown below. 
    

    

    

    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    4. Deploy DC VXLAN EVPN Fabric
    
  
    
  
    Deploy VXLAN EVPN Fabric in the Data Center
    

    In this section, you will deploy the foundation of PseudoCo’s modern data center by building a VXLAN EVPN fabric using Cisco Nexus Dashboard Fabric Controller (NDFC). This establishes both the underlay and overlay architecture required for scalable, policy-driven connectivity. This fabric becomes the backbone for extending segmentation into the data center.
    

    

    Create Fabric
    

    1.     Open Nexus Dashboard: From Jumphost1, Chrome Favorite  login with credentials admin / C1sco12345.
    

    2.     Navigate to Manage-> Fabrics-> Actions and click Create Fabric.
    

    3.     Select Create new LAN fabric and then click Next.
    

     
    

    

    In the Type select VXLAN with sub-type set to Data Center VXLAN EVPN and then click Next.
    

    

    For the Fabric settings set the following:
    

      

    • Configuration mode: Advanced
      • 

    • Name: Pseudoco-DC1
      • 

    • Location: San Jose, US
      • 

    • Overlay Routing Protocol: iBGP
      • 

    • BGP ASN: 65000
      • 

    • License Tier for fabric: Premier
      • 

    • Enabled Features: Telemetry - unchecked
      • 

    

       Click Next
    

    

    🛑NOTE: The following Advanced settings Tabs will be used in the upcoming steps by navigating between them.
    

    

    Under Advanced Settings > General Parameters tab, Scroll down for the Underlay Subnet IP Mask select 31.
    

    

    Select the Advanced tab and scroll down and in the Add Switches without Reload drop-down, select enable.
    

    

    Scroll back to top and click the Resources tab.
    

    Scroll down until you see VRF Lite Deployment and select Back2BackAndToExternal. (you can search for VRF)
    

    Check the Auto Deploy for Peer check box.
    

    

    🛑 NOTE: When you select this option, Nexus Dashboard can automate configuration on the external device if it is NXOS or ASR9k for VRF-Lite extension out of the VXLAN EVPN fabric.
    

    Scroll down until VRF Lite Subnet IP Range and change it to 192.168.252.0/24
    

    Select Auto Allocation of Unique IP on VRF Extension over VRF Lite IFC
    

    On the bottom right, click Next
    

    

    Review the summary and then click Submit
    

    

    Once the Fabric is created. Proceed to the next section.
    

    Add Switches
    

    In the Main menu, click Manage > Fabrics
    

    

    Click on Pseudoco-DC1 to open the fabric details
    

    

    In the Fabric Overview window, click Actions-> Add Switches.
    

    

    🛑NOTE: If a Warning Pops up asking to set the default credentials, do so by clicking Set Default Credentials.
    

    Username: admin
    

    Password: C1sco12345
    

    Click Save and OK to close the popup panel
    

    

    In the Add Switches dialog, enter the following details:
    

      

    1. Seed IP: 198.18.128.101
      2. 

    2. Authentication/Privacy: MD5
      3. 

    3. Username: admin
      4. 

    4. Password: C1sco12345
      5. 

    5. Max Hops: 2
      6. 

    6. Uncheck Preserve Config
      7. 

    7. Click Discover Switches
      8. 

    

    

    

    In the  configuration removal warning, click Confirm (If you did not get this waring you did not un-check preserve config)
    

    

    

    Click the Select all check box to select all 5 Manageable switches from Pseudoco-DC1 and then click Add Switches.
    

    🛑NOTE: You will likely see one unsupported device, that's fine.
    

    

    When the import completes, notice that the Status changed to Switch Added and then click Close. This process may take a few minutes to complete.
    

    🛑NOTE: With your switches added to your Pseudoco-DC1 fabric, the next Day0 task is to ensure each switch has assumed the correct role in the fabric. The importance of roles defines how and what best practice configuration templates you use to deploy a configuration to switches in the fabric. As part of the built-in automation of the discovery and import, the system automatically assigns default roles based on the switch model. For Nexus 9300 switches, the default role is set to leaf whereas for Nexus 9500 switches, the default role is set to spine.
    

    In a production VXLAN EVPN fabric, these default roles are correct for most of the switches in the fabric. The switches that assume the roles of border leaf switches or border gateway leaf switches, for example, must have their roles set correctly. Your XAR-DC fabric has a border leaf, DC-Service-Leaf that needs its respective role adjusted.
    

    

    

    In the Fabric Overview window, click the Inventory tab, then the Switches sub-tab. Check the DC-Service-Leaf switch and click Actions -> Set Role.
    

    

    In the list, choose Border and click Select.
    

    

    

    When you receive the warning message about performing a Recalculate Config, click Ok.
    

    

    

    

    Check DC-Spine-1 and DC-Spine-2, click Actions -> Set Role.
    

    

    Choose Spine and click Select.
    

    

    When you receive the warning message about performing a Recalculate Config, click Ok.
    

    

    

    

    Below is the final list of switches and their assigned roles.
    

    

    Using the blue Actions button at the top of the screen, Click Actions -> Recalculate and Deploy
    

    

    To preview and deploy the configuration in the Deploy Configuration window, you can click the link in the Pending Config column to see the configuration that will be pushed to the device as will as look at a diff of the current and proposed configuration. Close and preview windows then click Deploy All. 
    

    

    Verify that all the switches configs are in sync. You can re-run the re-calculate and deploy at anytime to sync the switches. 
    

    

    🎉 This concludes the fabric creation. It may take a few minutes for Nexus Dashboard to apply all the commands in the switches. Click Close.
    

    

    You have successfully deployed a VXLAN EVPN data center fabric using Cisco Nexus Dashboard, transforming the network into a scalable, software-defined architecture. A key Cisco differentiator is the use of NDFC to automate complex fabric provisioning—eliminating manual configuration and enabling rapid, consistent deployment. By leveraging EVPN as a control plane and VXLAN for data plane encapsulation, Cisco delivers a highly resilient and standards-based fabric that supports multi-tenancy and seamless integration with the broader enterprise network.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    
    

     💪With the fabric now live, it’s time to bring applications into the network—starting by connecting real workloads to the environment 🚀
    

    

    

    

    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    5. Configure Interfaces Connected to Endpoints
    
  
    
  
      Configure Interfaces connected to Endpoints
    

    In this section, you will connect data center endpoints into the fabric by configuring interfaces for workload connectivity. These endpoints represent PseudoCo’s core business segments and simulate real-world applications hosted in the data center. This step brings the fabric to life by attaching actual resources to the network.
    

    

    Create vPC Pairing 
    

    The last Day0 task you perform for your Pseudoco-DC1 fabric is making your DC-Leaf1 and DC-Leaf2 vPC pair or, said the classic way, a vPC domain.
    

    From the Switches tab, you can create vPC pairs between switches by working with only one of the switches in the pair. Nexus Dashboard automatically detects pairs for you.
    

    If not already open, in the menu click Manage -> Fabrics and then click Pseudoco-DC1.
    

    Navigate to Inventory -> Switches select the check box for DC-Leaf1, click Actions -> vPC Pairing.
    

    

    In the vPC Pairing pop-up, locate DC-Leaf2, check its corresponding check box and, at the bottom right, click Save.
    

    🛑NOTE: DC-Leaf2 shows as set to true and the only recommended switch capable of being a peer because it is connected to DC-Leaf1.
    

    

    When you receive the warning message about performing a Recalculate Config, click OK
    

    

    Using the blue Actions button at the top of the screen, select Actions > Recalculate and Deploy
    

    

    To preview and deploy the configuration in the Deploy Configuration window, you can click the link in the Pending Config column to see the configuration that will be pushed to the device as will as look at a diff of the current and proposed configuration. Close and preview windows then click Deploy All
    

    

    To return to the Fabric Overview, click Close. The deployment process will continue even if you navigate away from this window.
    

    Create vPC Interface
    

    We will now create a vPC Interface from our DC-Leaf1 and DC-Leaf2 vPC Pair
    

    Navigate to Manage-> Fabrics and click the Pseudoco-DC1 fabric.
    

    In the Fabric Overview page, navigate to Connectivity-> Interfaces tab, at the far right, click Actions-> Create Interface.
    

    

    Configure the following information
    

      

    • Type: Virtual Port Channel (vPC)
      • 

    • Mode: Access
      • 

    • Select a vPC pair: DC-Leaf1 ~ DC-Leaf2
      • 

    • vPC ID: 3
      • 

    • Peer-1 Member interfaces: eth1/3
      • 

    • Peer-2 Member interfaces: eth1/3
      • 

    

    

    Scroll down to set the last configuration then Click Save.
    

    

    Click Deploy
    

    

    On the next page, review the Pending Config for each device, then Click Deploy Config.
    

    

    Repeat the above steps  to create 2 more vPC interfaces.

    In the Fabric Overview page, navigate to Connectivity-> Interfaces tab, at the far right, click Actions-> Create Interface.
    

    

    Configure the following information for the 2nd vPC
    

      

    • Type: Virtual Port Channel (vPC)
      • 

    • Mode: Access
      • 

    • Select a vPC pair: DC-Leaf1 ~ DC-Leaf2
      • 

    • vPC ID: 4
      • 

    • Peer-1 Member interfaces: eth1/4
      • 

    • Peer-2 Member interfaces: eth1/4
      • 

    

    

    Scroll down to set the last configuration then Click Save. 
    

    

    Click Deploy
    

    

    On the next page, review the Pending Config for each device, then Click Deploy Config.
    

    

    Repeat the above steps  to create 1 more vPC interfaces.

    In the Fabric Overview page, navigate to Connectivity-> Interfaces tab, at the far right, click Actions-> Create Interface.
    

    

    Configure the following information for the 3rd vPC
    

      

    • Type: Virtual Port Channel (vPC)
      • 

    • Mode: Access
      • 

    • Select a vPC pair: DC-Leaf1 ~ DC-Leaf2
      • 

    • vPC ID: 5
      • 

    • Peer-1 Member interfaces: eth1/5
      • 

    • Peer-2 Member interfaces: eth1/5
      • 

    

    

    Scroll down to set the last configuration then Click Save. 
    

    

    Click Deploy
    

    

    On the next page, review the Pending Config for each device, then Click Deploy Config.
    

    

    In the main Interfaces overview, verify the interfaces were created. In the filter field at the top enter Interface contains vPC. Once you have verified your 3 vPC's, click <- Back and close the Pseudoco-DC1 fabric if you are in the fabric view.
    

    

    Validate vPC Config
    

    From Jumphost1 using mRemoteNG, SSH to DC-Leaf1 (198.18.128.101) using the credentials (admin/C1sco12345), or double click on the saved DC-Leaf1 session and run the command "show vpc" to verify the domain is configured and the vPC's are created.
    

    


    🎉 This completes this section. 

    

    

    You have successfully connected endpoints to the VXLAN EVPN fabric, enabling workloads to participate in the segmented data center environment. Cisco’s fabric automation simplifies endpoint onboarding by abstracting traditional interface and VLAN configurations into policy-driven constructs. This allows consistent mapping of workloads to business segments, ensuring that applications are aligned with the same Segmentation model used across campus and WAN.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    
    

    Now that workloads are connected, 🔒the next step is defining how they are logically segmented within the fabric 🔗
        
    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    6. Configure Overlay - VRFs and  Networks
    
  
    
  
    Configure Overlay – VRFs and Networks
    

    In this section, you will define the logical segmentation of the data center by creating VRFs and overlay networks aligned to PseudoCo’s business segments. These configurations establish isolated environments for MAIN, PROD, and IoT workloads. This is where segmentation is formally enforced within the fabric.
    

    

    Create VRF 
    

    To set up inter-VXLAN routing with symmetric Integrated Routing and Bridging (IRB) in VXLAN EVPN, you need a Layer-3 Virtual Network Identifier (VNI) for the overlay or tenant network.
    

    A Layer-3 VNI links to a tenant VRF routing instance, which helps separate the routing domain between tenant overlays. Nexus Dashboard simplifies this process by automating configuration checks, ensuring all VRF, BGP EVPN, and VXLAN settings are correct. This is particularly useful in large production environments with many peers and tenant overlays, where managing configurations can be complex. In this section, you will create and set up the initial tenant overlay VRF and its associated Layer-3 VNI. In the next section, you will follow the workflow to create networks that consist of Layer-2 (L2) VNIs and VLANs and link them to an overlay tenant VRF.
    

    In the Fabric Pseudoco-DC-1, click Segmentation and security, click on the VRFs tab, click the white Actions button and then finally click Create.
    

    

    In the Create VRF wizard, configure the following parameters
    

      

    • VRF Name: MAIN
      • 

    • VRF ID: 50000 (This comes from the pool of VNI's you created while creating the fabric)
      • 

    • VLAN ID: Propose VLAN (This pulls a VLAN from the VLAN pool you created while creating the fabric and places it in the VLAN ID Field)
      • 

    • VRF VLAN Name: MAIN
      • 

    • VRF Interface Description: MAIN
      • 

    • VRF Description: MAIN
      • 

    • Click Create
      • 

    

    

    Repeat the steps above for the 2nd and 3rd VRF creation.
    

    

    Configure the 2nd VRF
    

      

    • VRF Name: PROD
      • 

    • VRF ID: 50001 (This comes from the pool of VNI's you created while creating the fabric)
      • 

    • VLAN ID: Propose VLAN (This pulls a VLAN from the VLAN pool you created while creating the fabric and places it in the VLAN ID Field)
      • 

    • VRF VLAN Name: PROD
      • 

    • VRF Interface Description: PROD
      • 

    • VRF Description: PROD
      • 

    • Click Create
      • 

    

    

    Repeat the steps again for the last and 3rd VRF creation.
    

    

    Create the last VRF
    

      

    • VRF Name: IOT
      • 

    • VRF ID: 50002 (This comes from the pool of VNI's you created while creating the fabric)
      • 

    • VLAN ID: Propose VLAN (This pulls a VLAN from the VLAN pool you created while creating the fabric and places it in the VLAN ID Field)
      • 

    • VRF VLAN Name: IOT
      • 

    • VRF Interface Description: IOT
      • 

    • VRF Description: IOT
      • 

    • Click Create
      • 

    

    

    This is what the VRF's should look like once you are finished
    

    


    We have completed the VRF creation, now let's create some Networks.
    

    Create Networks
    

    In Nexus Dashboard, networks are where you set up Layer-2 (L2) Virtual Network Identifiers (VNIs) linked to VLANs for Layer-2 segments or bridge domains across the routed fabric. L2VNIs keep Layer-2 traffic within their boundaries, preventing it from crossing into other VNIs. For routing between different VNIs or VXLANs, a Layer-3 VNI with a VRF is necessary. If routing is needed, you can set up a Switched Virtual Interface (SVI), also known as an Anycast Gateway, on all leaf switches, which you then associate with the Layer-3 VNI VRF. If routing between L2VNIs is not required, the L2VNI can remain Layer-2 only. In this demo, you will set up two networks for inter-VXLAN routing, connecting them to the Layer-3 VNI VRF you created earlier.
    

    In the Fabric Pseudoco-DC1, click Segmentation and security, click the Networks tab, click the Blue Actions button and then finally, click Create.
    

    

    In the Create Network wizard, configure the following parameters
    

      

    • Network Name: MainNetwork1
      • 

    • Network Mode: Layer 3
      • 

    • VRF Name: MAIN
      • 

    • Network ID: 30000 (This is the first VNI from the L2VNI pool)
      • 

    • VLAN ID: Propose VLAN (This pulls a VLAN from the VLAN pool you created while creating the fabric and places it in the VLAN ID Field)
      • 

    • IPv4 Gateway/Netmask: 10.10.252.1/24
      • 

    • VLAN Name: MainNetwork1
      • 

    • Interface Description: MainNetwork1
      • 

    

    

    Scroll down to finish the config and then Click Create
    

    

    Repeat the steps above to create the 2nd Network
    

    

    Create the 2nd Network with the following information
    

      

    • Network Name: ProdNetwork1
      • 

    • Network Mode: Layer 3
      • 

    • VRF Name: PROD
      • 

    • Network ID: 30001 (This is the first VNI from the L2VNI pool)
      • 

    • VLAN ID: Propose VLAN (This pulls a VLAN from the VLAN pool you created while creating the fabric and places it in the VLAN ID Field)
      • 

    • IPv4 Gateway/Netmask: 10.101.252.1/24
      • 

    • VLAN Name: ProdNetwork1
      • 

    • Interface Description: ProdNetwork1
      • 

    

    

    Scroll down to finish the config and then Click Create
    

    

    Repeat the steps above to create the 3rd and last Network
    

    

    Create the 3rd Network with the following information
    

      

    • Network Name: IOTNetwork1
      • 

    • Network Mode: Layer 3
      • 

    • VRF Name: IOT
      • 

    • Network ID: 30002 (This is the first VNI from the L2VNI pool)
      • 

    • VLAN ID: Propose VLAN (This pulls a VLAN from the VLAN pool you created while creating the fabric and places it in the VLAN ID Field)
      • 

    • IPv4 Gateway/Netmask: 10.102.252.1/24
      • 

    • VLAN Name: IOTNetwork1
      • 

    • Interface Description: IOTNetwork1
      • 

    

    

    Scroll down to finish the config and then Click Create
    

    

    This is what the networks should look like once you are finished
    

    

    Create Attachments - Assign Switch Interfaces to Networks
    

    In Nexus Dashboard, VRF and Network attachments can happen independently.
    

    You can create VRFs and attach them to leaf switches then create Networks and attach them to leaf switches. You can combine the workflow, where you create the VRF, then associate the networks to the VRF, and finally deploy the networks which deploy the VRF. All this adhering to Cisco best practices.
    

    There are three Linux instances that are connected to your DC-Leaf1 and DC-Leaf2 switches via bond connected to interfaces Ethernet1/3, Ethernet1/4 and Ethernet1/5 of each switch. These interfaces are configured as VPCs in the switch side and a policy with an access interface leaving the access VLAN undefined.
    

    Let’s assign the switches vPCs interfaces to the VLANs we created in the previous section.
    

    Attach the first network to DC-Leaf1 vPC3.
    

    In the Pseudoco-DC1 fabric, open the Segmentation and security > Networks tab
    

    Click MainNetwork1
    

    


    Click the Network Attachments tab, check the box next to the DC-Leaf1 switch and then click Actions > Edit
    

    

    In the Edit Network Attachment screen, click the slider to Attach then click Actions > Add network interface attachment
    

    

    Change the filter to Mode == access, select port-channel3 in the DC-Leaf1 and in the DC-Leaf2 switches, click Add
    

    

    Click Save
    

    

    In the Network Overview screen, click <- Back on the top left.
    

    

    Attach the second network to DC-Leaf1 and DC-Leaf2 port-channel4.
    

    Click ProdNetwork1
    

    

    Click the Network Attachments tab, click the box next to the DC-Leaf1 switch and then click Actions > Edit
    

    

    In the Edit Network Attachment screen, click the slider to Attach then click Actions > Add network interface attachment
    

    

    Change the filter to Mode == access, check port-channel4 in the DC-Leaf1 and in the DC-Leaf2 switches, click Add
    

    


    Click Save
    

    

    In the Network Overview screen, click <- Back on the top left.
    

    

    Attach the third network to DC-Leaf1 and DC-Leaf2 port-channel5.
    

    Click IOTNetwork1
    

    

    Click the Network Attachments tab, check the box next to the DC-Leaf1 switch and then click Actions > Edit
    

    

    In the Edit Network Attachment screen, click the slider to Attach then click Actions > Add network interface attachment
    

    

    Change the filter to Mode == access, check port-channel5 in the DC-Leaf1 and in the DC-Leaf2 switches, click Add
    

    

    Click Save
    

    

    In the Network Overview screen, click <- Back on the top left.
    

    

    Now that you have attached the Networks and they are pending deployment in Nexus Dashboard, it is time to deploy.
    

    At the top-right corner, click Actions > Recalculate and Deploy.
    

    

    Review the pending configuration and then click Deploy all and when complete, click Close.
    

    

    To see the networks deployed, click Segmentation and security-> Networks.
    

    

    When performing the action to deploy the network attachments, the intent is for your VRF to deploy at the same time because of the VRF association with the Network when you created it. Confirm that the VRF is also deployed.
    

    In the top navigation, click the VRFs tab. You see that VRFs IOT, MAIN and PROD are deployed. Ensure it has a Deployed status.
    

    

    Verify Pseudoco-DC1 Fabric
    

    Through Nexus Dashboard, you have created and deployed your XAR-DC VXLAN EVPN fabric and performed a level of verification by checking the deployment status that Nexus Dashboard reports.
    

    Now, you are going to connect to some devices in your XAR-DC fabric to issue show commands to verify the configuration Nexus Dashboard deployed by Nexus Dashboard. Also, using the Linux servers that are connected to your DC-Leaf1 and DC-Leaf2, you perform data plane verification by checking connectivity to your Anycast Gateway and checking inter-VXLAN routing between your two servers.
    

    From the Jumphost using mRemoteNG, double click on the saved DC-Spine1 session
    

    To verify the Underlay OSPF Neighbors enter "show ip ospf neighbors"
    

    

    To verify DC-Spine1 BGP EVPN Neighbors, enter "show bgp l2vpn evpn summary"
    

    

    

    Verify DC-Leaf1.
    

    From the Jumphost using mRemoteNG, double click on the saved DC-Leaf1 session
    

    To show the vPC configuration enter "show vpc"
    


    To show the VXLAN VNI's, enter "show nve vni"
    


    Verify the Site-1 Data Plane. Three Linux servers are connected to your DC-Leaf1/DC-Leaf2 port-channel3, port-channel4 and port-channel5, respectively. Let’s test the access from the servers to the default gateway in the VLXAN EVPN fabric.
    

    From the Jumphost using mRemoteNG, double click on the saved MAIN-SERVER session and enter "ping 10.10.252.1" and verify you get responses. You can use ctrl + C to stop the ping
    


    From the Jumphost using mRemoteNG, double click on the saved PROD-SERVER session and enter "ping 10.101.252.1" and verify you get responses. You can use ctrl + C to stop the ping
    


    From the Jumphost using mRemoteNG, double click on the saved IOT-SERVER session and enter "ping 10.102.252.1" and verify you get responses. You can use ctrl + C to stop the ping
    


    Return to Nexus Dashboard and close the Pseudoco-DC1 view
    

    In the Navigation menu, click the Home > Topology tab. Double click Pseudoco-DC1
    

    

    Click on the Config-Sync Status toggle. When all the switches are green, they are synchronized, and you can hover on a link or switch to view more details.
    

    

    🎉 This completes this section! 
    

    

    You have built the logical overlay of the data center using VRFs and segmented networks, aligning directly with PseudoCo’s enterprise-wide segmentation model. A key Cisco differentiator is the consistent use of VRFs across campus, WAN, and data center—enabling seamless policy alignment without translation between domains. VXLAN EVPN allows these segments to scale efficiently while maintaining strict isolation, simplifying multi-tenant design and operational consistency.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    
    

    With segmentation defined ✅ inside the fabric, the next step is enabling connectivity beyond it—linking the data center to external networks 🌐
    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    7. Create an External Fabric
    
  
    
  
    Create an External Fabric
    

    In this section, you will extend the data center fabric beyond its internal boundaries by creating an external fabric connection. This enables communication between the VXLAN EVPN fabric and upstream networks such as the WAN. It is a critical step in integrating the data center into the broader enterprise architecture.
    

    

    These steps show you how to create the External Fabric required by Nexus Dashboard to extend a VRF to an external fabric device. Nexus Dashboard can create External Fabrics and manage the external devices if they are Nexus 9000, 7000, or 5600 series switches, or ASR9000 or NCS5500 series routers. Nexus Dashboard not only generates and pushes the configuration that is required for one or more leaf switches acting in the Border Leaf role but also maintains platform configuration compliance.
    

    Create a fabric for the external routers that will provide connectivity to and from the VXLAN EVPN fabric (Pseudoco-DC1).
    

    Create Fabric
    

    In Nexus Dashboard, click Manage > Fabrics
    

    Click Actions and select Create Fabric.
    

    

    Select Create new LAN fabric, and click Next.
    

    

    For the type select External and inter-fabric connectivity, and click Next.
    

    

    Configure the following for settings
    

      

    • Configuration Mode: Advanced
      • 

    • Name: External
      • 

    • Location: San Jose, US
      • 

    • BGP ASN: 65531
      • 

    • License Type: Premier
      • 

    • Telemetry: Unselect
      • 

    • Leave all other options default and click Next
      • 

    

    

    Verify the Fabric Monitor Mode box is checked and click Next
    

    🛑NOTE: The external equipment is already configured, Nexus Dashboard will not change its configuration. We will create the external fabric so that ND can detect the physical connection using CDP and can make the configuration in the border switch.
    

    

    Review the Fabric Summary and click Submit, once the fabric deploys, continue to the next step
    

    

    Add Switches
    

    Click Add Switches to Fabric
    

    


    Enter the following information in the ADD Switches - Fabric: External Screen
    

      

    • Seed IP: 198.18.133.14
      • 

    • Device Type: IOS XE and CSR/CAT8K
      • 

    • Authentication/Privacy: MD5
      • 

    • Username: admin
      • 

    • Password: C1sco12345
      • 

    • Max Hops: 0
      • 

    • Click Discover Switches
      • 

    

    

    Check the check box next to DC-SITE11-CEDGE8Kv and click Add Switches.
    

    

    When the import completes click Close
    

    

    In the Switches tab, verify that the role of DC-SITE11-CEDGE8Kv is Edge Router.
    

    

    🎉 This completes this section. Nice Work! 

    

    


    You have successfully configured an external fabric, allowing controlled communication between the data center and external domains. Cisco’s approach simplifies this integration by abstracting complex routing and border configurations into policy-driven workflows within NDFC. This ensures that external connectivity is both scalable and aligned with the overall segmentation and routing strategy.
    

    

    🧭 Next up
    
    

    With external connectivity established, 🌐 the next step is ensuring segmentation and routing are preserved across domains 🔄
    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    8. Create VRF-Lite Connections to External
    
  
    
  
    

    

    

    

    Create VRF-Lite Connections to External
    

    In this section, you will integrate the data center fabric with the SD-WAN environment using VRF-Lite. This enables route exchange while maintaining segmentation boundaries between business segments. This step ensures that the data center operates as a seamless extension of the enterprise fabric.
    

    

    Discover Connectivity Between Data Center VXLAN Fabric and External
    

    When you created your XAR-DC fabric, you set Fabric Settings under Resources to provision VRF Lite externally and to auto-deploy both the sub-interface and BGP configuration for external connection. Your border leaf and external router must detect each other so the provisioning of the sub-interface (with IP address) and BGP configuration can occur for both.
    

    Navigate to Manage > Fabrics and then click Pseudoco-DC1.
    

    

    To synchronize Pseudoco-DC1 within Nexus Dashboard with the new External fabric, in the top fabric-level Actions drop down, click Recalculate and Deploy.
    

    This synchronization aids in performing some automatic configuration and deployment from the Pseudoco-DC1 VXLAN EVPN fabric to the External fabric external router by staging the IP addresses for use on the sub-interfaces between your border leaf and external router.
    

    

    Click Deploy All to push out the new configuration. 
    

    

    Once complete Click Close
    

    

    Edit VRF Attachments in VRF PROD
    

    Because we now want to extend your tenant overlay externally out of your Pseudoco-DC1 fabric, return to VRFs, and deploy your VRFs to your DC-Service-Leaf.
    

    Previously, you only deployed this VRF to your DC-Leaf1 and DC-Leaf2 through the attachment of three networks.
    

    In the Pseudoco-DC1 fabric, navigate to Segmentation and security -> VRFs tab, and click PROD
    

    

    Click the VRF Attachments tab, click the check box for DC-Service-Leaf, click Actions -> Edit
    

    

    Click the Detach/Attach slider to enable the VRF attachment, in the EXTEND dropdown box select VRF_LITE. Click Edit
    

    

    In this table, you see that the interfaces connecting your border leaf and external router are automatically detected with being Ethernet1/8 and GigabitEthernet2. IP addresses are allocated from the VRF Lite sub-interface address pool defined in the Fabric Settings of your Pseudoco-DC1 fabric. The BGP ASN that you defined in your External fabric is also populated
    

    Configure the following in the Edit Extension Details screen, Nexus Dashboard pulls the parameter values from the VRF Lite sub-interface and IP address pools in your Pseudoco-DC1 fabric. The two key actions you must take are to enable the extension.
    

      

    1. Click the Detach/Attach slider to enable the external L3 extension
      2. 

    2. Encapsulation Dot1q VLAN ID: 2
      3. 

    3. IPv4 Prefix/mask: 192.168.252.5/30
      4. 

    4. Neighbor IPv4 Address: 192.168.252.6
      5. 

    

    🛑NOTE: Because in this lab the other side of the link is already configured and was done manually, we are forcing the IP addresses and encapsulation to be pre-determined values in the DC-Service-Leaf side. In a real implementation, if the other side of the link is configured later or if the external equipment is managed by NDFC, there is no need to change the parameters above.
    

    

    Scroll down an verify the config. Then Click Save
    

    

    Click Save again
    

    

    Check the box next to DC-Service-Leaf, click the blue Actions button, click Deploy
    

    

    Click Deploy
    

    

    Click Close once the deployment completes
    

    

    Click Back to close the VRF Overview panel
    

    

    Edit VRF Attachments in VRF IOT
    

    In the Pseudoco-DC1 fabric, navigate to Segmentation and security- > VRFs tab, and click IOT
    

    

    Click the VRF Attachments tab, click the check box for DC-Service-Leaf, click Actions -> Edit
    

    

    Click the Detach/Attach slider to enable the VRF attachment, in the EXTEND dropdown box select VRF_LITE. Click Edit
    

    

    In this table, you see that the interfaces connecting your border leaf and external router are automatically detected with being Ethernet1/8 and GigabitEthernet2. IP addresses are allocated from the VRF Lite sub-interface address pool defined in the Fabric Settings of your Pseudoco-DC1 fabric. The BGP ASN that you defined in your External fabric is also populated
    

    Configure the following in the Edit Extension Details screen, Nexus Dashboard pulls the parameter values from the VRF Lite sub-interface and IP address pools in your Pseudoco-DC1. The two key actions you must take are to enable the extension.
    

      

    • Click the Detach/Attach slider to enable the external L3 extension
      • 

    • Encapsulation Dot1q VLAN ID: 3
      • 

    • IPv4 Prefix/mask: 192.168.252.9/30
      • 

    • Neighbor IPv4 Address: 192.168.252.10
      • 

    

    🛑NOTE: Because in this lab the other side of the link is already configured and was done manually, we are forcing the IP addresses and encapsulation to be pre-determined values in the DC-Service-Leaf side. In a real implementation, if the other side of the link is configured later or if the external equipment is managed by NDFC, there is no need to change the parameters above.
    

    

    Scroll down an verify the config. Then Click Save
    

    

    Click Save again
    

    

    Check the box next to DC-Service-Leaf, click the blue Actions button, click Deploy
    

    

    Click Deploy
    

    

    Click Close once the deployment completes
    

    

    Click Back to close the VRF Overview panel
    

    

    Edit VRF Attachments in VRF MAIN
    

    In the Pseudoco-DC1 fabric, navigate to Segmentation and security -> VRFs tab, and click MAIN
    

    

    Click the VRF Attachments tab, select the check box for DC-Service-Leaf, click Actions -> Edit
    

    

    Click the Detach/Attach slider to enable the VRF attachment, in the EXTEND dropdown box select VRF_LITE. Click Edit
    

    

    In this table, you see that the interfaces connecting your border leaf and external router are automatically detected with being Ethernet1/8 and GigabitEthernet2.       •

      •

      •

Scroll down an verify the config. Then Click Save

🎉 Congratulations! You have successfully created the VRF-Lite connections to our External SD-WAN Fabric. Now lets test end-to-end connectivity and segmentation. 
    


    You have successfully integrated the data center with the WAN using VRF-Lite, enabling segmented communication across domains. A key Cisco differentiator is the ability to preserve segmentation end-to-end using consistent VRF constructs—without requiring complex reconfiguration or policy translation. This tight integration between Nexus fabrics and Catalyst SD-WAN demonstrates Cisco’s strength in delivering a unified architecture across traditionally siloed environments.

    

    🧭 Next up
    
    

    With integration complete, it’s time to validate that segmentation🔐 and connectivity work exactly as designed across the entire network ✅
          
    

    

    

    

    

    

    
  
    📄
    
    9. Test End to End Connectivity
    
  
    
  
    Test End to End Connectivity
    

    In this section, you will validate end-to-end connectivity across the campus, WAN, and data center environments. You will test communication between endpoints and workloads to ensure segmentation policies are correctly enforced. This confirms that PseudoCo’s unified architecture is functioning as intended.
    

    

    From Jumphost1 using mRemoteNG, double click on the saved DC-Service-Leaf session
    

    Verify BGP is working to the external router in each VRF
    

    Issue the command "show ip bgp summary vrf main"
    


    Issue the command "show ip bgp summary vrf prod"
    


    Issue the command "show ip bgp summary vrf iot"
    


    From the SSH connection to MAIN-SERVER verify connectivity to endpoint in the VRF MAIN in the campus network by running the command "ping 10.10.255.XXX" (Obtain the IP address from Workstation-3, using the pin-test app on the desktop, option 5)
    

    🛑 Below is just and example your IP might be different
    


    From the SSH connection to PROD-SERVER verify connectivity to endpoint in the VRF PROD in the campus network by running the command "ping 10.101.255.XXX" (Obtain the IP address from Workstation-1, using the pin-test app on the desktop, option 5)
    

    🛑 Below is just and example your IP might be different
    


    From the SSH connection to IOT-SERVER verify connectivity to endpoint in the VRF IOT in the campus network by running the command "ping 10.102.255.XXX" (Obtain the IP address from Workstation-IOT, using the pin-test app on the desktop, option 5)
    

    🛑 Below is just and example your IP might be different
    


    

    You have successfully validated true end-to-end segmentation across the entire PseudoCo environment—from campus to WAN to data center. Cisco’s integrated architecture ensures that segmentation policies are preserved across all domains, leveraging consistent constructs like VRFs and SGTs. This is a key differentiator, as it eliminates traditional policy gaps and enables a unified Zero Trust model where access is controlled based on identity and segment—not location.

    

    

    🧭 Complete
    
    

     Congratulations! 🎉 You have verified end-to-end connectivity from your Data Center into your Campus   network. Good Work!!

    2. cdFMC Automation Check
    
  
    
  
    cdFMC Automation Check
    

    When the session starts up there is automation that runs to associate the HQ-FTD with the SCC/cdFMC Org that was assigned to the session. This automation is based on terraform and take around 15 min to complete. Apart of session prep will be to verify the automation ran successfully at start-up. Later we will explain the procedure to follow if it was not successful.  
    

    First, locate the Terraform-Automation workstation on the topology. Click it to select it, then Click Web RDP to access the console. 
    

    

    Next Click the Terminal Emulator to launch a new terminal window.
    

    

    Next execute the following command to change directory: cd Documents/elevateLab
    

    

    Next, execute the following command to view the logs from the cdFMC automation: tail -f terraform.tasks.logs

    Verify it logged a successful Full Infrastructure deployment

    🛑NOTE:     If you see any errors or failures. Proceed to the Manual Automation Reset steps to try to get it to re-register correctly 
    

    

    🛑NOTE:  Your output might be truncated and look like the below if the session has been active for more than a hour, but both represent a successful deployment:
    

    

    You run the below cat command to verify from: cd Documents/elevateLab 

    cat terraform.tasks.logs
    

    

    You can verify what SCC Org the firewall was assigned to running the following command from: cd Documents/elevateLab 

     cat terraform.tfvars  
    

    

    

    cdFMC Verification: 
    

    The final step is to use the dashboard automation to log into the sessions SCC Org and verify the the FTD is registered and in sync with cdFMC. 
    

    From within SCC, Select Firewall, Then Security Devices and look for the Connectivity to be Online
    

    If this all checks out this Session is Ready! 
    


    

    

    Manual Automation Reset
    

    If your verification failed from the Terraform-Automation workstations terminal first run the reset from: cd Documents/elevateLab
    

    ./cli.py reset
    

    Sample Output:
    

    

    After the reset completes re-run the deploy from:  cd Documents/elevateLab (15 min process) 
    

    ./cli.py deploy
    

    Sample Output:
    

    

    Look for a successful completion then verify cdFMC registration that was shown above.
    

    🛑NOTE: If it fails again. Please reach out in the XAR Proctor Space and ask for help in troubleshooting.
    

    This concludes this section of Prep.
    

    
  
    📄
    
    3. Dashboard and AD Automation Verification
    
  
    
  
    Verify Dashboard Automation
    

    Log into  Jumphost1 and launch the Chrome Browser. Locate the dashboard favorite in the (Last Icon) on the top right, then Click to Launch the dashboard.
    

    🛑NOTE: If you do not have a dashboard favorite created, stop all pre-checks and engage the Core Lab Team for assistance.
    

    

    

    Next scroll  down and perform these (2) checks.
    

      

    1. First locate the Cisco SCC Tile. Then Click View. Verify SSO works to your assigned Org and then record the assigned SCC Org in the POD Prep Sheet. 
      2. 

    2. Next inspect the Cisco Duo Tile. Verify the user email addresses match the assigned hardware POD #
      3. 

    

    🛑NOTE: If SSO does not work correctly, stop all pre-checks and engage the Core Lab Team for assistance.
    

    

    AD Automation Verification
    

    Next we need to verify that the user accounts and AD were updated to match your assigned user emails in your Duo tile above. 

    Start by accessing AD1 from mRemote within Jumphost1.  Then launch Active Directory Users / Computers. Select the Users folder, Then right click and view the properties for the user Kit.

    

    Verify that's Kit's email matches the email on the Duo Tile. 

    

    Repeat for the Users: Lee, Pat, and Nik
    

    🛑NOTE: If the users email address was not updated correctly to match your Duo tile proceed to the next step to re-run the AD account automation. 

    

    Re-Run AD Automation 
    

    If the users email address was not updated correctly preform the following steps. 
    

    From Jumphost1, from the windows menu search for powershell, then Click to launch Windows Power Shell ISE

    

    First, Click Open. Then browse and open the ADDuoTenantUserProvisioning.ps1.


    

    It will take a brief moment to load. Once loaded Click the green run button to run the automation. The output should look like the below. 

    

    Finally  log back into AD and verify the the users: Kit, Lee, Pat and Nik have been updated correctly to match the Duo Tile. 

    

    This concludes this section of Prep.

    4. SCC Org Reset
    
  
    
  
    SCC Org Reset Procedure: 
    

    

      

    1. Delete the ISE pxGrid integration. From SCC Platform Management -> Platform integrations. Delete the  integration.
      2. 

    2. Secure Access. Admin -> API Keys. Delete the API Key configured for the Lab
      3. 

    3. Secure Access. Secure -> Access Policy. Delete All configured Policies

        

      1. Edit "For all Internet access" Disable Logging
        2. 

      

      4. 

    4. Secure Access. Secure -> Data Loss Prevention Policy. Delete configured Policy
      5. 

    5. Secure Access: Secure-> Endpoint Posture Profile: Delete PseudoCo Windows
      6. 

    6. Secure Access: Connect->Network Connections-> Network Tunnel Groups: Delete Router and Firewall
      7. 

    7. Secure Access: Connect->Users, Groups, and Endpoint Devices: Click Configuration management, Click edit and then Delete on both Duo and DuoSSO profiles. (Start with Duo then DuoSSO should delete also)
      8. 

    8. Secure Access: Connect-> End User Connectivity->Zero Trust Access: Delete PseudoCo ZTA Profile
      9. 

    9. Secure Access: Connect->End User Connectivity->Virtual Private Network: Delete  PseudoCo_RA_VPN_Profile

        

      1. Click Manage under the Regions and IP Pools: Delete the configured IP Pool
        2. 

      

      10. 

    10. Secure Access: Resources-> Private Resources: Delete Contractor and Intranet 
      11. 

    11. Secure Access: Resources->DNS Servers: Delete PseudoCo DNS
      12. 

    12. Secure Access: Experience and Insights, Account Management: Delete TE Integration
      13. 

    

    This concludes this section of Prep.
